GPU Infra Q26 of 200
How are multiple DGX nodes networked together to form a DGX SuperPOD, and what bandwidth does each DGX node contribute to the compute fabric?
A) DGX nodes are connected via standard 100GbE Ethernet — each node contributes 100 Gb/s to the compute fabric
B) DGX nodes use Wi-Fi 6E for cluster interconnect — no physical networking cables are needed between nodes
C) Each DGX H100 has 8 InfiniBand NDR400 ports (400 GB/s each) — providing 3.2 TB/s aggregate per node, connected through InfiniBand switches in a rail-optimized topology forming a non-blocking compute fabric
D) DGX nodes share GPUs over PCIe — GPUs in one DGX can directly access GPUs in another DGX through PCIe fabric extenders
✅ Correct Answer: C

Each DGX H100 is equipped with 8 ConnectX-7 InfiniBand NDR400 ports, each delivering 400 GB/s bidirectional bandwidth. This provides 3.2 TB/s aggregate per DGX node for inter-node GPU communication. In a DGX SuperPOD configuration, these ports are connected to a fat-tree InfiniBand switch fabric using rail-optimized topology — each GPU's IB port connects to a different switch rail, ensuring any GPU in any DGX can communicate with any other GPU at full NDR400 bandwidth without oversubscription.

📝 Detailed Explanation
DGX SuperPOD Networking — Rail-Optimized Topology
  DGX SuperPOD (32× DGX H100 = 256 GPUs, non-blocking fabric):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DGX 1: [GPU0]─Rail0──┐   [GPU1]─Rail1──┐   ...  [GPU7]─Rail7──┐    │
  │  DGX 2: [GPU0]─Rail0──┤   [GPU1]─Rail1──┤   ...  [GPU7]─Rail7──┤    │
  │  DGX 3: [GPU0]─Rail0──┤   [GPU1]─Rail1──┤   ...  [GPU7]─Rail7──┤    │
  │  ...                    │                │              ...      │    │
  │  DGX32: [GPU0]─Rail0──┘   [GPU1]─Rail1──┘   ...  [GPU7]─Rail7──┘    │
  │                         │                │              │            │
  │                    ┌────┴────┐     ┌────┴────┐   ┌────┴────┐        │
  │                    │IB Switch│     │IB Switch│   │IB Switch│        │
  │                    │  Rail 0 │     │  Rail 1 │   │  Rail 7 │        │
  │                    └─────────┘     └─────────┘   └─────────┘        │
  │                                                                      │
  │  Total bandwidth: 32 DGX × 8 ports × 400 GB/s = 102.4 TB/s          │
  │  Non-blocking: any GPU ↔ any GPU at full NDR400 (400 GB/s)           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost at SuperPOD scale, 32 DGX H100 nodes provide 256 GPUs for large-scale distributed training. The rail-optimized topology ensures that when training a large BERT model with NCCL all-reduce across all 256 GPUs, every GPU communicates at 400 GB/s — no bottlenecks, no oversubscription, linear scaling to 256 GPUs.

1.4 NVIDIA DPU & DOCA FrameworkQ27-Q32

GPU Infra Q27 of 200
What is an NVIDIA BlueField DPU and how does it differ from a GPU?
A) A DPU is a smaller, lower-cost GPU designed for edge inference on lightweight models
B) A DPU (Data Processing Unit) is a specialized processor that offloads data center infrastructure tasks — networking (OVS, NAT), storage (NVMe-oF, compression), and security (encryption, firewalling) — from the host CPU, combining Arm cores, accelerators, and high-speed networking on a single chip
C) A DPU is a power management unit that controls GPU power delivery and thermal throttling
D) A DPU is a display processing unit for rendering graphics output to monitors — it replaces the GPU for visualization workloads
✅ Correct Answer: B

The BlueField DPU (Data Processing Unit) is a fundamentally different processor from a GPU. While GPUs accelerate compute-heavy AI workloads, DPUs offload infrastructure tasks from the host CPU: networking (OVS switching, NAT, load balancing at 200/400 Gb/s), storage (NVMe-oF target/initiator, compression, encryption, RAID), and security (stateful firewall, TLS termination, root-of-trust). BlueField-3 combines 16 Arm Cortex-A78 cores, NVIDIA ConnectX-7 networking (400 Gb/s), and dedicated accelerators for crypto, storage, and packet processing — freeing host CPU cores for revenue-generating AI workloads.

📝 Detailed Explanation
BlueField DPU vs GPU — Different Processors, Different Purposes
  ┌──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ GPU (H100)                   │ DPU (BlueField-3)                    │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Purpose: AI compute          │ Purpose: Infrastructure offload      │
  │ 14,592 CUDA Cores           │ 16× Arm Cortex-A78 cores             │
  │ 528 TFLOPS FP16             │ 400 Gb/s networking (ConnectX-7)     │
  │ HBM3 80 GB, 3.35 TB/s      │ 32 GB DDR5, dual 200GbE ports        │
  │ Accelerates: Training, Inf  │ Accelerates: OVS, NVMe-oF, crypto    │
  │ Host CPU feeds it AI work   │ Host CPU freed from infra tasks      │
  └──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  BlueField-3 Architecture:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ 16× Arm Cortex-A78   │    │ CONNECTX-7 NETWORKING            │   │
  │  │ (Application Cores)  │    │ 400 Gb/s Ethernet / InfiniBand   │   │
  │  │                      │    │ RoCE, GPUDirect RDMA              │   │
  │  └──────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ HARDWARE ACCELERATORS │    │ SECURITY                         │   │
  │  │ • Crypto (TLS, IPsec) │    │ • Hardware Root of Trust         │   │
  │  │ • Storage (NVMe-oF)   │    │ • Secure Boot                    │   │
  │  │ • Regex/DPI           │    │ • Firmware attestation           │   │
  │  └──────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost infrastructure, a BlueField DPU in each GPU server offloads network virtualization (OVS) and storage encryption from the host CPU. This means 100% of the host CPU cores are available for data preprocessing and feeding the GPU inference pipeline — instead of wasting 20-30% on infrastructure overhead.

GPU Infra Q28 of 200
What is NVIDIA DOCA and how do developers use it with BlueField DPUs?
A) DOCA is a monitoring dashboard for viewing DPU temperature and power consumption metrics
B) DOCA is a GPU programming language similar to CUDA but specifically for BlueField GPUs
C) DOCA (Datacenter Infrastructure-on-a-Chip Architecture) is an SDK and runtime framework for BlueField DPUs — it provides libraries, APIs, drivers, and reference applications to build software-defined infrastructure services directly on the DPU
D) DOCA is a hardware version of the BlueField DPU — DOCA is the chip name, BlueField is the software stack
✅ Correct Answer: C

DOCA is NVIDIA's software development platform for BlueField DPUs — analogous to CUDA for GPUs. It provides the DOCA SDK (libraries for networking, storage, security, and management), DOCA Runtime (DPU OS and services), and DOCA reference applications (OVS acceleration, NVMe-oF target, stateful firewall, IPSec gateway). Developers write DOCA applications in C using DOCA libraries (doca_flow, doca_dpi, doca_crypto) that leverage the DPU's hardware accelerators — achieving line-rate 400 Gb/s packet processing with zero host CPU involvement.

📝 Detailed Explanation
DOCA Framework — Software Stack for BlueField DPUs
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        DOCA FRAMEWORK                                 │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  REFERENCE APPLICATIONS                                          │ │
  │  │  OVS Accelerator │ NVMe-oF Target │ Stateful Firewall │ IPsec GW│ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  DOCA LIBRARIES & APIs (C language)                              │ │
  │  │  doca_flow (flow pipeline) │ doca_dpi (deep packet inspection)    │ │
  │  │  doca_crypto (HW crypto)  │ doca_compress (HW compression)       │ │
  │  │  doca_rdma (GPUDirect RDMA) │ doca_arg (argument parsing)        │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  DOCA RUNTIME                                                    │ │
  │  │  DPU OS (Ubuntu-based) │ DPU Services │ Lifecycle Management    │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  BLUEFIELD HARDWARE                                              │ │
  │  │  Arm Cores │ ConnectX-7 │ HW Accelerators │ Secure Boot         │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, a custom DOCA application could implement GPU-aware load balancing: the DPU inspects inference request headers and routes each resume-to-job matching request to the GPU with the lowest queue depth — reducing tail latency by 40% compared to round-robin load balancing at the Kubernetes Service level.

GPU Infra Q29 of 200
An AI cluster administrator wants to achieve zero-trust security where even the host OS cannot see unencrypted data flowing between GPUs and storage. Which DPU use case enables this?
A) DPU networking offload — the DPU handles OVS switching to reduce CPU overhead
B) DPU GPU acceleration — the DPU runs inference models in place of a GPU for lightweight workloads
C) DPU power management — the DPU monitors and adjusts GPU power states for energy efficiency
D) DPU security isolation — the DPU terminates TLS, encrypts storage traffic, and enforces firewall rules in a hardware-isolated domain separate from the host OS, ensuring even a compromised host cannot access plaintext data
✅ Correct Answer: D

The BlueField DPU's hardware-isolated security domain is its most compelling use case for zero-trust architectures. The DPU runs its own operating system and security services separate from the host OS. It can terminate TLS connections, encrypt/decrypt storage traffic (NVMe-oF with inline AES-XTS-256), and enforce stateful firewall rules — all in the DPU's isolated domain. Even if the host OS is fully compromised by an attacker, the attacker cannot access plaintext data because encryption keys never leave the DPU and decrypted data is only visible to the GPU via GPUDirect RDMA.

📝 Detailed Explanation
DPU Security Isolation — Zero-Trust AI Infrastructure
  Without DPU (host-managed security):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HOST CPU (handles TLS + encryption)                                  │
  │  │  ⚠️ Compromised host = attacker sees plaintext data + keys        │
  │  ▼                                                                    │
  │  GPU ←── plaintext data ──→ Storage                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  With BlueField DPU (hardware-isolated security):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HOST CPU (compromised)                     DPU (ISOLATED DOMAIN)     │
  │  │  ❌ Cannot access plaintext              │  TLS termination       │
  │  │  ❌ Cannot access encryption keys        │  Storage encryption    │
  │  │                                         │  Stateful firewall     │
  │  ▼                                          │  Root of Trust         │
  │  GPU ←──── GPUDirect RDMA ────→ Storage     └────────────────────────┘
  │       (encrypted on wire,                   ✅ Plaintext only visible
  │        decrypted in DPU)                       to GPU, not host CPU
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost handling sensitive PII (resumes with addresses, phone numbers, work history), DPU security isolation ensures that even if the Kubernetes host node is compromised through a container escape, the attacker cannot access plaintext resume data — the DPU holds all encryption keys in its isolated domain and the host OS never sees decrypted data.

GPU Infra Q30 of 200
How can a BlueField DPU accelerate Kubernetes networking and what specific K8s components benefit from DPU offload?
A) The DPU replaces kubelet entirely — it schedules pods and manages container lifecycles without any CPU involvement
B) The DPU offloads the CNI data plane (OVS/Flannel/Calico), kube-proxy (service load balancing), and Ingress controller TLS termination — freeing host CPU cores and reducing pod-to-pod latency by 30-50% through hardware-accelerated packet processing
C) The DPU accelerates etcd by storing Kubernetes state in DPU memory, providing faster API server responses
D) The DPU replaces the container runtime — containers run directly on the DPU's Arm cores instead of the host CPU
✅ Correct Answer: B

The BlueField DPU accelerates Kubernetes by offloading the data plane from the host CPU to DPU hardware. The CNI (Container Network Interface) forwarding plane — whether OVS, Flannel VXLAN encap/decap, or Calico eBPF programs — runs on the DPU at line rate. kube-proxy service load balancing (iptables/IPVS rule processing) is replaced by hardware-accelerated flow steering in the DPU's ConnectX-7 ASIC. Ingress TLS termination is handled by the DPU's crypto accelerator. Result: host CPU utilization for networking drops from 20-30% to near zero, and pod-to-pod latency improves 30-50%.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes Networking — With vs Without DPU Offload
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT DPU (all networking on host CPU):                           │
  │  ┌────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌───────────────┐   ┌───────────┐     │
  │  │  Pod A │──▶│ OVS/VXLAN│──▶│ iptables/IPVS │──▶│ TLS (cpu) │──── │
  │  └────────┘   │ (cpu)    │   │ (cpu)         │   │ (cpu)     │     │
  │               └──────────┘   └───────────────┘   └───────────┘     │
  │  HOST CPU: ████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  (30% networking overhead)  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITH DPU (networking offloaded to DPU hardware):                    │
  │  ┌────────┐                                                          │
  │  │  Pod A │──┐                                                       │
  │  └────────┘  │    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │              └───▶│ DPU: OVS + VXLAN + LB + TLS (HW accelerated)│── │
  │                   └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │  HOST CPU: ████████████████████████████  (0% networking overhead)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost Kubernetes clusters, DPU offload frees 30% of CPU cores that were consumed by networking overhead — these cores can now run additional inference pods or data preprocessing containers, increasing the cluster's effective GPU-serving capacity without adding hardware.

GPU Infra Q31 of 200
Why do large-scale AI training clusters prefer InfiniBand over standard Ethernet for GPU-to-GPU communication?
A) InfiniBand provides ultra-low latency (~1 µs), high bandwidth (400 GB/s NDR), RDMA transport with zero CPU involvement, and built-in congestion control — delivering 3-5× higher effective NCCL bandwidth compared to standard Ethernet for distributed GPU workloads
B) InfiniBand cables are cheaper than Ethernet cables — cost savings is the primary driver for AI cluster adoption
C) InfiniBand supports longer cable distances (10 km+) compared to Ethernet (100m max) — needed for geographically distributed GPU clusters
D) InfiniBand is required by law for any cluster with more than 8 GPUs — it's a regulatory requirement, not a performance choice
✅ Correct Answer: A

InfiniBand is purpose-built for HPC and AI workloads. At the NDR400 generation, it delivers 400 GB/s per port with ~1 microsecond latency (vs ~10 µs for RoCE Ethernet). IB's RDMA (Remote Direct Memory Access) allows GPU memory to read/write remote GPU memory directly through the network — zero CPU involvement, zero memory copies. IB includes hardware-based congestion control (adaptive routing, forward error correction) that prevents the congestion collapse common in lossless Ethernet (PFC storms). For NCCL all-reduce across 256 GPUs, InfiniBand typically delivers 85-90% of theoretical peak bandwidth vs 40-60% on RoCE Ethernet.

📝 Detailed Explanation
InfiniBand vs Ethernet for AI Workloads
  ┌──────────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Feature          │ InfiniBand NDR400   │ Ethernet 400GbE (RoCE)   │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Bandwidth        │ 400 Gb/s            │ 400 Gb/s                 │
  │ Latency (MPI)    │ ~1 µs               │ ~10 µs (10× higher)      │
  │ RDMA             │ Native IB Verbs     │ RoCE v2 (over UDP)       │
  │ Congestion Ctrl  │ Hardware (AR, FEC)  │ ECN + PFC (complex)      │
  │ Message Rate     │ 215M msgs/sec       │ ~50M msgs/sec             │
  │ GPUDirect        │ Native              │ Requires compatible NICs  │
  │ NCCL Efficiency  │ 85-90%              │ 40-60% (variable)         │
  │ Cable Distance   │ 100m (active)       │ 100m (copper) / 10km (SMF)│
  │ Cost (per port)  │ Higher              │ Lower                     │
  │ Ecosystem        │ HPC/AI focused      │ Universal (data center)   │
  └──────────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if distributed training of custom NLP models becomes necessary, InfiniBand ensures that 8 GPUs across 4 DGX nodes communicate at 85%+ efficiency — meaning a 4-node job trains nearly 4× faster than a single node, with minimal communication overhead. On Ethernet, the same job might only achieve 2.5× speedup due to network bottlenecks.

GPU Infra Q32 of 200
What is RDMA and how does it benefit GPU-to-GPU communication in distributed AI training?
A) RDMA compresses GPU data before sending it over the network, reducing bandwidth requirements by 50%
B) RDMA is a GPU memory management technique that reduces GPU memory fragmentation during training
C) RDMA (Remote Direct Memory Access) allows one GPU's memory to directly read/write another GPU's memory across the network without involving the CPU or copying data through system memory — enabling zero-copy, low-latency inter-GPU communication
D) RDMA is a load balancing algorithm that distributes AI training data evenly across GPU nodes
✅ Correct Answer: C

RDMA (Remote Direct Memory Access) is a networking technology that enables one computer's memory to directly access another computer's memory without involving either CPU. The NIC (Network Interface Card) reads/writes remote memory directly via DMA — zero CPU cycles, zero memory copies, zero kernel involvement. In GPU clusters, GPUDirect RDMA extends this to GPU memory: GPU 1's HBM can write directly to GPU 2's HBM through the InfiniBand NIC, bypassing both host CPUs and system memory entirely. This is what makes distributed training across hundreds of GPUs feasible.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RDMA vs Traditional Network I/O — Zero-Copy GPU Communication
  Traditional Network I/O (CPU-mediated, 4 copies):
  GPU Mem ──Copy1──▶ Host RAM ──Copy2──▶ Kernel Buff ──Copy3──▶ NIC ──▶ Network
  (Repeat in reverse on receiver = 8 total copies, CPU busy throughout)

  GPUDirect RDMA (zero-copy, zero-CPU):
  GPU Mem ───────────────▶ NIC ──▶ Network ──▶ NIC ───────────────▶ GPU Mem
              (DMA)                                        (DMA)
  CPU: Doing AI work, not copying data! ✅

  Latency comparison (8 GPU all-reduce, 1 GB gradient):
  Traditional TCP/IP:  ~800 µs (8 copies through CPU + kernel)
  RDMA (InfiniBand):   ~12 µs (zero copies, hardware DMA)
  → RDMA is 65× faster for GPU-to-GPU communication!

For anihpj/jobpost distributed training, GPUDirect RDMA means gradient synchronization across 8 GPUs over 4 nodes consumes zero CPU resources and completes in microseconds — the host CPUs remain 100% available for data preprocessing and feeding the training pipeline, maximizing overall training throughput.

What is the role of an AI Operations (AIOps) engineer and how does it differ from a traditional DevOps or MLOps role?

B) AIOps focuses exclusively on training AI models — the operations part is delegated to the cloud provider
C) AIOps bridges infrastructure engineering and AI/ML — managing GPU clusters, high-performance networking (InfiniBand/RoCE), GPU scheduling, MIG partitioning, DCGM monitoring, and ensuring AI workloads run reliably at scale on specialized hardware that traditional DevOps tools don't cover

AI Operations (AIOps) sits at the intersection of infrastructure, AI/ML, and DevOps. Unlike traditional DevOps (managing CPUs, memory, disks, Kubernetes pods), AIOps engineers manage GPU-specific infrastructure: NVIDIA drivers, GPU Operator, MIG profiles, NVLink fabrics, InfiniBand networks, DCGM monitoring, GPU scheduling (Slurm + K8s), and the unique failure modes of GPU hardware (ECC errors, thermal throttling, XID errors, NVLink link failures). AIOps is the role that ensures GPUs — the most expensive and complex hardware in the data center — deliver their rated performance reliably.

For anihpj/jobpost, the AIOps engineer ensures the GPU inference stack delivers 99.9% uptime — when a GPU ECC error triggers a DCGM alert at 3 AM, AIOps knows to check XID codes, isolate the affected GPU, drain GPU workloads via Kubernetes, and schedule a hardware replacement — all without the ML team even noticing degraded performance.

GPU Infra Q34 of 200
When would you choose Slurm over Kubernetes for GPU workload scheduling, and what are their fundamental architectural differences?
A) Slurm is always better than Kubernetes — it should be used for all GPU workloads regardless of the use case
B) Kubernetes replaces Slurm entirely — Slurm is legacy HPC software with no role in modern AI infrastructure
C) Slurm and Kubernetes are identical in scheduling approach — the choice is purely about which YAML syntax you prefer
D) Slurm excels at batch HPC/AI jobs with queue-based scheduling, fair-share policies, and tight MPI integration — use it for large-scale distributed training; Kubernetes excels at long-running services, microservices, and container-native workloads — use it for inference serving and MLOps pipelines
✅ Correct Answer: D

Slurm is the dominant HPC workload manager — designed for batch job scheduling with queue priorities, fair-share accounting, and native MPI integration. It's ideal for large-scale distributed training where you need gang scheduling (all GPUs allocated simultaneously), time-limited jobs, and job arrays. Kubernetes is designed for long-running services with self-healing, rolling updates, and service discovery — ideal for inference serving, MLOps pipelines, and microservices. Many organizations use both: Slurm for training jobs on DGX clusters, Kubernetes for serving inference models.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Slurm vs Kubernetes for GPU Workloads
  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Feature      │ Slurm                     │ Kubernetes                   │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Workload     │ Batch jobs (finite run)   │ Services (run forever)       │
  │ Scheduling   │ Queue-based + fair-share  │ Pod scheduling + priority    │
  │ GPU Request  │ --gres=gpu:8              │ nvidia.com/gpu: 8            │
  │ Gang Sched   │ Native (all-or-nothing)   │ Requires plugin (Coscheduler)│
  │ MPI Support  │ Native (srun, PMIx)       │ Requires MPI Operator        │
  │ Autoscaling  │ Manual (elastic Slurm)    │ HPA/KEDA built-in            │
  │ Networking   │ MPI over IB/RoCE          │ CNI + Service mesh           │
  │ Container   │ Via Pyxis/Enroot plugin   │ Native (OCI containers)      │
  │ Job Arrays   │ Native                    │ Job/CronJob resources        │
  │ Best For     │ Training, HPC simulation  │ Inference, MLOps, microsvcs  │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Slurm schedules the weekly BERT fine-tuning job on 8 GPUs with gang scheduling (all GPUs allocated before training starts). Kubernetes runs the inference API as a Deployment with HPA autoscaling based on request queue depth — two different schedulers for two fundamentally different workload patterns.

GPU Infra Q35 of 200
How do AI training and inference workloads differ in their GPU resource requirements, and why does this matter for infrastructure planning?
A) Training and inference are identical — they use the same GPU resources in the same way, so infrastructure planning is identical for both
B) Training is GPU-intensive (hours/days of 100% GPU utilization, large memory footprint for model + optimizer states), while inference is latency-sensitive (sub-millisecond response times, lower GPU utilization but strict SLA requirements) — requiring different GPU provisioning, scheduling, and scaling strategies
C) Training uses CPU only — GPUs are exclusively for inference workloads
D) Inference requires more GPU memory than training because inference must process multiple user requests simultaneously
✅ Correct Answer: B

Training is a throughput-optimized batch workload: GPUs run at 90-100% utilization for hours or days. Memory requirements are large — a 70B parameter LLM needs ~140 GB in FP16 for model weights alone, plus optimizer states (Adam: 8 bytes/param), gradients (2 bytes), and activations — often requiring 8+ GPUs with tensor parallelism. Inference is latency-optimized: each request must complete in milliseconds. GPUs often run at 30-60% utilization (p95 latency matters more than throughput). Memory requirements are smaller (model weights only, no optimizer). Scaling is elastic — inference pods scale with user demand.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Training vs Inference — GPU Resource Profiles
  ┌──────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Characteristic   │ Training                 │ Inference                 │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Duration         │ Hours to weeks           │ Milliseconds per request  │
  │ GPU Utilization  │ 90-100% (sustained)      │ 30-60% (bursty)          │
  │ GPU Memory       │ HIGH: weights + optimizer│ LOWER: weights only       │
  │                  │ + gradients + activations│ (~2× model size in FP16)  │
  │                  │ (~4-8× model size)       │                          │
  │ Latency SLA      │ Not critical (batch)     │ CRITICAL: p99 < 100ms    │
  │ Scaling Pattern  │ Scale up (more GPUs/job) │ Scale out (more pods)     │
  │ Failure Impact   │ Lost compute time        │ User-facing errors        │
  │ Scheduling       │ Gang (all GPUs at once)  │ Elastic (HPA/KEDA)        │
  │ Best GPU         │ H100 SXM (high TDP)      │ L40S (efficient), A100    │
  └──────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the weekly BERT fine-tuning (training) runs on a dedicated DGX A100 with gang-scheduled 8 GPUs for 4 hours. The job matching inference API runs on 3 H100 GPUs with Kubernetes HPA, scaling from 3 to 12 pods during peak hiring hours. Different GPU pools, different schedulers, different scaling strategies — unified under AIOps management.

GPU Infra Q36 of 200
Your model is too large to fit on a single GPU (70B parameters ~140 GB in FP16). Which distributed training strategy should you use?
A) Tensor Parallelism (Model Parallelism) — split the model's layers and attention heads across multiple GPUs, with each GPU holding a portion of the model and communicating intermediate activations via NVLink/NCCL
B) Data Parallelism — replicate the entire model on each GPU and split the batch — each GPU processes a different data subset
C) Pipeline Parallelism only — split layers sequentially across GPUs but process only one micro-batch at a time
D) Reduce the batch size until the model fits on a single GPU — distributed training is always slower than single-GPU due to communication overhead
✅ Correct Answer: A

When the model itself exceeds single-GPU memory (common for LLMs > 10B parameters), Tensor Parallelism splits individual layers across GPUs. In a Transformer, the attention heads and FFN layers are sharded — GPU 0 computes heads 0-15, GPU 1 computes heads 16-31, with activations communicated via all-reduce. Modern frameworks combine all three strategies: Tensor Parallelism within a node (NVLink 900 GB/s), Pipeline Parallelism across nodes (InfiniBand), and Data Parallelism across pipeline stages — this is called 3D parallelism and is how GPT-4/LLaMA-70B are trained.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Three Distributed Training Strategies
  Data Parallelism (model fits per GPU):
  ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐
  │ GPU 0   │ │ GPU 1   │ │ GPU 2   │ │ GPU 3   │
  │Full Model│ │Full Model│ │Full Model│ │Full Model│
  │Batch/4  │ │Batch/4  │ │Batch/4  │ │Batch/4  │
  └────┬────┘ └────┬────┘ └────┬────┘ └────┬────┘
       └───────────┴───────────┴───────────┘
               All-Reduce Gradients (NCCL)

  Tensor Parallelism (model split across GPUs):
  ┌──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┐
  │   GPU 0      │   GPU 1      │   GPU 2      │   GPU 3      │
  │ Attn 0-15    │ Attn 16-31   │ FFN 0-15     │ FFN 16-31    │
  │ (1/4 model)  │ (1/4 model)  │ (1/4 model)  │ (1/4 model)  │
  └──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┘
       ▲ All GPUs process SAME batch — communicate activations

  Pipeline Parallelism (layers split across GPUs):
  GPU 0: [Layer 0-7] → GPU 1: [Layer 8-15] → GPU 2: [Layer 16-23] → GPU 3: [24-31]
  Micro-batch 1:      →→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→
  Micro-batch 2:          →→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→→
  (Pipeline bubbles exist at start/end — filled with more micro-batches)

For anihpj/jobpost, the current BERT-base model (~110M params, ~220 MB FP16) fits comfortably on a single GPU with data parallelism across 8 GPUs. If the team upgrades to a larger model like BERT-large or a custom LLM, tensor parallelism across 4 GPUs via NVLink becomes the primary strategy.

GPU Infra Q37 of 200
How do you track GPU utilization and allocate costs across different teams sharing a GPU cluster?
A) GPU cost allocation is impossible — GPUs are shared resources and usage cannot be attributed to specific teams
B) You divide the GPU purchase price by the number of teams and charge each team equally — fair-share regardless of actual usage
C) You manually ask each team to report their GPU hours at the end of the month — honor-system accounting
D) DCGM exports per-process GPU utilization metrics (GPU-Util, FB memory, Tensor Core usage) to Prometheus — combined with Kubernetes namespace labels and Slurm accounting (sacct), you can generate per-team GPU-hour reports for chargeback and capacity planning
✅ Correct Answer: D

GPU resource accounting combines DCGM metrics, Kubernetes resource tracking, and Slurm accounting. DCGM exports per-GPU utilization, memory, and process-level metrics to Prometheus. Kubernetes tracks nvidia.com/gpu requests per namespace/pod with timestamps. Slurm tracks sacct with GPU GRES usage per job/user/account. By joining these data sources with team labels (namespace=ml-team, Slurm account=research), you can generate accurate per-team GPU-hour reports for cost allocation — critical when a single H100 costs ~$30K and GPU time is the most expensive resource in the data center.

GPU Infra Q38 of 200
What is the NVIDIA Management Services Portal (MSP) and what does it provide?
A) MSP is a job board for NVIDIA-certified professionals to find employment
B) MSP is a cloud-based portal for monitoring multi-cluster AI infrastructure — it aggregates DCGM health data, NVSM system alerts, firmware versions, and support case management across all registered DGX and NVIDIA-Certified systems
C) MSP is a software update tool that automatically patches all NVIDIA drivers across a fleet
D) MSP is a marketplace for buying and selling used GPU hardware
✅ Correct Answer: B

NVIDIA Management Services Portal (MSP) provides a centralized cloud dashboard for monitoring the health and inventory of all your NVIDIA infrastructure — DGX systems, NVIDIA-Certified OEM servers, and DPUs. It aggregates DCGM GPU health metrics, NVSM system alerts (PSU, fan, temperature), firmware/driver versions, and warranty/support contract status. MSP also integrates with NVIDIA's support case system — when a GPU shows ECC errors, you can open a support case directly from the MSP dashboard with pre-attached diagnostic data.

GPU Infra Q39 of 200
A government agency needs to deploy NVAIE in a classified environment with no internet connectivity. How is this accomplished?
A) NVAIE cannot be deployed in air-gapped environments — it requires constant internet connectivity for license validation and driver updates
B) The agency must install commercial internet lines specifically for NVAIE — air-gapped deployments are not supported
C) NVAIE supports air-gapped deployments through offline license activation (DLS with manual license file), local NGC registry mirror (container images pre-downloaded), offline driver/CUDA repositories, and validated installation procedures that work entirely without internet
D) Air-gapped deployments use a special "offline" version of NVAIE that only supports CPU inference — GPU acceleration requires internet
✅ Correct Answer: C

NVAIE fully supports air-gapped (offline) deployments. The key components: DLS (Delegated License Server) can be activated with a one-time offline license file (transferred via secure media). NGC containers are pre-downloaded on an internet-connected staging server and transferred to the air-gapped registry mirror. Driver and CUDA repositories are downloaded as offline packages and served via local HTTP server. GPU Operator supports offline driver images and catalog mirrors. The entire stack operates indefinitely without internet after initial provisioning.

For anihpj/jobpost in a government deployment scenario, the air-gapped NVAIE deployment ensures the entire AI stack — from GPU drivers to BERT inference containers — runs in a fully isolated environment meeting classified network requirements, with all software transferred via secure, auditable physical media.
