🟡 Core: The Bridge Between GPU Hardware and AI Containers

This category is about how GPUs are exposed to containers — the critical infrastructure layer that makes GPU-accelerated Kubernetes possible. Think of it like the electrical wiring in a factory: the GPU Operator is the master electrician that installs drivers (wiring), the container toolkit (outlets), and the device plugin (labels) — so your AI containers can simply "plug in" and draw GPU power. For anihpj/jobpost, this category covers how the BERT inference pods actually access the H100 GPUs in the kubeadm cluster.

Q41-Q90 50 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

2.1 nvidia-container-toolkit & Container RuntimesQ41-Q52

Container/Ops Q41 of 200
What is nvidia-container-toolkit and what problem does it solve for GPU containers?
A) It's a monitoring tool that tracks GPU utilization inside containers and reports metrics to Prometheus
B) It's the bridge between the container runtime (containerd/CRI-O) and the NVIDIA GPU driver — it injects GPU device nodes, libraries, and binaries into containers so AI applications can access the GPU as if running natively on the host
C) It's a GPU driver installer — it downloads and installs the correct NVIDIA driver version for your GPU model
D) It's a container image builder specifically for GPU workloads — it replaces Dockerfile for CUDA applications
✅ Correct Answer: B

nvidia-container-toolkit is the critical middleware that enables GPU access inside containers. When a container runtime (containerd/CRI-O) creates a container with GPU requirements, the toolkit's OCI prestart hook (nvidia-container-runtime-hook) injects GPU device nodes (/dev/nvidia*), NVIDIA driver libraries (libcuda.so, libnvidia-ml.so), and GPU binaries (nvidia-smi) into the container's filesystem namespace. Without the toolkit, containers cannot see or use GPUs — they're isolated by the container boundary from the host's GPU hardware. The toolkit is the "GPU gateway" for containers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
nvidia-container-toolkit Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KUBERNETES POD                                                       │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Container: anihpj-inference                                     │ │
  │  │ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────────────────┐ │ │
  │  │ │ /dev/nvidia0 │ │ libcuda.so   │ │ /usr/bin/nvidia-smi      │ │ │
  │  │ │ /dev/nvidia1 │ │ libcudart.so │ │ (injected by toolkit!)   │ │ │
  │  │ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────────────────┘ │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              ▲                                        │
  │                              │ OCI prestart hook                      │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ nvidia-container-runtime-hook (runc hook)                       │ │
  │  │ → Reads NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES env var (e.g., "0,2")            │ │
  │  │ → Injects /dev/nvidia0, /dev/nvidia2 into container             │ │
  │  │ → Mounts driver libs from /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/            │ │
  │  │ → Mounts nvidia-smi, nvidia-persistenced, etc.                  │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              ▲                                        │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ containerd / CRI-O (container runtime)                          │ │
  │  │ → Config: default_runtime_name = "nvidia"                       │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              ▲                                        │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ NVIDIA GPU Driver (nvidia.ko) — kernel module                   │ │
  │  │ → Manages physical GPUs, exposes /dev/nvidia* device nodes      │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, nvidia-container-toolkit is what enables the BERT inference container to call torch.cuda.is_available() and get True — without it, PyTorch would only see CPU, and inference would be 100× slower. The toolkit is installed on every GPU node in the kubeadm cluster.

Container/Ops Q42 of 200
How does the nvidia-container-runtime-hook (OCI prestart hook) expose GPUs to containers, and when in the container lifecycle does it execute?
A) The hook executes during the OCI prestart phase — after the container namespace is created but before the entrypoint runs. It reads NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES, mounts the requested GPU device nodes and driver libraries into the container, and sets environment variables — ensuring the GPU is available before the application starts
B) The hook runs as a sidecar container alongside the main container — it continuously monitors GPU health and injects drivers when needed
C) The hook executes after the container starts — the application initially sees no GPU, then the hook hot-plugs GPU access 2-3 seconds later
D) The hook is a Kubernetes admission controller that modifies Pod specs before they reach the API server — it adds GPU resource requests automatically
✅ Correct Answer: A

The OCI prestart hook is defined in the OCI runtime specification as a hook that executes after the container process is created (namespaces exist) but before the user-specified command (entrypoint) runs. The nvidia-container-runtime-hook uses this precise timing to: (1) discover which GPUs to expose via NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES, (2) mount /dev/nvidia{0..N} device nodes into the container's /dev, (3) bind-mount NVIDIA driver libraries and binaries from host to container, and (4) set environment variables (LD_LIBRARY_PATH, PATH). All of this completes before the container's application starts.

📝 Detailed Explanation
OCI Container Lifecycle — Where the GPU Hook Runs
  Container Lifecycle Timeline:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. containerd receives Pod spec (nvidia.com/gpu: 1)                 │
  │       │                                                              │
  │       ▼                                                              │
  │  2. OCI Runtime creates namespaces (PID, net, mount, IPC, UTS)      │
  │       │                                                              │
  │       ▼                                                              │
  │  3. ⚡ OCI PRESTART HOOKS EXECUTE ⚡ ← nvidia-container-runtime-hook │
  │     • Mount /dev/nvidia0 → container                                 │
  │     • Mount libcuda.so → /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/                  │
  │     • Mount nvidia-smi → /usr/bin/                                   │
  │     • Set NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=GPU-                          │
  │       │                                                              │
  │       ▼                                                              │
  │  4. Container entrypoint runs                                        │
  │     → torch.cuda.is_available() = True ✅                            │
  │     → nvidia-smi shows GPU ✅                                        │
  │       │                                                              │
  │       ▼                                                              │
  │  5. Container exits → OCI POSTSTOP hooks execute                     │
  │     (GPU cleanup — unmount devices, release GPU allocation)          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the prestart hook ensures that when the inference pod starts, BERT's PyTorch initialization sees the H100 GPU immediately — no warm-up delay, no GPU discovery phase. The hook completes in milliseconds because it's just bind-mounting existing host resources.

Container/Ops Q43 of 200
What are the steps to install nvidia-container-toolkit on an Ubuntu 22.04 node running containerd in a kubeadm cluster?
A) Just install the NVIDIA driver — the toolkit is embedded in the driver and requires no separate installation
B) Run docker run --gpus all once — Docker automatically installs the toolkit as a dependency
C) Download the toolkit binary and copy it to /usr/bin — no configuration needed, it auto-detects the container runtime
D) Add NVIDIA's apt repository → apt install nvidia-container-toolkit → configure containerd to use nvidia runtime → restart containerd → verify with ctr run --gpus all nvidia/cuda:12.4-base nvidia-smi
✅ Correct Answer: D

Installing nvidia-container-toolkit requires: (1) adding the NVIDIA apt repository and GPG key, (2) apt install -y nvidia-container-toolkit, (3) configuring containerd by running nvidia-ctk runtime configure --runtime=containerd which adds the nvidia runtime to /etc/containerd/config.toml, (4) systemctl restart containerd, (5) verifying with ctr run --rm --gpus all nvcr.io/nvidia/cuda:12.4-base-ubuntu22.04 test nvidia-smi. The toolkit must be installed BEFORE deploying GPU pods — otherwise pods will fail with "Failed to create pod sandbox: unknown runtime specified: nvidia".

📝 Detailed Explanation
Step-by-Step: Install nvidia-container-toolkit on Ubuntu 22.04
  # 1. Add NVIDIA repository
  distribution=$(. /etc/os-release;echo $ID$VERSION_ID)
  curl -fsSL https://nvidia.github.io/libnvidia-container/gpgkey | \
      gpg --dearmor -o /usr/share/keyrings/nvidia-container-toolkit-keyring.gpg
  curl -s -L https://nvidia.github.io/libnvidia-container/$distribution/libnvidia-container.list | \
      sed 's#deb https://#deb [signed-by=/usr/share/keyrings/nvidia-container-toolkit-keyring.gpg] https://#g' | \
      tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/nvidia-container-toolkit.list

  # 2. Install the toolkit
  apt-get update && apt-get install -y nvidia-container-toolkit

  # 3. Configure containerd
  nvidia-ctk runtime configure --runtime=containerd
  # Adds to /etc/containerd/config.toml:
  #   [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".containerd.runtimes.nvidia]
  #     runtime_type = "io.containerd.runc.v2"
  #     [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".containerd.runtimes.nvidia.options]
  #       BinaryName = "/usr/bin/nvidia-container-runtime"

  # 4. Restart containerd
  systemctl restart containerd

  # 5. Verify
  ctr run --rm --gpus all nvcr.io/nvidia/cuda:12.4-base-ubuntu22.04 test nvidia-smi

For anihpj/jobpost kubeadm GPU nodes, this installation procedure is applied to every GPU node via Ansible playbook before joining the cluster. After containerd restart, the node registers nvidia.com/gpu as an allocatable resource, and inference pods can request GPUs.

Container/Ops Q44 of 200
For a Kubernetes cluster running GPU workloads, which container runtime is recommended: containerd or CRI-O?
A) Only Docker is supported for GPU workloads — containerd and CRI-O cannot access GPUs
B) CRI-O is the only supported runtime for GPU workloads — containerd was deprecated by NVIDIA
C) Both containerd and CRI-O are fully supported — containerd is the default in most K8s distributions (kubeadm, GKE, AKS) and has broader GPU Operator testing, while CRI-O is common in OpenShift and also supports GPU workloads through the same nvidia-container-toolkit integration
D) containerd and CRI-O are the same product with different names — they use identical configuration for GPU support
✅ Correct Answer: C

Both containerd and CRI-O support GPU workloads through nvidia-container-toolkit. containerd is the default in kubeadm, GKE, EKS, and AKS — it has the widest GPU Operator testing coverage and community support. CRI-O is common in OpenShift and also integrates with nvidia-container-toolkit via its runtime configuration. The GPU access mechanism is identical: the toolkit's OCI hook injects GPU resources into containers regardless of whether containerd or CRI-O created them. The choice is driven by your Kubernetes distribution, not GPU capability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
containerd vs CRI-O — GPU Configuration Comparison
  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Feature      │ containerd               │ CRI-O                        │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ K8s Default  │ ✅ (kubeadm, GKE, EKS)   │ OpenShift, Kubic, SUSE       │
  │ GPU Config   │ nvidia-ctk runtime       │ /etc/crio/crio.conf:         │
  │              │   configure --runtime    │ [crio.runtime.runtimes.nvidia]│
  │              │   =containerd            │ runtime_path="/usr/bin/       │
  │              │ config.toml location:    │   nvidia-container-runtime"  │
  │              │ /etc/containerd/         │                              │
  │ GPU Operator │ ✅ First-class support   │ ✅ Supported (v1.12+)        │
  │ Validation   │ Most CI-tested           │ OpenShift CI coverage        │
  │ Tool Config  │ nvidia-ctk               │ nvidia-ctk                   │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost's kubeadm cluster, containerd is the natural choice — it's the default kubeadm runtime, has the smoothest GPU Operator integration, and the team is already familiar with containerd configuration from existing Kubernetes operations.

Container/Ops Q45 of 200
What configuration is required in /etc/containerd/config.toml to enable GPU support, and how do you apply it?
A) Add a new runtime section under [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".containerd.runtimes] with runtime_type = "io.containerd.runc.v2" and BinaryName pointing to nvidia-container-runtime — then restart containerd. Use nvidia-ctk runtime configure --runtime=containerd for automated setup
B) Set the environment variable CONTAINERD_GPU=1 in /etc/default/containerd — no config file changes needed
C) Replace containerd entirely with nvidia-container-runtime — the two cannot coexist, containerd must be uninstalled
D) GPU support is automatic in containerd 2.0+ — no configuration is needed, it auto-detects NVIDIA GPUs
✅ Correct Answer: A

containerd requires explicit GPU runtime configuration in /etc/containerd/config.toml. The nvidia-ctk runtime configure command automates this by adding a [runtimes.nvidia] section pointing to nvidia-container-runtime. Key detail: the default runtime can remain runc — only pods requesting nvidia.com/gpu will use the nvidia runtime. The nvidia-device-plugin handles the runtime class selection automatically based on GPU requests.

📝 Detailed Explanation
containerd config.toml — GPU Runtime Section
  # Generated by: nvidia-ctk runtime configure --runtime=containerd
  # /etc/containerd/config.toml:

  version = 2

  [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri"]
    # Default runtime (CPU-only pods use this)
    [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".containerd]
      default_runtime_name = "runc"

      # NVIDIA GPU runtime (pods with nvidia.com/gpu use this)
      [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".containerd.runtimes.nvidia]
        runtime_type = "io.containerd.runc.v2"
        [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".containerd.runtimes.nvidia.options]
          BinaryName = "/usr/bin/nvidia-container-runtime"
          # The nvidia-container-runtime binary wraps runc:
          # It calls the OCI prestart hook before invoking runc to start the container

  # After editing: systemctl restart containerd
  # Verify: ctr run --rm --gpus all nvcr.io/nvidia/cuda:12.4-base test nvidia-smi

For anihpj/jobpost, this containerd configuration is applied to every GPU node via Ansible. After restart, pods requesting nvidia.com/gpu: 1 automatically use the nvidia runtime — the BERT inference container starts with full GPU access, confirmed by nvidia-smi showing the H100.

Container/Ops Q46 of 200
What is CDI (Container Device Interface) and how does it improve GPU device exposure compared to the traditional nvidia-container-runtime-hook approach?
A) CDI is a replacement for CUDA — it provides a new GPU programming model that doesn't require NVIDIA drivers
B) CDI is a container image format specifically for GPU workloads — it's an alternative to OCI images
C) CDI is NVIDIA's cloud deployment interface — it manages GPU instances on AWS, GCP, and Azure
D) CDI is a CNCF standard that defines a vendor-neutral way to expose devices (GPUs, FPGAs, etc.) to containers through declarative JSON specifications — it replaces the NVIDIA-specific OCI hook with a standardized approach supported by multiple device vendors
✅ Correct Answer: D

CDI (Container Device Interface) is a CNCF standard that provides a vendor-neutral mechanism for exposing hardware devices to containers. Instead of each device vendor writing custom OCI hooks, vendors provide a CDI specification (JSON file) that declaratively describes which device nodes, libraries, and binaries should be mounted into containers. NVIDIA adopted CDI in nvidia-container-toolkit v1.12+: the nvidia-ctk cdi generate command creates a CDI spec (/etc/cdi/nvidia.json), and containerd/CRI-O read this spec to inject GPU resources. Benefits: multi-vendor support, Kubernetes-native integration, and elimination of vendor-specific hooks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CDI vs Traditional OCI Hook — How GPU Exposure Works
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRADITIONAL: NVIDIA-Specific OCI Hook                               │
  │  Container → nvidia-container-runtime → nvidia-container-runtime-hook│
  │  (Vendor-locked, binary-only, opaque)                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CDI: Vendor-Neutral Device Specification                            │
  │                                                                      │
  │  /etc/cdi/nvidia.json (generated by nvidia-ctk cdi generate):        │
  │  {                                                                   │
  │    "cdiVersion": "0.6.0",                                            │
  │    "kind": "nvidia.com/gpu",                                         │
  │    "devices": [                                                      │
  │      {"name": "0", "containerEdits": {                                │
  │        "deviceNodes": [{"path": "/dev/nvidia0"}],                    │
  │        "mounts": [{"hostPath": "/usr/lib/...", "containerPath": ".."}],│
  │        "env": ["NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=GPU-"]                  │
  │      }}                                                              │
  │    ]                                                                 │
  │  }                                                                   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  Container Runtime reads CDI spec → injects devices declaratively    │
  │  Supports: NVIDIA GPUs, Intel GPUs, AMD GPUs, FPGAs — any device     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, CDI future-proofs the GPU infrastructure — if AMD or Intel GPUs are added to the cluster, the same CDI mechanism handles device exposure without changing the container runtime configuration. The migration from OCI hooks to CDI is transparent to running workloads.

Container/Ops Q47 of 200
How does the NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES environment variable control which GPUs a container can access?
A) NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES is a display setting — it controls which GPUs are shown in nvidia-smi output but doesn't restrict actual GPU access
B) NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES accepts GPU indices (0,1,2), UUIDs (GPU-), or "all" — the nvidia-container-runtime-hook reads this variable and ONLY mounts the specified GPU device nodes and their drivers into the container, physically preventing access to unlisted GPUs
C) NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES is a Kubernetes CRD that must be defined per namespace to enable GPU access
D) NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES controls GPU memory allocation — setting it to "0,1" gives the container 50% of each GPU's memory
✅ Correct Answer: B

NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES is the primary mechanism for GPU selection in containers. It accepts: GPU indices (NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0,2 — exposes GPU 0 and GPU 2), GPU UUIDs (NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=GPU- — exact GPU targeting), "all" (exposes all GPUs), or "void" (no GPUs — useful for testing CPU fallback). The nvidia-container-runtime-hook reads this variable during the OCI prestart phase and ONLY mounts the specified GPUs' device nodes and driver libraries. A container with NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0 cannot access GPU 1 — it simply doesn't exist from the container's perspective.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES — GPU Selection & Isolation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HOST NODE (4× H100 GPUs)                                            │
  │  /dev/nvidia0  /dev/nvidia1  /dev/nvidia2  /dev/nvidia3              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         │              │              │              │
         ▼              ▼              ▼              ▼
  ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐
  │ Container A  │ │ Container B  │ │ Container C  │ │ Container D  │
  │ VISIBLE=0,2  │ │ VISIBLE=1    │ │ VISIBLE=all  │ │ VISIBLE=void │
  │              │ │              │ │              │ │              │
  │ /dev/nv0 ✅  │ │ /dev/nv0 ❌  │ │ /dev/nv0 ✅  │ │ /dev/nv0 ❌  │
  │ /dev/nv1 ❌  │ │ /dev/nv1 ✅  │ │ /dev/nv1 ✅  │ │ /dev/nv1 ❌  │
  │ /dev/nv2 ✅  │ │ /dev/nv2 ❌  │ │ /dev/nv2 ✅  │ │ /dev/nv2 ❌  │
  │ /dev/nv3 ❌  │ │ /dev/nv3 ❌  │ │ /dev/nv3 ✅  │ │ /dev/nv3 ❌  │
  └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES ensures that the inference pod only sees the specific GPU allocated by the Kubernetes device plugin. In a MIG configuration, it might be set to a specific MIG device UUID — ensuring strict GPU isolation between the production inference tenant and development workloads sharing the same physical GPU.

Container/Ops Q48 of 200
What is nvidia-ctk and how does it simplify GPU container toolkit management compared to manual configuration?
A) nvidia-ctk is a container image that includes all NVIDIA drivers — you run it instead of installing the toolkit
B) nvidia-ctk is a Kubernetes operator — it replaces the GPU Operator for small clusters
C) nvidia-ctk is the new unified CLI for nvidia-container-toolkit — it replaces multiple legacy scripts (nvidia-container-runtime-hook config, nvidia-docker setup) with a single tool that can configure runtimes, generate CDI specs, and validate GPU container setups
D) nvidia-ctk is a GUI dashboard for monitoring GPU containers — it provides a visual interface for GPU allocation
✅ Correct Answer: C

nvidia-ctk (NVIDIA Container Toolkit CLI) is the unified command-line tool introduced with nvidia-container-toolkit v1.12+. It consolidates what previously required multiple scripts and manual edits into a single consistent CLI. Key commands: nvidia-ctk runtime configure (configures containerd/CRI-O/docker for GPU support), nvidia-ctk cdi generate (creates CDI specification for all installed GPUs), nvidia-ctk system create-dev-char-symlinks (creates /dev/char symlinks for GPU nodes), and nvidia-ctk info (displays toolkit version and configuration).

📝 Detailed Explanation
nvidia-ctk — Unified GPU Container Toolkit CLI
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  nvidia-ctk COMMANDS                                                  │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ nvidia-ctk runtime configure│ Configure container runtime for    ││
  │  │   --runtime=containerd     │ GPU support (auto-edits config file) ││
  │  │   --runtime=crio           │ Supported: containerd, CRI-O, Docker││
  │  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤│
  │  │ nvidia-ctk cdi generate     │ Generate CDI spec for all GPUs     ││
  │  │   --output=/etc/cdi/nvidia │ Output: JSON device specification   ││
  │  │   .json                     │ Used by containerd/CRI-O natively  ││
  │  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤│
  │  │ nvidia-ctk system create-   │ Create udev-style device symlinks  ││
  │  │   dev-char-symlinks         │ /dev/char/195:0 → /dev/nvidia0    ││
  │  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤│
  │  │ nvidia-ctk info             │ Show toolkit version, config paths ││
  │  │                             │ Driver version, GPU list, CDI info ││
  │  └─────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, nvidia-ctk simplifies GPU node provisioning — a single nvidia-ctk runtime configure --runtime=containerd && systemctl restart containerd replaces the previous multi-step manual configuration, reducing GPU node setup time from 15 minutes to 2 minutes and eliminating human error in config file editing.

Container/Ops Q49 of 200
A pod requesting nvidia.com/gpu: 1 fails with "could not select device driver 'nvidia' with capabilities: [[gpu]]". What is the most likely cause?
A) The GPU hardware is faulty — the GPU needs to be replaced immediately
B) The pod YAML has a syntax error — nvidia.com/gpu is misspelled
C) The NVIDIA driver version is too new — downgrade to driver 450 for compatibility
D) The nvidia container runtime is not configured in containerd/CRI-O — the container runtime doesn't recognize "nvidia" as a valid runtime. Fix with: nvidia-ctk runtime configure --runtime=containerd && systemctl restart containerd
✅ Correct Answer: D

The error "could not select device driver 'nvidia'" is the most common GPU container startup failure. It means containerd received the pod spec with GPU requirements, tried to use the "nvidia" runtime, but the runtime is not defined in containerd's configuration. This happens when: nvidia-container-toolkit is installed but containerd wasn't configured (nvidia-ctk runtime configure wasn't run), or containerd was restarted before the toolkit configuration completed. The fix is: nvidia-ctk runtime configure --runtime=containerd && systemctl restart containerd — then delete and recreate the pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
"could not select device driver 'nvidia'" — Root Cause & Fix
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ERROR FLOW:                                                          │
  │  1. Pod requests nvidia.com/gpu: 1 → kubelet schedules to GPU node   │
  │  2. kubelet calls CRI: RunPodSandbox with runtime: "nvidia"           │
  │  3. containerd looks for [runtimes.nvidia] in config.toml             │
  │  4. ❌ Section NOT FOUND → "could not select device driver 'nvidia'"  │
  │                                                                      │
  │  CHECK:                                                               │
  │  grep -A5 'runtimes.nvidia' /etc/containerd/config.toml              │
  │  # If no output → runtime not configured!                            │
  │                                                                      │
  │  FIX:                                                                 │
  │  nvidia-ctk runtime configure --runtime=containerd                    │
  │  systemctl restart containerd                                         │
  │  kubectl delete pod   # Recreate pod after containerd restart│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, this error is encountered during initial GPU node setup. The Ansible playbook ensures nvidia-ctk runtime configure runs before containerd starts, preventing this issue in production. For emergency debugging: grep nvidia /etc/containerd/config.toml should show the runtime section — if missing, the node was provisioned without GPU container support.

Container/Ops Q50 of 200
What files, devices, and libraries are mounted into a GPU container to enable full GPU access?
A) Only /dev/nvidia0 is mounted — the container uses its own built-in CUDA libraries
B) GPU device nodes (/dev/nvidia*), NVIDIA driver libraries (libcuda.so, libnvidia-ml.so, libcudart.so), configuration files (/etc/vdpau, /etc/OpenCL), and binaries (nvidia-smi, nvidia-persistenced) are bind-mounted from the host into the container's filesystem namespace
C) The entire host /usr directory is mounted — the container gets unrestricted access to ALL host binaries and libraries
D) Only the CUDA runtime library is mounted — all other NVIDIA components are included in the container image
✅ Correct Answer: B

The nvidia-container-toolkit mounts a carefully curated set of GPU device nodes and driver libraries from the host into each GPU container via bind-mounts. This includes: /dev/nvidia{0..N} (GPU device nodes), /dev/nvidiactl (control device), /dev/nvidia-uvm (Unified Virtual Memory), /dev/nvidia-modeset, driver libraries (libcuda.so — the core CUDA driver library, libnvidia-ml.so — management library for nvidia-smi, libnvidia-ptxjitcompiler.so — just-in-time PTX compiler), and binaries (nvidia-smi, nvidia-persistenced, nvidia-debugdump). The key insight: driver libraries are NOT in the container image — they come from the host, ensuring compatibility with the installed driver version.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Container Passthrough — What Gets Mounted
  HOST FILESYSTEM                          CONTAINER FILESYSTEM
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ /dev/nvidia0               │──bind──▶│ /dev/nvidia0               │
  │ /dev/nvidia1               │──bind──▶│ /dev/nvidia1               │
  │ /dev/nvidiactl             │──bind──▶│ /dev/nvidiactl             │
  │ /dev/nvidia-uvm            │──bind──▶│ /dev/nvidia-uvm            │
  │ /dev/nvidia-modeset        │──bind──▶│ /dev/nvidia-modeset        │
  │                            │          │                            │
  │ /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/ │          │ /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/│
  │   libcuda.so.550.90.07     │──bind──▶│   libcuda.so.1             │
  │   libnvidia-ml.so.550.90.07│──bind──▶│   libnvidia-ml.so.1        │
  │   libcudart.so.12.4.127    │──bind──▶│   libcudart.so.12          │
  │                            │          │                            │
  │ /usr/bin/nvidia-smi        │──bind──▶│ /usr/bin/nvidia-smi        │
  │ /usr/bin/nvidia-persistenced│──bind──▶│ /usr/bin/nvidia-persistened│
  └────────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────────────┘
  NOTE: Each bind-mount is READ-ONLY to prevent container from modifying host

For anihpj/jobpost, understanding exactly what's mounted into GPU containers is critical for security compliance — the bind-mounts are read-only and only include the minimum set of GPU libraries needed. The container image itself (nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch) contains the CUDA Toolkit libraries, but the driver-level libraries come from the host — ensuring they always match the installed driver version.

Container/Ops Q51 of 200
Why should you use the NVIDIA GPU Operator instead of manually installing nvidia-container-toolkit, drivers, and device plugin on each GPU node?
A) The GPU Operator provides higher GPU performance than manual installation — the operator includes performance optimizations not available in the standalone toolkit
B) Manual installation is deprecated by NVIDIA — the GPU Operator is the only supported method for GPU deployment in Kubernetes
C) The GPU Operator is a cloud service — it offloads GPU management to NVIDIA's servers, eliminating local configuration entirely
D) The GPU Operator automates the entire GPU stack lifecycle (driver compilation, toolkit config, device plugin, MIG, DCGM) across all GPU nodes — eliminating per-node manual setup, ensuring consistency, handling upgrades, and providing health validation that would take hours to replicate manually
✅ Correct Answer: D

The GPU Operator is not about performance — it's about automation and consistency. Manually installing the driver, toolkit, device plugin, MIG config, and DCGM exporter on 100 GPU nodes takes hours and inevitably results in version drift. The GPU Operator runs as a Kubernetes DaemonSet-based operator that automatically: detects GPU hardware, compiles the correct driver version, configures containerd/CRI-O, deploys the device plugin, manages MIG profiles, and exports DCGM metrics. When you add a new GPU node, the operator provisions it automatically. When you upgrade, the operator handles rolling updates with validation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator vs Manual Installation — Comparison
  ┌────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Task               │ Manual (per node)        │ GPU Operator (automated)       │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Driver Install     │ apt install + reboot     │ DaemonSet — auto detect HW    │
  │                    │ (5 min × N nodes)        │ compiles + loads driver        │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Toolkit Config     │ nvidia-ctk + edit config │ Automated via initContainer   │
  │                    │ + restart containerd     │ preconfigured for K8s          │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Device Plugin      │ kubectl apply + verify   │ Auto-deployed as DaemonSet    │
  │                    │                          │ with GPU discovery            │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ MIG Config         │ nvidia-smi -mig + manual │ ClusterPolicy CR — declarative│
  │                    │ profile creation         │ MIG profiles, auto-applied    │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ DCGM Monitoring    │ Manual DCGM install +    │ DCGM Exporter DaemonSet       │
  │                    │ Prometheus config        │ with ServiceMonitor            │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Health Validation  │ Manual nvidia-smi check  │ Validator pods — full GPU     │
  │                    │                          │ functional test suite          │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ New Node Join      │ Repeat ALL steps         │ Automatic — operator detects  │
  │                    │                          │ and provisions new GPU node    │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Upgrade            │ Manual per node + reboot │ Rolling update with validation │
  │                    │                          │ + automatic rollback detection │
  └────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost's kubeadm GPU cluster, the GPU Operator eliminated 4 hours of manual per-node setup. When a new H100 node joins, the operator provisions it in ~5 minutes with zero human intervention. When NVIDIA releases a critical driver security patch, the operator rolls it out across all GPU nodes during a maintenance window with automatic health validation.

Container/Ops Q52 of 200
What is the purpose of a multi-stage Dockerfile for GPU applications, and what does the anihpj/jobpost GPU Dockerfile look like?
A) Multi-stage builds are only for CPU applications — GPU Dockerfiles must be single-stage because CUDA libraries cannot be copied between stages
B) Multi-stage GPU builds combine CPU and GPU code into separate containers that communicate over the network — the CPU stage handles preprocessing, the GPU stage handles inference
C) Multi-stage builds separate the build environment (CUDA dev libraries, compilers, pip packages) from the runtime (minimal CUDA runtime libraries, no compilers) — dramatically reducing image size while keeping all GPU acceleration intact
D) Multi-stage GPU builds create separate images for training and inference — the first stage trains the model, the second stage optimizes it for deployment
✅ Correct Answer: C

Multi-stage Dockerfiles for GPU applications use the same principle as CPU multi-stage builds: a build stage with the full CUDA development toolkit (nvcc, cuDNN dev headers, pip/conda), and a runtime stage with only the CUDA runtime libraries needed for execution. The build stage compiles custom CUDA kernels, installs Python dependencies, and collects static files. The runtime stage copies only the built artifacts and uses a slim base image (nvidia/cuda:12.4-runtime-ubuntu22.04). Result: a 2GB runtime image vs. 8GB+ for a single-stage build with all dev tools.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost GPU Multi-Stage Dockerfile — Annotated
  # STAGE 1: BUILD (includes CUDA dev tools, compilers, headers)
  FROM nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch:24.06-py3 AS builder
  # Contains: CUDA 12.4, PyTorch 2.4, cuDNN 9.1, NCCL 2.21
  # Image size: ~7.5 GB

  WORKDIR /app
  COPY requirements.txt .
  RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt  # Django, gunicorn, etc.
  COPY . .
  RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput  # Django static files

  # STAGE 2: RUNTIME (minimal CUDA runtime, no compilers)
  FROM nvcr.io/nvidia/cuda:12.4-runtime-ubuntu22.04
  # Contains: CUDA 12.4 runtime only, ~1.2 GB base

  RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
      python3.11 python3-pip && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*

  # Copy only the built artifacts from builder stage
  COPY --from=builder /usr/local/lib/python3.11/dist-packages /usr/local/lib/python3.11/dist-packages
  COPY --from=builder /app /app
  COPY --from=builder /usr/local/bin/gunicorn /usr/local/bin/

  WORKDIR /app
  EXPOSE 8000
  USER 1000:1000  # Non-root user for security
  CMD ["gunicorn", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "4", "anihpj.wsgi:application"]

  # Final image: ~2.8 GB (vs 7.5 GB single-stage = 62% smaller!)
  # docker build -t nvcr.io/private/anihpj-inference:v1.0 .
  # docker push nvcr.io/private/anihpj-inference:v1.0

For anihpj/jobpost production, this multi-stage Dockerfile reduces the inference container from 7.5 GB to 2.8 GB — 62% smaller. This means faster image pulls (15s vs 45s on 10GbE), less registry storage, and smaller attack surface (no compilers, no dev headers, no pip cache). The BERT model is loaded at runtime from a shared PVC, not baked into the image.

2.2 NVIDIA GPU Operator ArchitectureQ53-Q66

Container/Ops Q53 of 200
What is the NVIDIA GPU Operator and what Kubernetes pattern does it implement?
A) The GPU Operator is a standalone binary that replaces kubelet for GPU nodes — it runs outside Kubernetes as a systemd service
B) The GPU Operator follows the Kubernetes Operator pattern (CRD + controller) — it uses a ClusterPolicy CRD to declaratively manage the entire GPU software lifecycle (drivers, toolkit, device plugin, MIG, DCGM) across all GPU nodes via DaemonSets and Jobs
C) The GPU Operator is a Helm chart that installs GPU drivers — it doesn't follow any Kubernetes pattern, it's just a deployment wrapper
D) The GPU Operator is a cloud service managed by NVIDIA — it runs outside the cluster and remotely controls GPU node configuration
✅ Correct Answer: B

The NVIDIA GPU Operator is a Kubernetes operator that follows the standard Operator pattern: it uses a ClusterPolicy CRD (Custom Resource Definition) as the declarative desired state, and a controller (operator pod) that continuously reconciles the actual GPU stack state against the ClusterPolicy. The controller manages sub-components as Kubernetes-native resources: driver installation via DaemonSets, toolkit configuration via init containers, device plugin via DaemonSet, MIG manager via Jobs, DCGM exporter via DaemonSet, and GPU Feature Discovery via DaemonSet. The reconciliation loop ensures: if a GPU node reboots without the driver → operator redeploys it. If MIG config drifts → operator reapplies it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator — Kubernetes Operator Pattern
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    GPU OPERATOR (Operator Pattern)                    │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ClusterPolicy CR (Desired State)                                    │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ apiVersion: nvidia.com/v1                                       │ │
  │  │ kind: ClusterPolicy                                             │ │
  │  │ spec:                                                           │ │
  │  │   driver:          {enabled: true,  version: "550.90.07"}       │ │
  │  │   toolkit:         {enabled: true,  version: "v1.16.1"}        │ │
  │  │   devicePlugin:    {enabled: true}                              │ │
  │  │   mig:             {strategy: mixed}                            │ │
  │  │   dcgmExporter:    {enabled: true}                              │ │
  │  │   gfd:             {enabled: true}                              │ │
  │  │   validator:       {enabled: true}                              │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              │                                        │
  │                              ▼                                        │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ OPERATOR CONTROLLER (Reconciliation Loop)                        │ │
  │  │ "Is driver 550.90.07 running on all GPU nodes? → NO → Deploy it" │ │
  │  │ "Is device plugin advertising GPUs? → YES → No action"           │ │
  │  │ "Is MIG configured per ClusterPolicy? → Check → Reconcile"       │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              │                                        │
  │         ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐                   │
  │         ▼                    ▼                     ▼                   │
  │  ┌────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐           │
  │  │ Driver DS  │    │ Device Plugin│    │ MIG Manager Job  │           │
  │  │ (DaemonSet)│    │  (DaemonSet) │    │ (one-time Job)   │           │
  │  └────────────┘    └──────────────┘    └──────────────────┘           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the GPU Operator's reconciliation loop provides self-healing GPU infrastructure: if a node reboot clears the driver, the operator detects it within 30 seconds and redeploys — the BERT inference pods may restart once, but the GPU stack recovers automatically without human intervention.

Container/Ops Q54 of 200
What are the main sub-components managed by the NVIDIA GPU Operator and what role does each play?
A) Driver (compiles and loads nvidia.ko), Toolkit (configures container runtimes), Device Plugin (advertises GPUs to kubelet), MIG Manager (configures MIG partitions), DCGM Exporter (GPU metrics for Prometheus), GFD (labels GPU nodes), Validator (end-to-end GPU functional tests)
B) All sub-components are bundled into a single binary — there's no separation of concerns, everything runs in one process
C) The sub-components are: CPU manager, memory manager, network manager, and storage manager — the GPU Operator manages all node resources, not just GPUs
D) Sub-components are user-defined plugins — the GPU Operator provides hooks but doesn't ship with any built-in components
✅ Correct Answer: A

The GPU Operator manages 7 core sub-components, each as a separate DaemonSet or Job: (1) Driver — compiles nvidia.ko kernel module for the node's Linux kernel, (2) Toolkit — configures containerd/CRI-O with nvidia runtime, (3) Device Plugin — registers GPUs as allocatable resources with kubelet, (4) MIG Manager — applies MIG partition profiles via mig-parted, (5) DCGM Exporter — exposes GPU metrics on :9400 for Prometheus, (6) GFD (GPU Feature Discovery) — auto-labels nodes with GPU model/memory/count, (7) Validator — runs functional GPU tests to verify the entire stack.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator Sub-Components — Relationship Diagram
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GPU OPERATOR POD (controller — manages all sub-components)          │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ SUB-COMPONENT                │ TYPE      │ PORT  │ RUNS ON       ││
  │  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────────┼───────┼───────────────┤│
  │  │ nvidia-driver-daemonset      │ DaemonSet │ —     │ GPU nodes     ││
  │  │ nvidia-container-toolkit-daemonset│ DS    │ —     │ GPU nodes     ││
  │  │ nvidia-device-plugin-daemonset│ DaemonSet │ —     │ GPU nodes     ││
  │  │ nvidia-mig-manager           │ Job       │ —     │ GPU nodes*    ││
  │  │ nvidia-dcgm-exporter         │ DaemonSet │ 9400  │ GPU nodes     ││
  │  │ gpu-feature-discovery        │ DaemonSet │ —     │ GPU nodes     ││
  │  │ nvidia-operator-validator    │ Job/Pod   │ —     │ Cluster-wide  ││
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │  *MIG Manager runs as a one-shot Job per MIG-capable GPU node        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the GFD sub-component automatically labels GPU nodes with nvidia.com/gpu.product=H100-SXM-80GB, enabling the inference Deployment to use nodeSelector to target H100 nodes specifically — ensuring BERT inference runs on the fastest available GPUs.

Container/Ops Q55 of 200
How do you install the NVIDIA GPU Operator using Helm on a kubeadm cluster?
A) kubectl apply -f https://nvidia.github.io/gpu-operator/gpu-operator.yaml — a single YAML manifest deploys everything
B) pip install nvidia-gpu-operator — the operator is a Python package, not a Kubernetes resource
C) helm repo add nvidia https://helm.ngc.nvidia.com/nvidia && helm install gpu-operator nvidia/gpu-operator -n gpu-operator --create-namespace --set driver.enabled=true --set toolkit.enabled=true
D) docker run nvidia/gpu-operator — the operator runs as a Docker container outside Kubernetes and manages all GPU nodes remotely
✅ Correct Answer: C

The GPU Operator is deployed via Helm from NVIDIA's Helm repository at https://helm.ngc.nvidia.com/nvidia. The standard deployment creates a dedicated gpu-operator namespace and installs the operator controller, ClusterPolicy CRD, and all sub-components. Key Helm values control which sub-components are enabled, driver version, MIG strategy, and DCGM configuration. The operator then auto-detects GPU hardware and provisions all nodes. For air-gapped environments, Helm values can point to private registries and offline driver repositories.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator Helm Installation — Full Command
  # 1. Add NVIDIA Helm repository
  helm repo add nvidia https://helm.ngc.nvidia.com/nvidia
  helm repo update

  # 2. Install GPU Operator with production values
  helm install gpu-operator nvidia/gpu-operator \
    --namespace gpu-operator \
    --create-namespace \
    --version v24.6.0 \
    --set driver.enabled=true \
    --set driver.version=550.90.07 \
    --set toolkit.enabled=true \
    --set toolkit.version=v1.16.1-ubuntu22.04 \
    --set devicePlugin.enabled=true \
    --set mig.strategy=mixed \
    --set dcgmExporter.enabled=true \
    --set dcgmExporter.serviceMonitor.enabled=true \
    --set gfd.enabled=true \
    --set validator.driver.env[0].name=DISABLE_DEV_CHAR_SYMLINK_CREATION \
    --set validator.driver.env[0].value=true

  # 3. Verify deployment
  kubectl get pods -n gpu-operator
  # NAME                                   READY   STATUS
  # gpu-operator-7d9f8b5c4-xxxx            1/1     Running
  # nvidia-driver-daemonset-xxxxx           1/1     Running (per GPU node)
  # nvidia-device-plugin-daemonset-xxxxx    1/1     Running (per GPU node)
  # nvidia-dcgm-exporter-xxxxx             1/1     Running (per GPU node)
  # gpu-feature-discovery-xxxxx            1/1     Running (per GPU node)

  # 4. Verify GPUs are available
  kubectl describe node  | grep nvidia
  # nvidia.com/gpu:     8
  # nvidia.com/gpu:     8  ← Allocatable GPUs

For anihpj/jobpost's kubeadm cluster, the GPU Operator is deployed via this Helm command as part of the cluster bootstrap Ansible playbook. After deployment, the operator provisions all GPU nodes within 5-10 minutes, and kubectl describe node confirms 8 allocatable H100 GPUs ready for inference workloads.

Container/Ops Q56 of 200
What CRDs (Custom Resource Definitions) does the GPU Operator create, and what is the primary CR that controls all GPU Operator behavior?
A) The GPU Operator creates no CRDs — it uses standard Kubernetes resources like Deployments and DaemonSets exclusively
B) The primary CRD is ClusterPolicy (cluster-wide GPU configuration) — it controls driver version, toolkit settings, MIG strategy, DCGM, GFD, and validator. The operator also creates NVIDIA Driver custom resources per node for driver lifecycle management
C) The GPU Operator creates hundreds of CRDs — one for each GPU model, driver version, and CUDA toolkit version combination
D) The GPU Operator uses the standard Kubernetes GPU CRD (gpu.resource.k8s.io/v1alpha2) — created by the Kubernetes project, not NVIDIA
✅ Correct Answer: B

The GPU Operator creates two main CRDs: ClusterPolicy (clusterpolicies.nvidia.com) — a cluster-scoped resource that defines the desired GPU software stack configuration for ALL GPU nodes, and NVIDIADriver (nvidiadrivers.nvidia.com) — a per-node resource that represents the driver lifecycle state on each GPU node. The ClusterPolicy is the single source of truth: when you update the driver version in ClusterPolicy, the operator controller detects the change and orchestrates rolling driver updates across all GPU nodes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator CRDs — ClusterPolicy & NVIDIADriver
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl get crd | grep nvidia                                       │
  │  clusterpolicies.nvidia.com           ← Main: GPU stack config       │
  │  nvidiadrivers.nvidia.com            ← Per-node: driver lifecycle    │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ClusterPolicy (cluster-scoped):                                     │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ spec.driver:          {enabled, version, repository, image}      │ │
  │  │ spec.toolkit:         {enabled, version, repository, image}      │ │
  │  │ spec.devicePlugin:    {enabled, version, config}                 │ │
  │  │ spec.mig:             {strategy: single|mixed, config}           │ │
  │  │ spec.dcgmExporter:    {enabled, serviceMonitor, config}          │ │
  │  │ spec.gfd:             {enabled, config}                          │ │
  │  │ spec.validator:       {enabled, plugin, env}                     │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                      │
  │  NVIDIADriver (per GPU node):                                        │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ spec: {driverVersion: "550.90.07", nodeSelector: {name: "gpu-1"}}│ │
  │  │ status: {conditions: [{type: Ready, status: True}], version: ...}│ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the ClusterPolicy CR is stored in Git (GitOps with ArgoCD). When the team wants to upgrade the driver from 550.90.07 to 555.42.02, they update the ClusterPolicy YAML in Git, ArgoCD syncs it, and the GPU Operator orchestrates the rolling upgrade across all GPU nodes — validated, auditable, and reversible.

Container/Ops Q57 of 200
What does a ClusterPolicy YAML look like and which sections control each GPU Operator sub-component?
A) ClusterPolicy uses one global "enabled: true/false" flag — all sub-components are either all on or all off
B) ClusterPolicy has dedicated sections for each sub-component — driver, toolkit, devicePlugin, mig, dcgmExporter, gfd, and validator — each with its own enabled flag, version, repository, and configuration options, enabling precise control over which components run and how
C) ClusterPolicy is written in Python, not YAML — it uses NVIDIA's SDK API to configure GPU nodes programmatically
D) ClusterPolicy only controls the driver and device plugin — all other components are managed through separate Helm charts
✅ Correct Answer: B

The ClusterPolicy CR provides fine-grained control over each sub-component with dedicated YAML sections. Each section has its own enabled flag, image repository/tag, and component-specific configuration. This allows precise composability: enable driver and device plugin on worker nodes (no MIG), enable MIG manager only on A100/H100 nodes (no H100 without MIG), enable DCGM everywhere, disable validator in air-gapped environments. The operator controller reconciles each section independently against the cluster state.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Complete ClusterPolicy YAML Structure
  apiVersion: nvidia.com/v1
  kind: ClusterPolicy
  metadata:
    name: cluster-policy
  spec:
    driver:
      enabled: true
      version: "550.90.07"
      repository: nvcr.io/nvidia
      image: driver
      # driverType: precompiled (default) | kernel-open
    toolkit:
      enabled: true
      version: "v1.16.1-ubuntu22.04"
      repository: nvcr.io/nvidia/k8s
      image: container-toolkit
    devicePlugin:
      enabled: true
      version: "v0.15.0"
      config:
        name: time-slicing-config  # Optional: time-slicing configmap
    mig:
      strategy: mixed  # single | mixed | none
    dcgmExporter:
      enabled: true
      serviceMonitor:
        enabled: true  # Creates Prometheus ServiceMonitor
    gfd:
      enabled: true
      repository: nvcr.io/nvidia
      image: gpu-feature-discovery
    validator:
      enabled: true
      repository: nvcr.io/nvidia/cloud-native
      image: gpu-operator-validator

For anihpj/jobpost, the ClusterPolicy enables driver, toolkit, devicePlugin, DCGM exporter, and GFD on all GPU nodes. MIG is set to strategy: none for the H100 inference nodes (full GPU performance preferred over partitioning). The validator runs in a CI pipeline to verify GPU stack health before deploying inference workloads.

Container/Ops Q58 of 200
How does the GPU Operator's driver container deploy and manage the NVIDIA driver on each GPU node?
A) The driver container runs nvidia-smi on a schedule to check if the driver is loaded — it doesn't actually install drivers
B) The driver container downloads a pre-built .deb package and runs dpkg -i — standard package management, no compilation
C) The driver container (DaemonSet) uses precompiled driver packages matched to the host kernel — it downloads the driver, compiles the kernel module against the host's kernel headers if needed, loads nvidia.ko, and runs nvidia-persistenced to keep the GPU initialized across idle periods
D) The driver container builds the NVIDIA driver from source code — it's a full source compilation taking 30+ minutes per node
✅ Correct Answer: C

The driver DaemonSet runs a privileged container on every GPU node. It detects the host kernel version, downloads the matching precompiled driver package, compiles the NVIDIA kernel module against the host's kernel headers (required for DKMS-based drivers), loads nvidia.ko/nvidia-uvm.ko/nvidia-modeset.ko kernel modules, and starts nvidia-persistenced to keep GPUs initialized. The driver container runs as a DaemonSet with hostNetwork, hostPID, and privileged access — necessary for kernel module loading and GPU device node creation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator Driver Container — Lifecycle
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DRIVER DAEMONSET LIFECYCLE (per GPU node)                           │
  │                                                                      │
  │  1. DETECT: Identify GPU model + host kernel version                 │
  │     → lspci | grep NVIDIA, uname -r                                  │
  │                                                                      │
  │  2. DOWNLOAD: Pull matching driver package from registry             │
  │     → nvcr.io/nvidia/driver:550.90.07-ubuntu22.04                   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  3. COMPILE (if needed): Compile kernel module against host headers  │
  │     → /usr/src/kernel-headers-$(uname -r) must be mounted           │
  │     → nvidia-modprobe builds nvidia.ko for this specific kernel      │
  │                                                                      │
  │  4. LOAD: Load kernel modules                                        │
  │     → modprobe nvidia → /dev/nvidia0...N created                    │
  │     → modprobe nvidia-uvm → Unified Virtual Memory                   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  5. PERSIST: Start nvidia-persistenced                               │
  │     → Keeps GPU initialized across idle periods                     │
  │     → Prevents 2-second wake-up delay on first GPU access            │
  │                                                                      │
  │  6. VALIDATE: Run nvidia-smi to confirm GPU visibility              │
  │     → Driver container stays Running as long as node is up           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the driver DaemonSet ensures every H100 node runs the validated driver 550.90.07. If a kernel update (apt upgrade) changes the kernel version, the DaemonSet automatically recompiles and reloads the driver within minutes — no manual intervention, no reboot required.

Container/Ops Q59 of 200
How does the nvidia-device-plugin register GPUs as Kubernetes resources, and what happens when a pod requests nvidia.com/gpu: 1?
A) The device plugin registers with kubelet via the Device Plugin API — it advertises nvidia.com/gpu as an extended resource with a count matching physical GPUs. When a pod requests nvidia.com/gpu: 1, kubelet calls the device plugin's Allocate() method, which returns the GPU device path and environment variables (NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES) for container creation
B) The device plugin edits the pod YAML to add GPU requests — it's a mutating admission webhook, not a kubelet plugin
C) The device plugin runs nvidia-smi periodically and updates a ConfigMap with GPU counts — pods read the ConfigMap to find available GPUs
D) The device plugin replaces the containerd runtime — it intercepts all container creation requests and injects GPU access
✅ Correct Answer: A

The nvidia-device-plugin implements the Kubernetes Device Plugin API (v1beta1). It registers a Unix socket at /var/lib/kubelet/device-plugins/nvidia-gpu.sock. Kubelet discovers this socket and calls ListAndWatch() to get the count and health of available GPUs. When a pod requests nvidia.com/gpu: 1, the scheduler filters nodes by GPU availability, then kubelet calls Allocate() on the device plugin with the pod's GPU request. The plugin returns: GPU device paths (/dev/nvidia0), environment variables (NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=GPU-<uuid>), and mount paths for driver libraries.

📝 Detailed Explanation
nvidia-device-plugin — Kubelet Device Plugin API Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. DEVICE PLUGIN REGISTRATION                                       │
  │  nvidia-device-plugin → Register(/var/lib/kubelet/device-plugins/)   │
  │  → Kubelet discovers plugin via Unix socket                          │
  │                                                                      │
  │  2. GPU ADVERTISEMENT                                                │
  │  kubelet → ListAndWatch() → device-plugin                            │
  │  device-plugin → ["GPU-0", "GPU-1", ..., "GPU-7"] (8 GPUs)          │
  │  kubelet updates Node.status.capacity: nvidia.com/gpu: 8             │
  │                                                                      │
  │  3. POD SCHEDULING                                                   │
  │  Pod: resources.requests.nvidia.com/gpu: 1                           │
  │  Scheduler: "GPU node has 8 allocatable, 0 used → Schedule here"     │
  │                                                                      │
  │  4. GPU ALLOCATION                                                   │
  │  kubelet → Allocate(pod, gpu_request=1) → device-plugin              │
  │  device-plugin returns:                                              │
  │    Envs:    NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=GPU-                        │
  │    Devices: /dev/nvidia0                                             │
  │    Mounts:  /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libcuda.so → container         │
  │                                                                      │
  │  5. CONTAINER CREATION                                               │
  │  containerd creates container with GPU env + devices + mounts        │
  │  → Container sees exactly 1 GPU, as requested                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the device plugin is what makes nvidia.com/gpu: 1 in a Deployment spec actually work. When the inference HPA scales from 3 to 8 pods, the scheduler sees 5 remaining GPUs, allocates them via the device plugin, and each new BERT inference pod gets its own dedicated H100 GPU with proper environment isolation.

Container/Ops Q60 of 200
What does the GPU Operator Validator do and how does it confirm the GPU stack is working end-to-end?
A) The validator checks the ClusterPolicy YAML for syntax errors — it doesn't actually test GPU functionality
B) The validator runs nvidia-smi once and reports the output — a simple pass/fail GPU enumeration check
C) The validator monitors GPU temperature and fan speed — it's a hardware health monitoring tool, not a functional test
D) The validator runs a comprehensive functional test suite: CUDA vectorAdd (compute), bandwidthTest (HBM bandwidth), deviceQuery (GPU properties), NCCL all-reduce (multi-GPU communication) — confirming every layer of the GPU stack works end-to-end before accepting production workloads
✅ Correct Answer: D

The GPU Operator Validator is a critical quality gate — it runs a complete functional test suite that validates every layer of the GPU stack: (1) CUDA vectorAdd — confirms CUDA runtime + driver work, (2) bandwidthTest — verifies HBM bandwidth matches expected spec, (3) deviceQuery — enumerates GPU properties (SM count, memory size, CUDA version), (4) NCCL all-reduce — validates multi-GPU communication via NVLink. Only after ALL tests pass does the validator report success — if any test fails, the GPU node is marked as not ready until the issue is resolved.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator Validator — Functional Test Suite Output
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VALIDATOR OUTPUT (successful run):                                  │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [PASS] Driver: NVIDIA-SMI 550.90.07, CUDA 12.4                      │
  │  [PASS] Toolkit: nvidia-container-toolkit v1.16.1                    │
  │  [PASS] CUDA DeviceQuery: Detected 8 × NVIDIA H100 80GB HBM3        │
  │  [PASS] CUDA VectorAdd: 1,048,576 elements, result=2.0, no errors   │
  │  [PASS] CUDA BandwidthTest: H2D 28.3 GB/s, D2H 29.1 GB/s            │
  │  [PASS] NCCL All-Reduce: 8 GPUs, 1 GB, 900 GB/s NVLink, 1.1 ms     │
  │  [PASS] DCGM: All GPU health checks OK                               │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────                            │
  │  RESULT: ALL 7 TESTS PASSED — GPU stack is healthy                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the validator runs as part of the GPU node readiness gate. A new H100 node is cordoned until the validator passes — only then is the node uncordoned and inference pods scheduled. If a weekly validator run detects NCCL bandwidth degradation (NVLink link failure), the node is automatically cordoned and the ops team is alerted.

Container/Ops Q61 of 200
How do you deploy the GPU Operator in an air-gapped environment with no internet access?
A) The GPU Operator cannot run in air-gapped environments — it requires constant access to nvcr.io for driver and container images
B) The GPU Operator has a built-in offline mode that caches all required images during initial internet-connected setup
C) All GPU Operator images and driver packages are mirrored to a local private registry (Harbor/Artifactory) — Helm values are updated to point to the local registry, and driver packages are pre-staged on an HTTP server accessible to GPU nodes
D) Air-gapped deployments use a special GPU Operator binary that includes all images embedded — a single 50 GB download replaces the need for a registry
✅ Correct Answer: C

Air-gapped GPU Operator deployment requires offline mirroring. On an internet-connected staging host: pull all GPU Operator images (operator, driver, toolkit, device-plugin, DCGM exporter, GFD, validator) from nvcr.io, retag them for the local registry, and push. Download driver packages (.run files) and host on a local HTTP server. Update Helm values to point repository and image fields to the local registry, and set driver.repository and driver URL to the local HTTP server. The operator then functions identically to the online deployment — all images and drivers come from local infrastructure.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator — Air-Gapped Deployment Flow
  ┌───────────────────────┐              ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │  STAGING (internet)   │              │  AIR-GAPPED (no internet)    │
  │                       │              │                              │
  │  Pull from nvcr.io:   │    ──────▶   │  Local Registry (Harbor)     │
  │  gpu-operator:v24.6   │   (push)    │  harbor.anih.local/nvidia/   │
  │  driver:550.90.07     │              │    gpu-operator:v24.6        │
  │  toolkit:v1.16.1      │              │    driver:550.90.07          │
  │  device-plugin:v0.15  │              │    toolkit:v1.16.1           │
  │  dcgm-exporter:3.3    │              │                              │
  │                       │              │  Local HTTP Server:          │
  │  Download drivers:    │    ──────▶   │  http://repo.anih.local/     │
  │  NVIDIA-Linux-*.run   │   (copy)    │    NVIDIA-Linux-x86_64.run   │
  └───────────────────────┘              └──────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost in a government/classified deployment, the GPU Operator runs entirely from the local Harbor registry and driver repository — zero internet dependency after initial mirroring. The weekly driver security patch process involves updating the mirrored images on the staging host, then syncing to the air-gapped registry via secure media transfer.

Container/Ops Q62 of 200
What is the recommended strategy for upgrading the GPU Operator and its managed components?
A) Delete all GPU workloads, uninstall the operator, install new version, redeploy workloads — a full teardown and rebuild
B) helm upgrade with the new operator version — the operator controller performs a rolling update of all sub-component DaemonSets, one GPU node at a time, with automatic health validation between nodes and automatic rollback if validation fails
C) The GPU Operator auto-updates itself from nvcr.io — no administrator action is needed, updates happen automatically
D) Each GPU node must be manually drained, driver unloaded, operator reinstalled, and uncordoned — the operator upgrade is manual per-node
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU Operator upgrades follow a rolling update strategy: (1) helm upgrade gpu-operator nvidia/gpu-operator --version v24.9.0 updates the operator controller and ClusterPolicy, (2) the operator controller rolls out driver DaemonSet updates one node at a time with maxUnavailable: 1, (3) after each node's driver update, the validator runs the full functional test suite, (4) if validation passes → proceed to next node, if validation fails → stop rollout and rollback. Existing GPU workloads continue running on unaffected nodes during the rollout.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator Rolling Upgrade — Node-by-Node
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  4-GPU-NODE CLUSTER UPGRADE:                                         │
  │                                                                      │
  │  Step 0: helm upgrade gpu-operator → operator controller updated     │
  │                                                                      │
  │  Step 1: Node 1 driver upgrade                                       │
  │    → Drain GPU pods from Node 1 (PDB ensures min 3 pods running)     │
  │    → Driver DaemonSet pod restarts with new driver 555.42.02         │
  │    → Validator runs functional tests on Node 1                       │
  │    → [PASS] → Uncordon Node 1 → GPU pods reschedule                  │
  │                                                                      │
  │  Step 2: Node 2 (same process)...                                    │
  │  Step 3: Node 3 (same process)...                                    │
  │  Step 4: Node 4 (same process)...                                    │
  │                                                                      │
  │  If ANY validator fails:                                             │
  │    → Rollout pauses → Alert ops team → Rollback via helm rollback   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, GPU Operator upgrades are scheduled during Sunday maintenance windows. With PodDisruptionBudget set to ensure at least 4 of 8 inference pods remain running, the rolling upgrade completes in ~40 minutes with zero downtime for job seekers — the inference service remains responsive throughout because at least 4 H100 GPUs are always available.

Container/Ops Q63 of 200
After deploying the GPU Operator, no GPUs appear in kubectl describe node. What is the systematic troubleshooting approach?
A) Check GPU Operator pod logs → verify driver DaemonSet is Running → check device plugin pod is Running → run nvidia-smi inside driver container → verify containerd GPU config → check kubelet logs for device plugin registration errors — follow the GPU stack layer by layer
B) Immediately reinstall the GPU Operator — configuration issues can only be fixed by a clean Helm uninstall + reinstall
C) Check if the GPU is physically connected by opening the server chassis — software debugging is unnecessary for GPU detection issues
D) Disable all GPU Operator sub-components and manually install each component — the operator is unreliable and manual setup is preferred
✅ Correct Answer: A

Troubleshooting "no GPUs in Kubernetes" follows a bottom-up stack verification: (1) Is the GPU physically detected? kubectl exec -it nvidia-driver-daemonset-xxx -- nvidia-smi, (2) Is the driver loaded? Check /dev/nvidia* exist, (3) Is containerd configured with nvidia runtime? grep nvidia /etc/containerd/config.toml, (4) Is the device plugin registered? Check kubelet logs for "device plugin registered", (5) Is the device plugin healthy? kubectl logs nvidia-device-plugin-xxx, (6) Does kubelet report GPU capacity? kubectl get node -o json | jq '.status.capacity'. The issue is almost always at one specific layer — systematic verification pinpoints it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator Troubleshooting — "No GPUs in Kubernetes" Checklist
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEBUGGING CHECKLIST: No GPUs in kubectl describe node              │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [ ] 1. Driver DaemonSet: kubectl get pods -n gpu-operator | grep driver
  │         → Must be Running on every GPU node                          │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [ ] 2. Driver loaded: kubectl exec driver-xxx -- nvidia-smi         │
  │         → Should list all GPUs with driver version                   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [ ] 3. containerd config: cat /etc/containerd/config.toml | grep nvidia
  │         → Must show [runtimes.nvidia] section                        │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [ ] 4. Device plugin pod: kubectl get pods -n gpu-operator | grep device-plugin
  │         → Must be Running, check logs for registration errors        │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [ ] 5. Kubelet device plugin: journalctl -u kubelet | grep device-plugin
  │         → "device plugin registered" for nvidia-gpu.sock             │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [ ] 6. Node capacity: kubectl describe node  | grep nvidia   │
  │         → nvidia.com/gpu: 8 ← Should show GPU count                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost ops, this checklist is laminated on the wall. 90% of "no GPUs" issues are resolved at step 3 (containerd config never applied after toolkit install). The remaining 10% are kernel header mismatches preventing driver compilation (step 1).

Container/Ops Q64 of 200
What GPU sharing and configuration options does the GPU Operator support through its ClusterPolicy?
A) The GPU Operator only supports exclusive GPU allocation — one GPU per pod, no sharing possible
B) The GPU Operator only supports MIG partitioning — time-slicing and other sharing strategies require different operators
C) GPU sharing is configured through Kubernetes ResourceQuotas — the GPU Operator doesn't control sharing, it only provides GPU access
D) The GPU Operator supports multiple GPU sharing/configuration strategies through ClusterPolicy: MIG (hardware partitioning via mig.strategy), time-slicing (via devicePlugin.config referencing a time-slicing ConfigMap), vGPU (via NVAIE vGPU manager integration), and GPU Feature Discovery for automatic node labeling by GPU capabilities
✅ Correct Answer: D

The GPU Operator is a unified platform for all GPU sharing and configuration strategies. Through ClusterPolicy: (1) MIG — mig.strategy: mixed enables hardware GPU partitioning for multi-tenant isolation, (2) Time-Slicing — a time-slicing ConfigMap referenced via devicePlugin.config.name enables multiple pods to share a GPU through time-division multiplexing, (3) vGPU — NVAIE license integration enables virtual GPU profiles for VM-based sharing, (4) GFD — GPU Feature Discovery auto-labels nodes with precise GPU model, memory, and capability information for intelligent scheduling.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator — Sharing & Config Strategies via ClusterPolicy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GPU SHARING STRATEGIES (ClusterPolicy):                             │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ MIG: Hardware partitioning for multi-tenant isolation            │ │
  │  │   mig.strategy: mixed  (allows mixed MIG + full GPU on A100/H100)│ │
  │  │   → 1 GPU → up to 7 isolated instances with dedicated HW        │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │ TIME-SLICING: Software interleaving for dev/CI                   │ │
  │  │   devicePlugin.config.name: time-slicing-config                  │ │
  │  │   ConfigMap: time-slicing-config with replicas: 4                │ │
  │  │   → 1 GPU appears as 4 allocatable GPUs (4 pods share via time)  │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │ vGPU: Virtual GPU for VM-based sharing (NVAIE license required) │ │
  │  │   vgpuManager.enabled: true                                      │ │
  │  │   → 1 GPU → multiple vGPU profiles (time-sliced + memory QoS)    │ │
  │  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │ GFD: Automatic GPU labeling for intelligent scheduling           │ │
  │  │   gfd.enabled: true                                              │ │
  │  │   → Labels: nvidia.com/gpu.product=H100-SXM-80GB                 │ │
  │  │   → Labels: nvidia.com/gpu.count=8, nvidia.com/gpu.memory=80     │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the GPU Operator is configured with time-slicing (4 replicas) for development namespaces (multiple dev pods share GPUs), and MIG (mixed strategy) on A100 nodes for production multi-tenant inference — each customer tenant gets a dedicated MIG slice with hardware isolation.

Container/Ops Q65 of 200
What is GPU Feature Discovery (GFD) and what labels does it automatically apply to GPU nodes?
A) GFD is a manual labeling tool — administrators run it to generate labels they must apply themselves
B) GFD is a DaemonSet that auto-discovers GPU hardware properties and applies labels like nvidia.com/gpu.product, nvidia.com/gpu.count, nvidia.com/gpu.memory, nvidia.com/cuda.driver-version, and nvidia.com/gpu.compute.major/minor — enabling GPU-aware scheduling without manual labeling
C) GFD is a GPU benchmark that discovers the maximum TFLOPS a GPU can achieve under load
D) GFD is a driver feature that auto-detects connected displays — it's for graphics output, not GPU compute labeling
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU Feature Discovery (GFD) runs as a DaemonSet on every GPU node. It queries the GPU via NVML (NVIDIA Management Library) and automatically applies Kubernetes labels describing the GPU hardware. Key labels include: nvidia.com/gpu.product=H100-SXM-80GB, nvidia.com/gpu.count=8, nvidia.com/gpu.memory=81559 (MiB), nvidia.com/cuda.driver-version=550.90.07, nvidia.com/gpu.compute.major=9 (Hopper = SM 9.0), nvidia.com/gpu.family=hopper. These labels enable precise nodeSelector and nodeAffinity rules — schedule workloads on H100 SXM, not L40S, without manually labeling nodes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
