For anihpj/jobpost, GFD labels enable precise GPU targeting: the BERT inference Deployment uses nodeSelector: nvidia.com/gpu.product=NVIDIA-H100-80GB-HBM3 to ensure it only runs on H100 nodes. If an A100 node joins the cluster, GFD labels it as A100, and inference pods won't accidentally schedule there — no manual labeling, no configuration drift.

Container/Ops Q66 of 200
A cluster has both A100 and H100 GPUs with different driver requirements. How does the GPU Operator handle this?
A) The GPU Operator can only manage one GPU model per cluster — you must create separate clusters for A100 and H100
B) The GPU Operator installs the driver common to both GPU models — potentially running an older driver on H100 to match A100 compatibility
C) The GPU Operator supports heterogeneous GPU fleets — the driver DaemonSet uses nodeSelector to deploy different driver versions to different GPU models, GFD labels identify each GPU type, and workloads use nodeSelector/nodeAffinity to target specific GPU models
D) All NVIDIA GPUs use the same driver — there are no version differences between GPU models, so multi-version management is unnecessary
✅ Correct Answer: C

The GPU Operator fully supports heterogeneous GPU fleets. Through the ClusterPolicy, you can define multiple driver configurations with nodeSelector targeting specific GPU models. The device plugin and GFD discover each GPU's model and capabilities, applying appropriate labels. Workloads use nodeSelector/nodeAffinity to target specific GPU models: training jobs → H100 SXM (high TDP, NVSwitch), inference → L40S (lower power, passive cooling), development → A100 (MIG for multi-tenant sharing). The operator manages all GPU types in a single cluster with their respective driver versions and configurations.

For anihpj/jobpost, the cluster runs H100 nodes for production inference (low latency, high throughput) and A100 nodes with MIG for multi-tenant development environments. 2.3 NGC Containers & GPU Image ManagementQ67-Q76

Container/Ops Q67 of 200
What is the NGC Catalog and how does it differ from Docker Hub for GPU container images?
A) NGC Catalog is an alternative name for Docker Hub — they are the same registry with different branding
B) NGC Catalog is NVIDIA's GPU-optimized container registry at nvcr.io — all images are tested with specific CUDA/cuDNN/NCCL versions, include NVIDIA performance optimizations, and come with security scan reports and Dockerfiles. Docker Hub images may not include GPU support or NVIDIA optimizations
C) NGC Catalog only contains NVIDIA's own software — it doesn't include third-party frameworks like PyTorch or TensorFlow
D) NGC Catalog is a local cache for Docker Hub — it mirrors popular images to reduce download times for GPU users
✅ Correct Answer: B

The NGC Catalog at nvcr.io is NVIDIA's curated registry of GPU-optimized containers. Every image is tested with a specific CUDA/cuDNN/NCCL combination, includes NVIDIA's performance patches (e.g., NCCL tuning for DGX), comes with SBOM and vulnerability scan reports, and includes the full Dockerfile for auditability. Docker Hub images (e.g., pytorch/pytorch) may work with GPUs but aren't optimized for specific NVIDIA hardware configurations and may have mismatched CUDA/cuDNN versions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NGC Catalog vs Docker Hub — GPU Container Comparison
  ┌──────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │                  │ NGC (nvcr.io/nvidia)    │ Docker Hub (pytorch/...) │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ CUDA Version     │ Pinned (e.g., 12.4)     │ May vary per tag         │
  │ cuDNN Version    │ Pinned (e.g., 9.1)      │ Not guaranteed            │
  │ NCCL Version     │ Pinned + DGX-optimized  │ Generic build             │
  │ Performance      │ NVIDIA-tuned kernels    │ Upstream defaults         │
  │ Security Scans   │ Included per image      │ Varies by publisher      │
  │ Dockerfile       │ Published for each image│ Not always available      │
  │ Support          │ NVIDIA-supported        │ Community                │
  │ Example          │ nvcr.io/nvidia/         │ pytorch/pytorch:2.4.0    │
  │                  │   pytorch:24.06-py3     │                          │
  └──────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost production, using NGC images ensures the BERT inference stack runs on a tested CUDA 12.4 + cuDNN 9.1 + NCCL 2.21 combination that NVIDIA has validated — eliminating the "works on my machine" problem caused by mismatched library versions from Docker Hub images.

Container/Ops Q68 of 200
How are NGC containers named and versioned, and what does nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch:24.06-py3 signify?
A) 24.06 means the container was built on June 24th — it follows a day-month format with no relationship to CUDA or framework versions
B) The naming is random — each container gets a unique hash with no semantic meaning
C) NGC containers use the format nvcr.io/nvidia/<framework>:<YY.MM>-<runtime> — 24.06 indicates June 2024 release with CUDA 12.4, cuDNN 9.1, and NCCL 2.21. py3 indicates Python 3. The YY.MM version corresponds to a fixed, tested CUDA/cuDNN/NCCL combination
D) 24.06 is the PyTorch version — NGC uses the same versioning as the framework, so 24.06 means PyTorch 24.06
✅ Correct Answer: C

NGC containers follow the naming convention nvcr.io/nvidia/<framework>:<YY.MM>-<runtime>. The YY.MM is NVIDIA's monthly release cadence (24.06 = June 2024), NOT the framework version. Each monthly release pins specific versions: CUDA 12.4, cuDNN 9.1, NCCL 2.21, PyTorch 2.4.0. The runtime suffix: py3 (Python 3 only), runtime (minimal), devel (includes compilers/tools). This versioning ensures reproducibility — nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch:24.06-py3 always contains the same tested software stack.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NGC Container Naming Convention
  nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch:24.06-py3
  │         │       │       │    └─ Runtime: py3 (Python 3)
  │         │       │       └─ Version: 24.06 = June 2024 release
  │         │       └─ Framework: pytorch
  │         └─ Publisher: nvidia (official NVIDIA)
  └─ Registry: nvcr.io (NVIDIA GPU Cloud Registry)

  What's inside nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch:24.06-py3:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ubuntu 22.04 LTS                                                     │
  │ NVIDIA Driver: 550.90.07 (not included — comes from host)            │
  │ CUDA Toolkit: 12.4.1                                                 │
  │ cuDNN: 9.1.1                                                         │
  │ NCCL: 2.21.5 (with DGX optimizations)                                │
  │ PyTorch: 2.4.0                                                       │
  │ torchvision: 0.19.0, torchaudio: 2.4.0                              │
  │ Python: 3.10.12                                                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost CI/CD, the Dockerfile always references the exact NGC image tag — FROM nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch:24.06-py3. When NVIDIA releases 24.07, the team tests it in staging before updating the Dockerfile. The pinned YY.MM format ensures the production inference container never changes unexpectedly.

For anihpj/jobpost CI/CD, the Dockerfile always references the exact NGC image tag — FROM nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch:24.06-py3. Container/Ops Q69 of 200
How do you select the right NGC base image for your AI framework, considering CUDA, cuDNN, and framework version compatibility?
A) Always use the latest tag — newer is always better and backward compatible with older CUDA versions
B) Choose based on the framework version only — CUDA and cuDNN versions are automatically matched by the NGC build system
C) Use the oldest supported tag for maximum stability — newer NGC images may have undiscovered bugs
D) Check the NGC release notes for your framework — each YY.MM release documents the pinned CUDA version, cuDNN version, and framework version. The host GPU driver must support the container's CUDA version (driver ≥ CUDA version). Choose the image whose CUDA version matches your driver's maximum supported CUDA
✅ Correct Answer: D

NGC image selection requires checking three compatibility layers: (1) Host driver → CUDA: the driver version dictates the maximum CUDA version supported (driver 550 supports CUDA ≤ 12.4), (2) CUDA → cuDNN: each NGC release pins a specific cuDNN version tested with that CUDA, (3) Framework → CUDA: PyTorch 2.4 requires CUDA 12.1+. The NGC release notes at docs.nvidia.com/deeplearning/frameworks document these combinations. Rule: nvidia-smi shows "CUDA Version: 12.4" — this is the MAXIMUM CUDA the driver supports, not the installed CUDA.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NGC Image Compatibility Matrix
  ┌──────────────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐
  │ NGC Release      │ CUDA     │ cuDNN    │ PyTorch  │ TF       │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┤
  │ 24.06 (Jun 2024) │ 12.4     │ 9.1      │ 2.4.0    │ 2.16.1   │
  │ 24.03 (Mar 2024) │ 12.3     │ 9.0      │ 2.3.1    │ 2.15.0   │
  │ 23.12 (Dec 2023) │ 12.3     │ 8.9      │ 2.2.0    │ 2.14.0   │
  └──────────────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┘

  CHECK: nvidia-smi → "CUDA Version: 12.4"
  → Driver MAX CUDA: 12.4 → NGC 24.06 (CUDA 12.4) = COMPATIBLE ✅
  → NGC 24.09 (CUDA 12.5) = NOT compatible ❌ (need driver 555+)

For anihpj/jobpost, the H100 nodes run driver 550.90.07 (max CUDA 12.4). The team uses NGC 24.06 (CUDA 12.4 + PyTorch 2.4 + cuDNN 9.1). When NGC 24.09 releases with CUDA 12.5, the team must upgrade to driver 555+ before adopting the new image.

Container/Ops Q70 of 200
How do you authenticate to pull containers from nvcr.io, and what is the NGC API key?
A) NGC containers are freely accessible without authentication — anyone can pull any image anonymously
B) You authenticate using an NGC API key generated from ngc.nvidia.com/setup — the username is always '$oauthtoken' and the password is your API key. This key is managed in the NGC portal, can be rotated, and is required for both docker login and Kubernetes imagePullSecrets
C) Authentication uses your NVIDIA developer forum username and password — the same credentials as the NVIDIA community site
D) NGC authentication requires a paid NVAIE license — free accounts cannot access any NGC containers
✅ Correct Answer: B

The NGC API key is a personal access token generated from ngc.nvidia.com/setup. Authentication uses the special username $oauthtoken (with the dollar sign) and the API key as the password. The key provides access to NGC containers, models, and Helm charts. API keys can be created, rotated, and revoked from the NGC portal. For CI/CD, generate a dedicated service account API key rather than using a personal key.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NGC API Key Authentication — Docker & Kubernetes
  # 1. Generate API key from: https://ngc.nvidia.com/setup/api-key
  #    Copy the key (shown only once!)

  # 2. Docker login
  docker login nvcr.io -u '$oauthtoken' -p <NGC_API_KEY>
  #    Note: Username is literally '$oauthtoken' with the dollar sign

  # 3. Kubernetes imagePullSecrets
  kubectl create secret docker-registry ngc-secret \
    --namespace=anihpj \
    --docker-server=nvcr.io \
    --docker-username='$oauthtoken' \
    --docker-password=<NGC_API_KEY>

  # 4. Use in Pod spec
  spec:
    imagePullSecrets:
    - name: ngc-secret
    containers:
    - image: nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch:24.06-py3

For anihpj/jobpost, the ngc-secret is created in every namespace that runs GPU workloads. The API key is stored in HashiCorp Vault and injected via External Secrets Operator — never committed to Git. Key rotation is performed quarterly via automated Vault policy.

Container/Ops Q71 of 200
A pod referencing an NGC image fails with ImagePullBackOff. You've verified the NGC API key is valid. What is the most likely cause?
A) The NGC registry is down — nvcr.io experiences frequent outages during business hours
B) The NGC image tag is incorrect — NGC images use a different naming convention than standard Docker images
C) The imagePullSecrets is missing from the Pod spec or the ServiceAccount — even with a valid key, Kubernetes won't use it unless explicitly referenced in the Pod or its ServiceAccount
D) NGC images require GPU nodes to pull — the pod must be scheduled on a GPU node before the image can be downloaded
✅ Correct Answer: C

imagePullSecrets must be explicitly referenced in the Pod spec or attached to the Pod's ServiceAccount. Creating the Secret alone is insufficient — Kubernetes doesn't automatically use registry credentials. The most common failure pattern: the Secret exists (kubectl get secret ngc-secret shows it), but the Pod spec or ServiceAccount doesn't reference it. Fix: add imagePullSecrets: [{name: ngc-secret}] to the Pod spec, or patch the ServiceAccount with the secret.

📝 Detailed Explanation
imagePullSecrets Debugging Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEBUG: Pod ImagePullBackOff on nvcr.io image                        │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [1] kubectl describe pod <name> | grep -A5 Events                  │
  │      → "pull access denied for nvcr.io/..." → Auth issue            │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [2] kubectl get secret ngc-secret → EXISTS?                        │
  │      → Yes, but is it referenced?                                    │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [3] kubectl get pod <name> -o yaml | grep imagePullSecrets        │
  │      → NOT FOUND → Secret exists but not referenced! ❌             │
  │                                                                      │
  │  FIX A: Patch the Pod (if recreatable):                              │
  │      spec.imagePullSecrets: [{name: ngc-secret}]                     │
  │                                                                      │
  │  FIX B: Patch the ServiceAccount (applies to all pods using it):     │
  │      kubectl patch sa default -p '{"imagePullSecrets":               │
  │        [{"name": "ngc-secret"}]}'                                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the default ServiceAccount in the anihpj namespace is patched with the ngc-secret at namespace creation time — all pods automatically inherit NGC pull access. This prevents new deployments from failing with ImagePullBackOff due to missing imagePullSecrets.

Container/Ops Q72 of 200
How do you build a custom GPU container image that extends an NGC base image with your application code?
A) Use a Dockerfile with FROM nvcr.io/nvidia/<framework>:<tag>, copy your application code, install additional Python/pip dependencies, and set your entrypoint — the NGC base provides the CUDA/cuDNN/NCCL stack, your layers add application logic
B) Replace the NGC image's entrypoint by overwriting /usr/bin/nvidia-smi — NGC images are immutable and cannot be extended
C) Install CUDA and cuDNN manually in your Dockerfile — extending NGC images is not recommended, you should build from ubuntu:22.04
D) Use docker commit on a running NGC container — the only way to customize NGC images is to modify a running container and commit the changes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Custom GPU images extend NGC base images just like any Docker multi-stage or single-stage build. The NGC image provides the GPU software stack (CUDA, cuDNN, NCCL, framework) — your Dockerfile adds application code, pip dependencies, configuration files, and the entrypoint. Key best practices: (1) pin the exact NGC tag (NOT :latest), (2) install pip packages with --no-cache-dir to reduce layer size, (3) use a non-root USER for production, (4) COPY application code after pip install for layer caching — code changes won't invalidate the pip cache.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Custom GPU Dockerfile Extending NGC PyTorch
  FROM nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch:24.06-py3  # GPU stack: CUDA 12.4 + PyTorch 2.4

  WORKDIR /app
  COPY requirements.txt .  # anihpj-specific Python deps
  RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt  # Django, gunicorn, transformers

  COPY . .  # Application code (done AFTER pip for layer caching)
  RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput

  EXPOSE 8000
  USER 1000:1000
  CMD ["gunicorn", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "anihpj.wsgi:application"]

For anihpj/jobpost, the custom inference image extends nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch:24.06-py3 with Django + HuggingFace Transformers + the BERT model loading code. The GPU stack (CUDA/cuDNN/NCCL) comes from NGC — only ~200 MB of application layers are added on top.

Container/Ops Q73 of 200
What are the best practices for optimizing GPU Docker image size and layer caching?
A) Combine all commands into a single RUN instruction — fewer layers always means smaller images
B) GPU images cannot be optimized — the CUDA and cuDNN libraries are inherently large and must be fully included
C) Always use the devel NGC variant for production — it includes debugging tools essential for troubleshooting
D) Use multi-stage builds (build stage with devel image, runtime with runtime image), order COPY/install from least to most frequently changing, use .dockerignore, clean pip/apt caches, use slim/runtime NGC variants, and verify with docker history
✅ Correct Answer: D

GPU image optimization follows Docker best practices with GPU-specific considerations: (1) use multi-stage builds with the NGC devel image for building and the NGC runtime image for production — saves 3-5 GB, (2) order layers from least to most frequently changing (OS packages → pip install → application code), (3) use .dockerignore to exclude .git, __pycache__, datasets, and checkpoints, (4) always use pip install --no-cache-dir and apt-get clean, (5) avoid including model weights in the image — mount them at runtime via PVC.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Image Layer Optimization Strategies
  ┌─────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Strategy                    │ Size Impact for GPU Images               │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Multi-stage (devel→runtime) │ 7.5 GB → 2.8 GB (62% reduction)         │
  │ NGC runtime vs devel base   │ runtime = ~1.2 GB, devel = ~2.5 GB       │
  │ --no-cache-dir (pip)        │ Saves ~500 MB of pip cache               │
  │ apt-get clean               │ Saves ~200 MB of package cache           │
  │ .dockerignore               │ Prevents .git (can be 100s of MB)        │
  │ Model weights in PVC        │ Saves 1-4 GB (BERT-large = 1.3 GB)      │
  │ Layer ordering              │ Pip cache hits → faster builds           │
  └─────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the inference image is 2.8 GB (vs 7.5 GB single-stage). Build times dropped from 8 minutes to 2 minutes (pip layer cached). The BERT model weights (~1.3 GB) are stored in a ReadWriteMany PVC and loaded at pod startup — changing the model doesn't require rebuilding the image.

Container/Ops Q75 of 200
How do you convert a trained PyTorch model to an optimized TensorRT engine using NGC containers?
A) TensorRT is a standalone compiler — you run it on the host, not inside a container
B) NGC containers for TensorRT are only for inference — model conversion must be done with a separate desktop tool
C) Pull the NGC TensorRT container (nvcr.io/nvidia/tensorrt:24.06-py3), export your PyTorch model to ONNX, then use trtexec (TensorRT CLI) or the Python API to parse the ONNX model, apply INT8/FP16 calibration, and serialize the optimized TensorRT engine — all within the container
D) TensorRT conversion modifies the original PyTorch model file in-place — no separate engine file is created, the conversion is permanent
✅ Correct Answer: C

The NGC TensorRT container provides the complete toolchain for model optimization. Workflow: (1) export PyTorch → ONNX via torch.onnx.export(), (2) use trtexec --onnx=model.onnx --saveEngine=model.plan --fp16 to build the optimized engine, (3) load the .plan file in Triton Inference Server or a custom TensorRT runtime. The optimization is GPU-specific — an engine built for H100 won't run on A100. For production, build separate engines for each GPU model in your cluster.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PyTorch → ONNX → TensorRT Engine Pipeline
  [1] PyTorch Model (.pt) ──▶ ONNX Model (.onnx)
       torch.onnx.export(model, dummy_input, "model.onnx")

  [2] ONNX Model ──▶ TensorRT Engine (.plan)
       trtexec --onnx=model.onnx --saveEngine=model.plan \
               --fp16 --workspace=4096
       # --fp16: FP16 precision (2× faster, <0.1% accuracy loss)
       # --int8: INT8 quantization (4× faster, requires calibration)

  [3] TensorRT Engine ──▶ Triton Inference Server
       # Place model.plan in Triton model repository
       # Triton loads engine in ms — no compilation at inference time

  Performance: PyTorch eager → 8ms, TensorRT FP16 → 2.5ms (3.2× faster)

For anihpj/jobpost, the BERT model is converted to TensorRT FP16 engine inside the NGC container during CI/CD. The optimized engine is stored in the model PVC. Triton loads it in under 100ms at pod startup, delivering 2.5ms inference latency — 3.2× faster than raw PyTorch serving.

Container/Ops Q76 of 200
What does a GPU-aware CI/CD pipeline look like for building, scanning, and deploying GPU container images?
A) Build GPU image → scan with Trivy/Grype (CUDA-specific CVEs) → push to private NGC/Harbor registry → deploy to staging GPU cluster → run DCGM validation tests → promote to production via GitOps (ArgoCD)
B) GPU CI/CD is identical to CPU CI/CD — no special steps are needed for GPU images because CUDA libraries are the same as CPU libraries for scanning purposes
C) GPU images cannot be scanned for vulnerabilities — security scanners don't understand CUDA library versions
D) GPU CI/CD requires physical access to GPU hardware in the CI runner — virtual/cloud CI runners cannot build GPU images
✅ Correct Answer: A

GPU CI/CD follows standard container CI/CD with GPU-specific additions. Key differences from CPU CI/CD: (1) vulnerability scanners must understand CUDA/cuDNN/NCCL packages (Trivy and Grype support NVIDIA package databases), (2) functional testing requires GPU nodes in the staging cluster (not the CI runner — testing happens post-deploy), (3) the pipeline must manage GPU-specific artifacts (TensorRT engines, NGC image tags, driver compatibility checks), (4) deployment uses GPU-aware scheduling (nodeSelector, tolerations for GPU nodes).

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU CI/CD Pipeline for anihpj/jobpost
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GPU CI/CD PIPELINE                                                   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [1] BUILD: docker build -t anihpj-inference:v2.1 .                  │
  │      → Multi-stage build with NGC base image                         │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [2] SCAN: trivy image anihpj-inference:v2.1                         │

2.4 GPU Driver Management in KubernetesQ77-Q84

Container/Ops Q77 of 200
What is the NVIDIA GPU driver lifecycle and how does the GPU Operator manage each phase?
A) The driver lifecycle is a single "install" step — once installed, it runs forever without management
B) The driver lifecycle is managed entirely by the Linux kernel — the GPU Operator has no role in driver management
C) The driver lifecycle follows install → load → validate → update → unload phases. The GPU Operator manages each: install (driver DaemonSet compiles/installs), load (modprobes nvidia.ko), validate (runs validator functional tests), update (rolling upgrade), unload (graceful node drain + driver removal)
D) The GPU Operator only handles the install phase — all other lifecycle phases are manual administrator responsibilities
✅ Correct Answer: C

The driver lifecycle has 5 phases automated by the GPU Operator: (1) Install — driver DaemonSet downloads and compiles the kernel module, (2) Load — modprobe loads nvidia.ko, creating /dev/nvidia* devices, (3) Validate — validator runs CUDA functional tests to confirm driver works, (4) Update — rolling upgrade with per-node validation, (5) Unload — graceful drain of GPU workloads, driver unload, node reboot if needed. The entire lifecycle is declarative through the ClusterPolicy CR.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Driver Lifecycle — State Machine
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DRIVER LIFECYCLE STATE MACHINE (managed by GPU Operator)            │
  │                                                                      │
  │  [Not Installed] ──Install──▶ [Installed] ──Load──▶ [Loaded]         │
  │        ▲                         │                    │               │
  │        │                         │                    ▼               │
  │        │                    [Failed]              [Validated]          │
  │        │                         ▲                    │               │
  │        └──────Unload─────────────┘                    ▼               │
  │                                                [Serving Workloads]    │
  │                                                       │               │
  │                                                       ▼               │
  │                                              [Update Available]       │
  │                                                       │               │
  │                                              Rolling Upgrade ────────┘
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the driver lifecycle is fully automated — new GPU nodes go from bare metal to "Serving Workloads" in ~7 minutes with zero human intervention. Driver updates are scheduled for Sunday maintenance and complete automatically with per-node validation.

Container/Ops Q78 of 200
How do you determine which NVIDIA driver version is compatible with your GPU model, CUDA version, and Linux kernel?
A) All NVIDIA drivers are universal — any driver version works with any GPU, CUDA version, and kernel
B) Use nvidia-smi to auto-select the best driver — the tool downloads and installs the optimal version automatically
C) Choose the latest driver always — backward compatibility ensures it works with older GPUs and kernels
D) Check NVIDIA's driver release notes for GPU support (H100 requires R525+, Blackwell requires R550+), CUDA compatibility (driver ≥ CUDA toolkit version), and kernel compatibility (driver supports specific kernel versions) — use nvidia-smi to verify after installation
✅ Correct Answer: D

Driver compatibility follows a matrix: GPU → Minimum driver: H100 requires R525+, B200 requires R550+, A100 works with R450+. Driver → Max CUDA: Driver 550 supports CUDA ≤ 12.4, Driver 535 supports CUDA ≤ 12.2. Driver → Kernel: Each driver release supports specific kernel versions — NVIDIA publishes the supported kernel list. For production, pin the driver version and test kernel updates in staging before rolling out.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NVIDIA Driver Compatibility Matrix
  ┌──────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Driver   │ Min GPU Arch │ Max CUDA     │ Supported Kernels (Ubuntu)    │
  ├──────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ R550     │ Maxwell+     │ 12.4         │ 5.15 (22.04), 6.5 (24.04)    │
  │ R545     │ Pascal+      │ 12.3         │ 5.15 (22.04)                 │
  │ R535     │ Volta+       │ 12.2         │ 5.15 (22.04)                 │
  │ R525     │ Turing+      │ 12.0         │ 5.15 (22.04)                 │
  └──────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  VERIFY: nvidia-smi → Driver Version: 550.90.07 → CUDA Version: 12.4 ✅

For anihpj/jobpost H100 nodes running Ubuntu 22.04 (kernel 5.15), driver 550.90.07 is the validated combination. The team checks NVIDIA's release notes monthly for critical security patches and tests new driver versions in staging before production rollout.

Container/Ops Q79 of 200
What is the difference between the precompiled and kernel-open NVIDIA driver types in the GPU Operator?
A) Kernel-open drivers are open-source replacements for the proprietary driver — they provide identical performance to precompiled drivers
B) Precompiled drivers are the traditional proprietary nvidia.ko module — kernel-open drivers split the driver into a proprietary firmware blob and an open-source kernel module, improving compatibility with newer Linux kernels at the cost of some GPU features (no SLI, limited power management) on older GPUs
C) Precompiled drivers are faster because they bypass the Linux kernel entirely — kernel-open drivers go through the kernel scheduler
D) Kernel-open drivers are only for data center GPUs — precompiled drivers are only for consumer GPUs
✅ Correct Answer: B

Since driver R515, NVIDIA offers kernel-open modules alongside traditional precompiled modules. The kernel-open variant splits GPU initialization into an open-source kernel module (GPU system processor, memory management, display) and a proprietary firmware blob (CUDA, Tensor Cores, NVLink). For Turing+ GPUs (T4, A100, H100, L40S), both variants provide equivalent performance and features. For older GPUs (Volta and earlier), stick with precompiled. The GPU Operator selects kernel-open automatically for compatible GPUs when driver.type: kernel-open is set in ClusterPolicy.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Precompiled vs Kernel-Open Driver Architecture
  ┌────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Precompiled (legacy)   │ Kernel-Open (R515+, Turing+ GPUs)            │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ nvidia.ko (proprietary)│ nvidia.ko (OPEN SOURCE — GSP, memory, disp)  │
  │ All GPU functions in   │ nvidia-drm.ko (OPEN SOURCE — DRM/KMS)       │
  │ one closed-source .ko  │ GPU firmware blob (PROPRIETARY — CUDA, etc.) │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ✅ All GPUs supported  │ ✅ Turing+ recommended (H100, A100, L40S)    │
  │ ✅ All features        │ ⚠️ Limited on pre-Turing (no SLI, power mgmt)│
  │                        │ ✅ Better kernel compatibility (in-tree goal) │
  └────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost H100 nodes, the kernel-open driver is used — it provides identical performance with better kernel compatibility, simplifying Ubuntu kernel security updates without driver recompilation delays.

Container/Ops Q80 of 200
What is the recommended procedure for updating the NVIDIA driver on a running GPU node without disrupting production workloads?
A) Run apt upgrade nvidia-driver-550 on the node — the driver hot-reloads without disrupting running GPU processes
B) Immediately reboot the node with the new driver — GPU workloads will restart automatically via Kubernetes self-healing
C) Cordon the node → drain GPU pods (respecting PDB) → update driver via GPU Operator → validator runs functional tests → uncordon node → GPU pods reschedule. Repeat node-by-node with maxUnavailable: 1 to maintain cluster GPU capacity
D) Delete all GPU pods cluster-wide, update all nodes simultaneously, redeploy pods — a brief 5-minute downtime for all GPU workloads
✅ Correct Answer: C

Driver updates must be treated as disruptive operations — the kernel module must be unloaded and reloaded, which kills all processes using the GPU (nvidia-smi, CUDA contexts). The GPU Operator orchestrates a node-by-node rolling update: (1) cordon node, (2) drain GPU pods (kubelet evicts pods using GPU, respecting PDB), (3) driver container restarts with new driver version, (4) validator confirms GPU stack healthy, (5) uncordon node. With PDB ensuring min pods available, production inference continues uninterrupted on other nodes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Driver Rolling Update — Safe Procedure
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  4-GPU-NODE CLUSTER — DRIVER UPDATE                                  │
  │                                                                      │
  │  kubectl cordon gpu-node-1                                          │
  │  kubectl drain gpu-node-1 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data │
  │    → PodDisruptionBudget ensures min 3 inference pods still Running  │
  │  helm upgrade gpu-operator --set driver.version=555.42.02            │
  │    → Driver DaemonSet on node-1 restarts with new driver             │
  │  validator completes → ALL TESTS PASSED ✅                            │
  │  kubectl uncordon gpu-node-1                                         │
  │    → Inference pods reschedule on node-1                             │
  │                                                                      │
  │  Repeat for node-2, node-3, node-4                                    │
  │  → Zero downtime for job seekers!                                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, driver updates run during Sunday maintenance with PDB ensuring minimum 4 of 8 inference pods. Each node drain/reschedule cycle takes ~5 minutes — the full fleet update completes in ~20 minutes with zero user-facing downtime.

Container/Ops Q81 of 200
How does nvidia-smi communicate with the NVIDIA driver kernel module?
A) nvidia-smi communicates via standard Linux sysfs — reading /sys/class/drm/card0/device/gpu_stats files
B) nvidia-smi uses the NVIDIA Management Library (libnvidia-ml.so) which communicates with the nvidia.ko kernel module via ioctl calls through /dev/nvidiactl — providing query, monitoring, and management capabilities
C) nvidia-smi reads GPU registers directly via PCIe MMIO — it bypasses the kernel driver entirely for maximum performance
D) nvidia-smi is a network service that queries GPUs via TCP — the driver exposes a REST API on port 9999
✅ Correct Answer: B

nvidia-smi uses NVML (NVIDIA Management Library) — libnvidia-ml.so — which communicates with the kernel driver through ioctl system calls on /dev/nvidiactl. NVML provides a structured API for querying GPU state (temperature, utilization, memory, ECC, power), managing GPU configuration (MIG, clocks, ECC mode), and receiving events (XID errors, thermal warnings). This is the same library used by DCGM, nvidia-docker, and GPU monitoring tools — nvidia-smi is just the CLI frontend to NVML.

📝 Detailed Explanation
nvidia-smi Communication Path
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  nvidia-smi (CLI)                                                     │
  │       │                                                              │
  │       ▼                                                              │
  │  libnvidia-ml.so (NVML — NVIDIA Management Library)                  │
  │       │ ioctl() calls via /dev/nvidiactl                             │
  │       ▼                                                              │
  │  nvidia.ko (Kernel Module)                                           │
  │       │ MMIO (Memory-Mapped I/O)                                     │
  │       ▼                                                              │
  │  GPU Hardware (registers, sensors, counters)                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost monitoring, the DCGM Exporter also uses NVML — same library, different consumer. If nvidia-smi works but DCGM doesn't, the issue is with the DCGM hostengine, not the driver or NVML.

Container/Ops Q82 of 200
What happens when the NVIDIA driver crashes or the GPU falls off the PCIe bus, and how do you recover?
A) The Linux kernel automatically reloads the NVIDIA driver — no manual intervention or reboot is needed
B) The GPU automatically resets itself within 30 seconds and reconnects to the driver — transparent recovery
C) When the driver crashes (XID error, GPU falls off bus), all CUDA applications on that GPU receive errors and must be restarted. Recovery requires: drain GPU node, unload/reload nvidia.ko (or reboot), run DCGM diagnostics to confirm GPU health, then uncordon the node. nvidia-persistenced helps prevent idle-time GPU sleep but doesn't auto-recover crashes
D) Driver crashes are impossible — the NVIDIA driver has built-in ECC and redundancy that prevents any crash scenario
✅ Correct Answer: C

NVIDIA driver crashes are not self-recovering. When the GPU hits an XID error (e.g., XID 79: GPU has fallen off the bus), the driver marks the GPU as inaccessible, all CUDA contexts on that GPU are terminated, and applications receive CUDA errors. Recovery: (1) drain the node via Kubernetes, (2) unload nvidia.ko with rmmod nvidia (may require killing processes still holding /dev/nvidia*), (3) reload with modprobe nvidia or reboot if rmmod fails, (4) run dcgmi diag -r 3 to verify GPU health, (5) uncordon only if diagnostics pass.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Driver Crash Recovery Procedure
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. DETECT: XID 79 in dmesg → "GPU has fallen off the bus"          │
  │     nvidia-smi → "Unable to determine the device handle"             │
  │                                                                      │
  │  2. DRAIN: kubectl cordon gpu-node-01 && kubectl drain --force       │
  │                                                                      │
  │  3. RECOVER: rmmod nvidia_uvm nvidia_drm nvidia_modeset nvidia       │
  │     → If rmmod fails → reboot (systemctl reboot)                     │
  │     → modprobe nvidia (if rmmod succeeded)                            │
  │                                                                      │
  │  4. VALIDATE: dcgmi diag -r 3 → ALL TESTS PASSED?                    │
  │     → YES: uncordon node                                              │
  │     → NO: Keep cordoned → RMA GPU                                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, GPU driver crashes trigger a PagerDuty alert via DCGM XID monitoring. The on-call AIOps engineer follows this runbook. If the GPU fails DCGM diagnostics after recovery, the node stays cordoned and the GPU is RMA'd — inference capacity is maintained by the remaining 7 GPUs on other nodes.

Container/Ops Q83 of 200
What are all the methods to check the installed NVIDIA driver version on a Linux system?
A) nvidia-smi (top line shows Driver Version), modinfo nvidia | grep version, cat /proc/driver/nvidia/version, dpkg -l | grep nvidia-driver (Ubuntu), rpm -qa | grep nvidia-driver (RHEL) — each method queries a different layer of the driver stack
B) Only nvidia-smi shows the driver version — all other methods return inaccurate information about the graphics stack
C) The driver version is stored in /etc/nvidia-driver.conf — it's a text configuration file set during installation
D) The driver version is encoded in the GPU firmware — it can only be read via a physical jumper on the GPU board
✅ Correct Answer: A

Multiple methods verify the driver version at different layers: (1) nvidia-smi — queries the running driver via NVML, shows Driver Version + CUDA Version in the header, (2) modinfo nvidia | grep ^version — reads the kernel module's embedded version string, (3) cat /proc/driver/nvidia/version — reads the driver's procfs entry, (4) package manager — shows installed package version. All should match — discrepancies indicate a partial install or multiple driver versions on the system.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Driver Version — Verification Methods
  $ nvidia-smi | head -1
  NVIDIA-SMI 550.90.07    Driver Version: 550.90.07    CUDA Version: 12.4

  $ modinfo nvidia | grep ^version
  version:        550.90.07

  $ cat /proc/driver/nvidia/version
  NVRM version: NVIDIA UNIX x86_64 Kernel Module  550.90.07

  $ dpkg -l | grep nvidia-driver
  ii  nvidia-driver-550  550.90.07-0ubuntu1  amd64  NVIDIA driver metapackage

For anihpj/jobpost node validation, the Ansible playbook checks all 4 methods and asserts they match — preventing the "driver mismatch" issues where nvidia-smi reports one version but the loaded kernel module is different, a common problem after partial driver upgrades.

Container/Ops Q84 of 200
What is the difference between DKMS and precompiled NVIDIA driver installation methods?
A) DKMS drivers are faster because they skip kernel compilation — precompiled drivers must be rebuilt for every kernel update
B) Precompiled drivers are open-source while DKMS drivers are proprietary — the license model is the main difference
C) DKMS is the only supported method for NVIDIA drivers — the precompiled method was deprecated in driver R450
D) DKMS automatically rebuilds the kernel module when the Linux kernel is updated (apt upgrade) — essential for systems with automatic security updates. Precompiled drivers are matched to a specific kernel version and don't auto-rebuild, making them simpler but requiring manual reinstallation after kernel updates
✅ Correct Answer: D

DKMS (Dynamic Kernel Module Support) registers the NVIDIA kernel module with the DKMS framework. When the kernel is updated (e.g., apt upgrade installs a new kernel), DKMS automatically rebuilds nvidia.ko against the new kernel headers at boot time — the GPU works immediately after reboot. Precompiled drivers are built for a specific kernel version and don't auto-rebuild. For Kubernetes GPU nodes with automatic security updates, DKMS is strongly recommended — it prevents the "driver not found after kernel update" issue that causes GPU pods to fail after unattended upgrades.

📝 Detailed Explanation
DKMS vs Precompiled — Kernel Update Behavior
  ┌────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ DKMS                   │ Precompiled                                  │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Kernel update → DKMS   │ Kernel update → nvidia.ko INCOMPATIBLE      │
  │ auto-rebuilds nvidia.ko│ → nvidia-smi fails                          │
  │ → GPU works immediately│ → Must manually reinstall driver             │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Recommended:           │ Use when:                                    │
  │ ✅ Auto-updating sys   │ • Immutable OS (Flatcar, CoreOS)             │
  │ ✅ K8s GPU nodes       │ • GPU Operator manages driver (container)    │
  │ ✅ Ubuntu with u-a-t   │ • Kernel version pinned (no auto-update)     │
  └────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost Ubuntu GPU nodes with unattended-upgrades enabled, DKMS ensures GPU drivers survive kernel security patches. The GPU Operator uses precompiled driver containers (kernel-independent) which achieve the same goal through a different mechanism — the driver runs in a container matching the host kernel.

2.5 GPU Operator Advanced ConfigurationQ85-Q90

Container/Ops Q85 of 200
How does the GPU Operator configure MIG profiles declaratively through ClusterPolicy?
A) MIG must be configured manually via nvidia-smi — the GPU Operator cannot manage MIG profiles
B) The ClusterPolicy mig section defines MIG strategy and profiles — mig.strategy: mixed enables MIG, and the MIG Manager runs mig-parted with the declarative profile configuration to partition GPUs. The device plugin then advertises MIG devices as separate allocatable GPU resources
C) MIG configuration is done through a separate ConfigMap — the ClusterPolicy only enables/disables the MIG Manager
D) MIG via GPU Operator only supports single strategy — all GPUs must use the same MIG profile, mixed configurations are not supported
✅ Correct Answer: B

The GPU Operator manages MIG declaratively through ClusterPolicy with mig.strategy: mixed. The MIG Manager (one-shot Job) runs on each MIG-capable GPU node with a mig-parted configuration defining which MIG profiles to create. The device plugin then discovers the MIG instances and advertises each as a separate allocatable GPU resource with labels indicating the MIG profile (e.g., nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb). Pods request specific MIG profiles: nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb: 1.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator MIG Configuration via ClusterPolicy
  ClusterPolicy:
    mig:
      strategy: mixed  # single | mixed | none
      config:
        name: custom-mig-config
        default: all-1g.5gb  # Default profile for unspecified GPUs

  MIG ConfigMap (custom-mig-config):
    config.yaml: |
      version: v1
      mig-configs:
        all-1g.5gb:
          - devices: all
            mig-enabled: true
            profiles:
              - device: 0
                profile: 1g.5gb
                count: 7

For anihpj/jobpost A100 nodes, MIG is configured with 7× 1g.5gb slices for multi-tenant development. The device plugin advertises 7 MIG devices per A100, and dev pods request nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb: 1 — each team gets a dedicated, hardware-isolated GPU slice.

For anihpj/jobpost A100 nodes, MIG is configured with 7× 1g.5gb slices for multi-tenant development. The device plugin advertises 7 MIG devices per A100, and dev pods request nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb: 1 — each team gets a dedicated, hardware-isolated GPU slice.

Container/Ops Q86 of 200
How does the GPU Operator configure time-slicing to allow multiple pods to share a single GPU?
A) Time-slicing is a hardware feature built into H100 GPUs — no GPU Operator configuration is needed
B) Time-slicing is configured via nvidia-smi on each node — the GPU Operator cannot manage time-slicing configurations
C) A time-slicing ConfigMap is created with the desired number of replicas per GPU, referenced in ClusterPolicy via devicePlugin.config.name — the device plugin then advertises N replicas per GPU, and the GPU scheduler time-slices between pods at the CUDA context level
D) Time-slicing is always enabled with 8 replicas per GPU — it's a fixed default that cannot be changed
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU time-slicing is configured via a ConfigMap and referenced in ClusterPolicy. The ConfigMap defines how many replicas each physical GPU should appear as: replicas: 4 makes one H100 appear as 4 allocatable GPUs. Kubernetes schedules up to 4 pods on the same GPU, and the GPU driver time-slices between CUDA contexts. Unlike MIG (hardware isolation), time-sliced pods share the GPU and can interfere with each other — suitable for development/CI, not for production multi-tenant isolation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Time-Slicing Configuration
  # Time-Slicing ConfigMap
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: ConfigMap
  metadata:
    name: time-slicing-config
  data:
    any: |-
      version: v1
      flags:
        migStrategy: none
      sharing:
        timeSlicing:
          resources:
          - name: nvidia.com/gpu
            replicas: 4  # 1 physical GPU → 4 allocatable GPUs

  # ClusterPolicy references the ConfigMap
  devicePlugin:
    config:
      name: time-slicing-config
      default: any

For anihpj/jobpost dev namespaces, time-slicing with 4 replicas allows 4 developers to share one H100 for model experimentation — each gets 25% GPU time on average. Production inference uses dedicated GPUs (no time-slicing) for guaranteed latency.

B) The GPU Operator directly writes GPU metrics to Prometheus TSDB — no exporter component is needed

D) The GPU Operator exports GPU metrics via SNMP — Prometheus integration requires an SNMP-to-Prometheus bridge

Container/Ops Q88 of 200
How does the GPU Operator support GPU workload isolation through sandboxing?
A) GPU sandboxing requires a separate security product — the GPU Operator doesn't provide sandboxing capabilities
B) Sandboxing is done by running each GPU pod in a separate VM — the GPU Operator integrates with VMware vSphere for isolation
C) The GPU Operator uses Linux cgroups to limit GPU memory per container — standard Kubernetes resource limits provide GPU sandboxing
D) The GPU Operator supports container runtime sandboxing (gVisor, Kata Containers) for GPU workloads — the sandbox runtime provides an additional isolation layer between GPU containers and the host kernel, useful for untrusted or multi-tenant GPU workloads
✅ Correct Answer: D

For GPU workloads requiring additional security isolation (multi-tenant SaaS, untrusted code execution), the GPU Operator supports sandboxed container runtimes like gVisor and Kata Containers. These runtimes add a kernel-level isolation layer between the container and the host, preventing GPU container escapes. The GPU Operator's validator tests sandboxed GPU access — confirming nvidia-smi and CUDA operations work correctly through the sandbox. Note: sandboxing adds 5-10% GPU performance overhead from the virtualization layer.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Operator Sandboxing — Isolation Layers
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT SANDBOXING:          │  WITH gVisor/Kata:                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────┐      │  ┌────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │ GPU Container      │      │  │ GPU Container              │      │
  │  │ (shared host kernel)│      │  │ (sandboxed kernel)         │      │
  │  └────────┬───────────┘      │  └────────────┬───────────────┘      │
  │           │                  │               │                       │
  │  ┌────────┴───────────┐      │  ┌────────────┴───────────────┐      │
  │  │ Host Kernel        │      │  │ Sandbox (gVisor/Kata)     │      │
  │  │ (direct GPU access) │      │  │ → Filtered GPU access     │      │
  │  └────────────────────┘      │  └────────────┬───────────────┘      │
  │                              │               │                       │
  │                              │  ┌────────────┴───────────────┐      │
  │                              │  │ Host Kernel (isolated)     │      │
  │                              │  └────────────────────────────┘      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost multi-tenant SaaS deployment, GPU sandboxing ensures that even if one customer's inference container is compromised, the attacker cannot escape to the host or access other customers' GPU memory — critical for SOC 2 and GDPR compliance.

Container/Ops Q89 of 200
What labels does GPU Feature Discovery (GFD) apply and how do they enable GPU-aware scheduling?
A) GFD only labels nodes as "has-gpu: true" — a simple boolean flag for GPU presence
B) GFD applies detailed GPU labels (product name, memory size, compute capability, driver version, MIG capability, GPU family) — enabling nodeSelector rules like gpu.product=H100-SXM-80GB to precisely target specific GPU models for different workload requirements
C) GFD labels are user-defined — administrators manually specify what labels to apply, GFD only reads them back
D) GFD applies taints to GPU nodes, not labels — taints are the primary mechanism for GPU-aware scheduling
✅ Correct Answer: B

GFD auto-discovers and labels GPU nodes with: nvidia.com/gpu.product (H100-SXM-80GB), nvidia.com/gpu.count (8), nvidia.com/gpu.memory (81559 MiB), nvidia.com/gpu.compute.major (9 for Hopper), nvidia.com/gpu.family (hopper), nvidia.com/mig.capable (true/false), nvidia.com/cuda.driver-version (550.90.07). These labels enable precise scheduling: training → H100 SXM (high TDP, NVSwitch), inference → L40S (passive cooling), MIG dev → A100 (MIG capable).

For anihpj/jobpost, GFD labels ensure inference pods only schedule on H100 nodes (lowest latency), while A100 nodes with MIG are reserved for development — all enforced automatically by Kubernetes scheduler via nodeSelector, with zero manual labeling maintenance.

What is the difference between the NVIDIA GPU Operator and the NVIDIA AI Enterprise (NVAIE) Operator?
A) They are the same product with different names — GPU Operator is the open-source name, NVAIE Operator is the licensed name
B) The NVAIE Operator manages AI frameworks while the GPU Operator manages drivers — they handle completely different layers
C) The GPU Operator is the open-source Kubernetes operator for GPU lifecycle management (drivers, toolkit, device plugin). The NVAIE Operator is a commercial add-on that extends the GPU Operator with NVAIE-specific features: vGPU management, license server integration, DLS/CLS configuration, and NVAIE-validated driver/framework combinations with enterprise support
D) The NVAIE Operator replaces the GPU Operator entirely — you cannot use both in the same cluster
✅ Correct Answer: C

The GPU Operator is free and open-source — it handles the core GPU lifecycle (driver, toolkit, device plugin, MIG, DCGM). The NVAIE Operator is part of the licensed NVIDIA AI Enterprise suite — it builds on the GPU Operator adding vGPU management, DLS license server configuration, NVAIE-validated driver/framework bundles, and enterprise support integration. Both can coexist: the GPU Operator manages the base GPU stack, and the NVAIE Operator adds enterprise features on top. For most Kubernetes GPU deployments, the open-source GPU Operator is sufficient.

For anihpj/jobpost, the open-source GPU Operator is sufficient — it manages H100 drivers, device plugin, DCGM monitoring, and GFD labels. 🟡 Core: Making GPUs Usable — Partitioning, Sharing, and Scheduling

This category is about how to partition GPUs with MIG, share them between workloads, and schedule AI jobs efficiently. Think of it like managing a fleet of high-performance sports cars — you need to schedule who drives which car, when, and ensure no car sits idle. For anihpj/jobpost, this covers how the BERT inference and training workloads share the GPU cluster through MIG partitioning and intelligent scheduling.

Q91-Q140 50 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

3.1 MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) ConfigurationQ91-Q104

Sched/MIG Q91 of 200
What is MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) and how does it differ from other GPU sharing technologies?
A) MIG is a software scheduler that time-slices GPU access between containers — similar to GPU time-slicing but with stricter quotas
B) MIG is a hardware-level GPU partitioning technology that physically divides one GPU into up to 7 isolated instances — each with dedicated SM slices, memory, cache, and memory bandwidth. Unlike time-slicing or MPS, MIG provides hardware-enforced isolation with predictable performance and no cross-instance interference
C) MIG is a cloud service that provides virtual GPU instances over the network — it doesn't run on physical GPUs
D) MIG combines multiple physical GPUs into one large virtual GPU — it's the opposite of partitioning, it's aggregation
✅ Correct Answer: B

MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) is a hardware-level feature on A100, A30, and H100 GPUs that physically partitions a single GPU die into multiple independent GPU instances. Each MIG instance gets dedicated: Streaming Multiprocessor slices, GPU memory with ECC, L2 cache, and memory bandwidth. With MIG, one A100 80GB can become 7 isolated "mini-GPUs" — each running completely independent workloads with no cross-instance interference. Unlike time-slicing (software interleaving), MIG provides guaranteed, predictable performance because resources are physically partitioned, not shared.

📝 Detailed Explanation
A100 80GB MIG Partitioning — 7 Isolated GPU Instances
  ONE A100 80GB GPU → 7 MIG INSTANCES:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐  │
  │  │ MIG 0    │ │ MIG 1    │ │ MIG 2    │ │ MIG 3    │ │ MIG 4-6  │  │
  │  │ 1g.5gb   │ │ 1g.5gb   │ │ 1g.5gb   │ │ 1g.5gb   │ │ 1g.5gb   │  │
  │  │ 1 SM     │ │ 1 SM     │ │ 1 SM     │ │ 1 SM     │ │ 1 SM     │  │
  │  │ 5 GB HBM │ │ 5 GB HBM │ │ 5 GB HBM │ │ 5 GB HBM │ │ 5 GB HBM │  │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘  │
  │  7 independent instances — each a fully functional GPU               │
  │  Hardware isolation: separate memory, cache, compute — no sharing!   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, MIG on A100 nodes enables 7 different development teams to each get a dedicated, isolated GPU slice. One team's PyTorch experiment cannot crash another team's inference workload — hardware isolation guarantees this.

Sched/MIG Q92 of 200
Which NVIDIA GPUs support MIG, and what are the maximum MIG instances per GPU?
A) All NVIDIA GPUs from Turing onwards support MIG — it's a universal feature of modern GPU architectures
B) Only the H100 supports MIG — it was introduced with the Hopper architecture and is exclusive to H100/H200
C) MIG is supported on A100 (max 7 instances), A30 (max 4 instances), H100 (max 7 instances), and H200 (max 7 instances) — it requires Ampere architecture or newer, and is NOT available on V100, T4, L40S, or consumer GPUs
D) MIG is a software feature that works on any GPU with the GPU Operator installed — hardware support is not required
✅ Correct Answer: C

MIG requires hardware support in the GPU silicon — it cannot be enabled through software alone. Supported GPUs: A100 (up to 7 instances, Ampere architecture), A30 (up to 4 instances), H100/H200 (up to 7 instances, Hopper architecture). NOT supported: V100 (Volta), T4 (Turing), L40S (Ada Lovelace), RTX consumer GPUs, L4, A10, A40. MIG must be explicitly enabled via nvidia-smi -mig 1 and requires a GPU reset — all running workloads on that GPU must be stopped first.
