  ├──────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ A100 80GB    │ Ampere (SM80) │ ✅ Yes           │ 7 (1g.5gb × 7)      │
  │ A100 40GB    │ Ampere (SM80) │ ✅ Yes           │ 7 (1g.5gb × 7)      │
  │ A30 24GB     │ Ampere (SM80) │ ✅ Yes           │ 4 (1g.6gb × 4)      │
  │ H100 80GB    │ Hopper (SM90) │ ✅ Yes           │ 7 (1g.5gb × 7)      │
  │ H200 141GB   │ Hopper (SM90) │ ✅ Yes           │ 7                    │
  ├──────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ V100 32GB    │ Volta (SM70)  │ ❌ No            │ N/A                  │
  │ T4 16GB      │ Turing (SM75) │ ❌ No            │ N/A                  │
  │ L40S 48GB    │ Ada (SM89)    │ ❌ No            │ N/A                  │
  └──────────────┴───────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the A100 nodes were explicitly chosen because they support MIG — enabling multi-tenant GPU sharing for development teams. H100 inference nodes run without MIG (full GPU performance for BERT serving). L40S nodes (no MIG) are used for CI/CD GPU testing where isolation isn't required.

For anihpj/jobpost, the A100 nodes were explicitly chosen because they support MIG — enabling multi-tenant GPU sharing for development teams. H100 inference nodes run without MIG (full GPU performance for BERT serving). L40S nodes (no MIG) are used for CI/CD GPU testing where isolation isn't required.

Sched/MIG Q93 of 200
What are the standard MIG profiles available on an A100 80GB GPU?
A) 7g.80gb (1 full GPU), 4g.40gb (2 instances), 3g.40gb (2 instances/some combo), 2g.20gb (4 instances), 1g.10gb (7 instances), 1g.5gb (7 instances) — plus mixed combinations like 3g.20gb + 2×2g.10gb
B) Only 1g.5gb and 7g.80gb — MIG supports exactly 1 or 7 instances with no intermediate profiles
C) Any arbitrary combination of SM count and memory size — MIG profiles are user-defined with no restrictions
D) MIG profiles are determined by the Linux kernel — the GPU driver has no control over partition sizes
✅ Correct Answer: A

MIG profiles follow a g.memory naming convention: the "g" number represents the fraction of total GPU compute (SMs), and "memory" is the dedicated HBM in GB. On A100 80GB, profiles range from 1g.5gb (1/7 compute, 5 GB) to 7g.80gb (full GPU). Mixed configurations are possible (e.g., 3g.20gb + 2× 2g.10gb). On H100, profiles are similar but with HBM3 memory sizes. Profile selection depends on workload: small inference → 1g.5gb, medium training → 2g.20gb, large training → 7g.80gb.

📝 Detailed Explanation
A100 80GB MIG Profile Chart
  ┌─────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Profile     │ GPU Compute  │ Memory       │ Max Instances per A100 80GB   │
  ├─────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ 7g.80gb     │ 100%         │ 80 GB       │ 1 (full GPU)                  │
  │ 4g.40gb     │ ~57%         │ 40 GB       │ 2 (on A100 80GB)              │
  │ 3g.40gb     │ ~43%         │ 40 GB       │ 2 (with 1g.10gb remaining)   │
  │ 2g.20gb     │ ~29%         │ 20 GB       │ 4                            │
  │ 1g.10gb     │ ~14%         │ 10 GB       │ 7 (on A100 40GB = 4 on 80GB) │
  │ 1g.5gb      │ ~14%         │ 5 GB        │ 7                            │
  └─────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost dev environments, A100 nodes are configured with 7× 1g.5gb MIG slices — each developer gets a 5 GB GPU slice sufficient for BERT fine-tuning experiments on small datasets. Production training jobs use full 7g.80gb GPUs without MIG.

Sched/MIG Q94 of 200
How do you enable MIG mode on an A100 and what precautions must you take?
A) MIG is enabled by default — no explicit action is needed, just create MIG instances
B) Run nvidia-smi -mig 1 at any time — MIG enabling is a live operation that doesn't affect running processes
C) MIG is enabled through the BIOS — it's a motherboard setting, not a driver command
D) Run nvidia-smi -mig 1 on the target GPU — this REQUIRES a GPU reset, meaning ALL processes using that GPU must be stopped first. In Kubernetes, drain the GPU node, stop all GPU pods, then enable MIG, then recreate MIG instances, then uncordon the node
✅ Correct Answer: D

Enabling MIG is a destructive operation — nvidia-smi -mig 1 triggers a GPU reset that kills ALL running CUDA processes and clears GPU memory. The GPU becomes unavailable for 10-30 seconds during the reset. In Kubernetes: (1) cordon the node, (2) drain GPU pods, (3) ssh to node and run nvidia-smi -mig 1, (4) create MIG instances with nvidia-smi mig -cgi, (5) uncordon node. The GPU Operator MIG Manager automates this via ClusterPolicy.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Enabling MIG — Safe Procedure
  # 1. Drain GPU node in Kubernetes
  kubectl cordon gpu-node-01
  kubectl drain gpu-node-01 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data

  # 2. Enable MIG mode (GPU reset — DESTRUCTIVE!)
  nvidia-smi -mig 1
  # GPU resets, ALL processes killed → ~15 second outage

  # 3. List available MIG profiles
  nvidia-smi mig -lgip
  # Shows: 1g.5gb, 2g.10gb, 3g.20gb, 4g.20gb, 7g.40gb, etc.

  # 4. Create MIG instances
  nvidia-smi mig -cgi 19,19,19,19,19,19,19  # 7× 1g.5gb

  # 5. Uncordon node
  kubectl uncordon gpu-node-01

For anihpj/jobpost, MIG enabling is part of the A100 node provisioning playbook. Nodes are drained during initial setup, MIG is enabled via the GPU Operator's MIG Manager declaratively, and validation confirms all 7 instances are healthy before the node is uncordoned and dev pods are scheduled.

Sched/MIG Q95 of 200
How do you create and destroy MIG instances, and what are GPU Instances vs Compute Instances?
A) MIG instances are permanent once created — they cannot be destroyed without factory-resetting the GPU
B) GPU Instances (GI) are physical partitions of GPU resources (SM + memory). Compute Instances (CI) are created inside GIs and represent CUDA contexts. Created via nvidia-smi mig -cgi (create GI) and -cci (create CI). Destroyed via -dgi and -dci. CI count within a GI can be changed without recreating the GI
C) GPU Instances and Compute Instances are the same thing — the terms are used interchangeably with no functional difference
D) MIG instances are created via a YAML file applied to the GPU — there's no CLI for MIG management, only declarative configuration
✅ Correct Answer: B

MIG has two levels: GPU Instances (GI) are physical partitions — each GI gets dedicated SM slices and GPU memory. Compute Instances (CI) are created within a GI and represent CUDA execution contexts. A single GI can contain multiple CIs (e.g., one 1g.5gb GI with 2 CIs = two processes can share that MIG slice). Commands: nvidia-smi mig -cgi <profile_id> creates GIs from a profile, nvidia-smi mig -cci creates CIs within a GI, nvidia-smi mig -dci/dgi destroys them.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Instance (GI) vs Compute Instance (CI)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  A100 80GB GPU                                                    │
  │  ┌───────────────────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐          │
  │  │ GI 0: 3g.20gb         │  │ GI 1:    │  │ GI 2:    │          │
  │  │ ┌─────────┐ ┌───────┐ │  │ 2g.10gb  │  │ 2g.10gb  │          │
  │  │ │ CI 0    │ │ CI 1  │ │  │ ┌───────┐ │  │ ┌───────┐ │          │
  │  │ │ CUDA ctx│ │ CUDA  │ │  │ │ CI 0  │ │  │ │ CI 0  │ │          │
  │  │ └─────────┘ └───────┘ │  │ └───────┘ │  │ └───────┘ │          │
  │  └───────────────────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, each 1g.5gb MIG slice uses one GI with one CI — one CUDA context per BERT inference pod, providing hardware isolation between development teams sharing the same physical A100.

Sched/MIG Q96 of 200
How does Kubernetes expose MIG devices to pods, and how do you request a specific MIG profile?
A) MIG devices appear as standard GPUs — pods request nvidia.com/gpu: 1 and the scheduler randomly assigns a MIG slice
B) The nvidia-device-plugin-MIG exposes each MIG profile as a separate resource type — pods request nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb: 1 for a specific profile. The scheduler matches the request to a node with that exact MIG profile available, ensuring the right GPU slice is allocated
C) MIG devices are exposed via a custom CSI driver — pods mount MIG slices as volumes, not as resource requests
D) MIG in Kubernetes requires a special MIG scheduler plugin — the default Kubernetes scheduler cannot handle MIG resources
✅ Correct Answer: B

With MIG enabled, the device plugin advertises each MIG profile as a separate extended resource: nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb, nvidia.com/mig-2g.10gb, etc. Pods request the exact profile they need. The scheduler filters nodes by MIG resource availability. This ensures a pod requesting a 1g.5gb slice never accidentally gets a larger 7g.80gb GPU — resource types prevent mismatch.

📝 Detailed Explanation
MIG Resources in Kubernetes — How Pods Request MIG Slices
  Node Status (after MIG configuration + device plugin restart):
  kubectl describe node gpu-node-01 | grep nvidia.com
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb:     7    ← 7× 1g.5gb MIG slices   │
  │ nvidia.com/mig-2g.10gb:    3    ← 3× 2g.10gb MIG slices  │
  │ nvidia.com/mig-3g.20gb:    2    ← 2× 3g.20gb MIG slices  │
  │ nvidia.com/mig-7g.40gb:    1    ← 1× 7g.40gb MIG slice   │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Pod A (dev):    resources: nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb: 1   → Gets 5 GB slice
  Pod B (train):  resources: nvidia.com/mig-3g.20gb: 1  → Gets 20 GB slice
  Pod C (CI/CD):  resources: nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb: 1   → Gets another 5 GB

  ResourceQuota (per namespace):
    apiVersion: v1
    kind: ResourceQuota
    spec:
      hard:
        nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb: "2"  # Max 2 MIG slices per dev namespace

For anihpj/jobpost dev environments, each team's namespace has a ResourceQuota limiting nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb to 2 — preventing any team from monopolizing all MIG slices on a shared A100 node.

Sched/MIG Q97 of 200
What is mig-parted and how does it configure MIG declaratively?
A) mig-parted is a partitioning tool for NVMe drives — it has nothing to do with GPU MIG configuration
B) mig-parted is a GUI tool for visually designing MIG layouts — it generates nvidia-smi commands for manual execution
C) mig-parted is NVIDIA's declarative MIG configuration tool — a YAML config defines desired MIG profiles per GPU, and mig-parted applies them idempotently (only changing what differs from current state). The GPU Operator MIG Manager uses mig-parted internally to apply ClusterPolicy MIG configurations
D) mig-parted replaces nvidia-smi entirely — all GPU management is done through mig-parted, not nvidia-smi
✅ Correct Answer: C

mig-parted is a declarative configuration tool for MIG. It reads a YAML specification describing desired MIG profiles per GPU and applies them idempotently — only changing GPUs where the current MIG configuration differs from the desired state. This is critical for GPU Operator integration: the MIG Manager runs mig-parted with the ClusterPolicy's MIG config, ensuring all nodes converge to the declared MIG state. Manual nvidia-smi changes are detected and corrected by mig-parted on the next reconciliation cycle.

📝 Detailed Explanation
mig-parted Declarative MIG Configuration
  # mig-parted config.yaml
  version: v1
  mig-configs:
    dev-partition:
      - devices: [0,1,2,3]        # GPUs 0-3: MIG for dev
        mig-enabled: true
        profiles:
          - profile: 1g.5gb
            count: 7               # 7 × 1g.5gb per GPU
      - devices: [4,5,6,7]        # GPUs 4-7: Full GPU for training
        mig-enabled: false

  # Apply: nvidia-mig-parted apply -f config.yaml
  # Status: nvidia-mig-parted status

For anihpj/jobpost, mig-parted configuration is stored in Git and applied via GPU Operator ClusterPolicy. GPUs 0-3 on each A100 node are MIG-partitioned for development; GPUs 4-7 remain full for CI/CD GPU testing.

Sched/MIG Q98 of 200
Why does MIG GPU instance placement matter for CPU-GPU affinity and NUMA topology?
A) MIG instances are independent of CPU topology — any MIG slice can be used by any CPU core with identical performance
B) Each MIG instance is physically connected to a specific PCIe root complex tied to one CPU socket — placing a pod on a CPU core from the wrong NUMA node causes all GPU data to cross the UPI/QPI interconnect, reducing PCIe bandwidth by 30-50%. Topology-aware scheduling ensures the pod's CPU and its MIG slice are on the same NUMA node
C) NUMA only affects system RAM, not GPU memory — GPU-MIG placement is independent of CPU topology by design
D) MIG automatically handles NUMA placement through the GPU firmware — no Kubernetes scheduling configuration is needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

In dual-socket GPU servers, each GPU is PCIe-attached to one CPU socket. A pod running on CPU socket 0 but using a GPU on socket 1 must traverse the UPI/QPI interconnect for ALL GPU data transfers — adding latency and reducing effective PCIe bandwidth. The Kubernetes Topology Manager with single-numa-node policy ensures the pod's CPU cores, system memory, and GPU MIG slice all reside on the same NUMA node. With MIG, each slice inherits the parent GPU's NUMA affinity.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NUMA-Aware MIG Placement
  ┌──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │ NUMA Node 0 (CPU Socket 0)  │ NUMA Node 1 (CPU Socket 1)  │
  │ CPU cores: 0-55              │ CPU cores: 56-111            │
  │ RAM: 1 TB                    │ RAM: 1 TB                    │
  │ GPU 0 ── PCIe Gen4 x16 ──┐  │ GPU 4 ── PCIe Gen4 x16 ──┐  │
  │ GPU 1 ── PCIe Gen4 x16 ──┤  │ GPU 5 ── PCIe Gen4 x16 ──┤  │
  │ GPU 2 ── PCIe Gen4 x16 ──┤  │ GPU 6 ── PCIe Gen4 x16 ──┤  │
  │ GPU 3 ── PCIe Gen4 x16 ──┘  │ GPU 7 ── PCIe Gen4 x16 ──┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘
  Pod on NUMA 0 using MIG from GPU 7 (NUMA 1) = 50% bandwidth loss!
  Fix: Topology Manager → single-numa-node policy

For anihpj/jobpost DGX nodes, the Topology Manager with single-numa-node policy ensures BERT inference pods are scheduled on CPU cores matching their GPU's NUMA node — preserving full PCIe bandwidth and avoiding the 30-50% performance penalty of cross-NUMA GPU access.

Sched/MIG Q99 of 200
After enabling MIG, your pods requesting nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb stay Pending. What is the most likely cause?
A) MIG is not supported on your GPU model — check nvidia-smi mig -lgip to confirm MIG capability
B) The MIG instances were created with the wrong profile ID — destroy and recreate with correct profiles
C) The Kubernetes scheduler doesn't recognize MIG resources — upgrade to Kubernetes 1.27+ for MIG support
D) You're using the standard nvidia-device-plugin instead of nvidia-device-plugin-MIG — the standard device plugin advertises GPUs as nvidia.com/gpu (full GPU), not as MIG slices. The MIG device plugin is required to expose individual MIG profiles as allocatable resources
✅ Correct Answer: D

The most common MIG-Kubernetes issue: MIG is enabled and instances are created (nvidia-smi shows them), but the standard device plugin ignores MIG and advertises full GPUs. The MIG device plugin (nvidia-device-plugin-MIG) must be deployed, either through GPU Operator ClusterPolicy (mig.strategy: mixed) or as a standalone DaemonSet with MIG-specific configuration. Without it, pods requesting nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb find zero nodes with that resource.

📝 Detailed Explanation
MIG Troubleshooting Checklist
  [1] nvidia-smi mig -lgi → Shows MIG instances? YES ✅
  [2] kubectl get pods -n gpu-operator | grep mig → MIG Manager Ran? YES ✅
  [3] kubectl describe node | grep mig → MIG resources listed?
      → nvidia.com/mig-1g.5gb: 7 ← CORRECT
      → (nothing) ← MIG device plugin NOT deployed! ❌
  [4] FIX: Enable MIG strategy in ClusterPolicy:
      mig.strategy: mixed
      → GPU Operator deploys MIG device plugin automatically

For anihpj/jobpost, this was the #1 issue during initial MIG rollout — the ops team spent 2 hours debugging why MIG slices existed but pods wouldn't schedule, only to discover the standard device plugin was running instead of the MIG variant. The fix was one line in ClusterPolicy.

Sched/MIG Q100 of 200
How do you disable MIG and return a GPU to full-GPU mode, and what happens to existing MIG instances?
A) nvidia-smi -mig 0 disables MIG mode — ALL MIG instances are destroyed, all data in MIG GPU memory is lost, and the GPU resets to full-GPU mode. This requires stopping all processes using any MIG instance on that GPU first
B) MIG instances can be individually disabled while others remain active — nvidia-smi mig -dci disables specific instances without affecting others
C) Disabling MIG is not possible once enabled — it's a one-way operation that permanently partitions the GPU
D) MIG automatically disables when all MIG instances are idle for 5 minutes — the GPU returns to full mode dynamically
✅ Correct Answer: A

Disabling MIG is equally disruptive as enabling it. nvidia-smi -mig 0 triggers a GPU reset that destroys ALL MIG instances on that GPU — every GI and CI is deleted, GPU memory is cleared, and the GPU restarts in full monolithic mode. All processes using MIG slices are killed. In Kubernetes: drain the node, disable MIG, uncordon. The GPU Operator handles this automatically when you change the ClusterPolicy MIG strategy to none — the MIG Manager runs mig-parted to disable MIG on affected nodes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Disabling MIG — Safe Node Draining Procedure
  STEP 1: Cordon & Drain GPU Node
    kubectl cordon gpu-node-01
    kubectl drain gpu-node-01 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
    → All GPU pods gracefully evicted, MIG slices released

  STEP 2: Disable MIG Mode (requires GPU reset!)
    nvidia-smi -mig 0
    → GPU resets all SMs and HBM
    → All MIG instances destroyed (~15 second reset time)
    → GPU returns to full-GPU mode

  STEP 3: Verify & Uncordon
    nvidia-smi -q | grep "MIG Mode"   → "MIG Mode: Disabled"
    kubectl uncordon gpu-node-01
    → Node ready, now shows nvidia.com/gpu: 1 (full GPU, not MIG slices)

  ⚠️  IMPORTANT: MIG disable/inable requires GPU RESET.
      All running GPU processes on that GPU will be killed!
      Always drain the node first or data will be lost.

For anihpj/jobpost, MIG is disabled when a full-GPU training job needs the entire A100. The node is drained during a maintenance window, MIG is disabled, and the training job runs on the full 80GB GPU. After training completes, MIG is re-enabled for multi-tenant development.

Sched/MIG Q101 of 200
A startup needs GPU sharing for 4 developers working on small PyTorch models. One developer works with sensitive customer data requiring strict isolation. Which GPU sharing strategy should you recommend?
A) Time-slicing for all 4 developers — it's the simplest to configure and provides equal GPU access
B) MPS for all 4 developers — MPS provides the best performance for multi-process GPU sharing
C) vGPU for all 4 developers — vGPU provides memory QoS guarantees suitable for sensitive workloads
D) MIG for the developer with sensitive data (hardware isolation), time-slicing for the other 3 developers (simpler setup, acceptable isolation for non-sensitive work) — mixed strategies per GPU via GPU Operator ClusterPolicy
✅ Correct Answer: D

GPU sharing strategies differ fundamentally in isolation strength. MIG provides hardware-level isolation (dedicated SM, memory, cache) — required for sensitive/regulated data. Time-slicing provides no isolation — pods share GPU memory and can interfere. MPS shares a CUDA context — least isolation. The GPU Operator supports mixed strategies: some GPUs configured for MIG, others for time-slicing, via ClusterPolicy and node labels.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Sharing Strategies — Decision Matrix
  ┌────────────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐
  │ Strategy       │ Isolation│ Overhead │ Fragmentation│ Best For  │
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┤
  │ MIG            │ ✅ HARDWARE│ ~0%    │ Yes (fixed│ Regulated │
  │                │ dedicated│          │ profiles) │ multi-tenant│
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┤
  │ Time-Slicing   │ ❌ None  │ ~5% ctx │ No        │ Dev/CI/CD │
  │                │          │ switch   │           │           │
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┤
  │ MPS            │ ❌ None  │ ~0%      │ No        │ Single-user│
  │                │ shared CUDA│        │           │ multi-proc │
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┤
  │ vGPU           │ ⚠️ SW QoS│ ~8% virt │ Configurable│ VDI/VM  │
  └────────────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: MIG on A100s for production tenant isolation, time-slicing on L40S for dev/CI. The GPU Operator ClusterPolicy configures this per node-group via nodeSelector — A100 nodes get MIG, L40S nodes get time-slicing, H100 nodes stay full-GPU for inference.

Sched/MIG Q102 of 200
Which scenario is best suited for MIG rather than other GPU sharing strategies?
A) A single user running 4 PyTorch training jobs simultaneously on one GPU — MPS is more efficient for single-user multi-process
B) A SaaS platform with 7 different customer tenants, each needing guaranteed GPU performance with provable isolation for SOC 2 compliance — MIG provides hardware-enforced memory and compute isolation that auditors can verify
C) A CI/CD pipeline running short-lived GPU tests — time-slicing with 8 replicas provides better GPU utilization
D) A single large LLM training job that needs all GPU memory — MIG partitioning would reduce available memory, use full GPU instead
✅ Correct Answer: B

MIG's killer use case is regulated multi-tenant GPU sharing. Financial services, healthcare, and government customers require provable data isolation — MIG's hardware-level memory and cache separation satisfies audit requirements that software-based sharing (time-slicing, MPS) cannot. Real-world examples: a bank running 7 trading models from different desks on one A100 (each in a MIG slice), a hospital running 3 different AI diagnostic models with patient data isolation, a government agency separating classified and unclassified AI workloads on the same physical GPU.

📝 Detailed Explanation
MIG Use Cases — When to Choose MIG
  ┌────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ USE MIG                │ DON'T USE MIG                                │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ✅ Multi-tenant SaaS   │ ❌ Single-user multi-process (use MPS)       │
  │ ✅ Regulated industry  │ ❌ CI/CD short-lived jobs (use time-slicing) │
  │ ✅ SOC2/GDPR/HIPAA     │ ❌ Large model training (need full GPU)      │
  │ ✅ Dev + Prod on same  │ ❌ GPU without MIG support (V100, T4, L40S)  │
  │    physical GPU        │ ❌ Dynamic resource needs (MIG is static)     │
  └────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  anihpj MIG setup: A100 nodes: 7×1g.5gb for dev teams, 2×3g.20gb for staging

For anihpj/jobpost multi-tenant SaaS, MIG on A100 nodes provides each customer tenant a dedicated 1g.5gb MIG slice. SOC 2 auditors can verify that customer A's GPU memory is physically isolated from customer B's — no software vulnerability can cross the MIG hardware boundary.

Sched/MIG Q103 of 200
What happens when you run nvidia-smi -mig 0 to disable MIG on a running GPU?
A) MIG instances gracefully shut down — running CUDA processes complete, then instances are destroyed
B) Only idle MIG instances are destroyed — active instances continue running until processes exit
C) ALL MIG instances are immediately destroyed — GPU resets, all CUDA processes are killed, GPU memory is cleared, and the GPU returns to full monolithic mode. This is a destructive operation requiring a GPU reset
D) MIG instances are paused and can be resumed later — disable is non-destructive, MIG state is preserved across resets
✅ Correct Answer: C

Disabling MIG is just as destructive as enabling it. All GI/CI instances are destroyed, all CUDA contexts terminated, all GPU memory cleared. The GPU resets and restarts in full monolithic mode. This takes 10-30 seconds. Any process holding a reference to a MIG device receives a CUDA error. In Kubernetes: all pods using MIG slices on that GPU will crash and restart (or be evicted). Always drain the node before disabling MIG.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Disabling MIG — What Happens
  Before: A100 with 7× MIG slices (7 dev pods running)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ MIG 0 │ MIG 1 │ MIG 2 │ MIG 3 │ MIG 4 │ MIG 5 │ MIG 6 │         │
  │ 1g.5gb│ 1g.5gb│ 1g.5gb│ 1g.5gb│ 1g.5gb│ 1g.5gb│ 1g.5gb│         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  nvidia-smi -mig 0 → GPU RESET (10-30s)
  → All 7 MIG instances DESTROYED
  → All 7 pods CRASH (CUDA error)
  → GPU returns to full 80GB mode

  After: A100 full GPU (ready for large training job)

For anihpj/jobpost, MIG disable is scheduled during maintenance windows. The GPU Operator automates this: change ClusterPolicy MIG strategy to none → operator drains node → disables MIG → uncordons node. Pods are rescheduled to other nodes during drain (PDB ensures minimum capacity).

Sched/MIG Q104 of 200
How does CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES work with MIG instances, and what CUDA API functions manage MIG?
A) CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES cannot be used with MIG — MIG instances are managed exclusively through nvidia-smi
B) CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES accepts MIG device UUIDs (e.g., MIG-GPU-//) to select specific MIG instances. The CUDA MIG Management API provides functions for enumerating, querying, and managing MIG devices programmatically
C) MIG instances are automatically selected by CUDA — the runtime picks the best available MIG slice based on workload requirements
D) CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES only sees full GPUs — MIG instances are invisible to CUDA and can only be used through the MIG-specific nvidia-mig API
✅ Correct Answer: B

MIG instances appear as CUDA-capable devices with their own UUIDs. CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES accepts MIG UUIDs to select specific instances. The CUDA API provides cudaGetDeviceCount() (counts MIG instances + full GPUs), cudaSetDevice() (selects a MIG instance), and cudaDeviceGetAttribute() with MIG-specific attributes. The nvidia-container-toolkit sets CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES to the specific MIG UUID allocated by the Kubernetes device plugin, ensuring the container only sees its assigned MIG slice.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES with MIG
  # List MIG devices visible to CUDA
  $ CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-GPU-/7/0 nvidia-smi
  # Shows only the specific MIG Compute Instance

  # CUDA API for MIG
  cudaGetDeviceCount(&count);  // count = MIG instances + full GPUs
  cudaSetDevice(0);             // Select first visible MIG instance
  cudaDeviceGetAttribute(&val, cudaDevAttrMigEnabled, 0);  // Check MIG

For anihpj/jobpost containers, the Kubernetes device plugin sets CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES to the assigned MIG slice UUID — the BERT inference code just calls torch.cuda.is_available() and PyTorch sees exactly the MIG slice allocated, not the full GPU.

3.2 GPU Sharing & Scheduling in KubernetesQ105-Q116

Sched/MIG Q105 of 200
How does Kubernetes track and allocate nvidia.com/gpu as a resource, and what happens when a pod requests it?
A) nvidia.com/gpu is a standard Kubernetes resource like CPU and memory — it's built into the kubelet and always available
B) nvidia.com/gpu is a custom metric used by HPA — it doesn't affect pod scheduling, only autoscaling decisions
C) nvidia.com/gpu is an extended resource advertised by the nvidia-device-plugin via the Device Plugin API — kubelet adds it to node capacity, the scheduler filters nodes by GPU availability, and kubelet calls the device plugin's Allocate() to assign a specific GPU to the pod
D) nvidia.com/gpu is a label on nodes, not a schedulable resource — pods use nodeSelector to find GPU nodes
✅ Correct Answer: C

nvidia.com/gpu is an extended resource — not built into Kubernetes, but dynamically registered by the device plugin. The flow: (1) device plugin registers with kubelet, (2) kubelet calls ListAndWatch() → gets GPU count → updates node status with nvidia.com/gpu: 8, (3) pod requests nvidia.com/gpu: 1, (4) scheduler filters nodes by GPU allocatable count, (5) kubelet calls device plugin Allocate() → gets GPU UUID + env vars + device mounts, (6) container starts with assigned GPU.

📝 Detailed Explanation
nvidia.com/gpu — Extended Resource Lifecycle in Kubernetes
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  nvidia.com/gpu Resource Lifecycle                         │
  │                                                            │
  │  1. Device Plugin registers with kubelet:                  │
  │     → "I have 8 GPUs of type nvidia.com/gpu"              │
  │                                                            │
  │  2. Kubelet updates Node Status:                           │
  │     Node: dgx-1                                            │
  │       capacity: nvidia.com/gpu: 8                          │
  │       allocatable: nvidia.com/gpu: 8                       │
  │                                                            │
  │  3. Pod requests GPU:                                      │
  │     resources:                                             │
  │       limits: nvidia.com/gpu: 2                            │
  │                                                            │
  │  4. Scheduler filters nodes:                               │
  │     dgx-1: allocatable=8 ≥ 2 requested → ELIGIBLE ✅       │
  │     dgx-2: allocatable=1 < 2 requested → INELIGIBLE ❌     │
  │                                                            │
  │  5. Kubelet calls DevicePlugin.Allocate(GPU:0, GPU:1)      │
  │     → Returns: /dev/nvidia0, /dev/nvidia1 device paths     │
  │     → Sets: NVIDIA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0,1 env in container    │
  │                                                            │
  │  6. Allocatable decrements: 8 → 6 (2 GPUs now in use)      │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, each inference pod requests nvidia.com/gpu: 1. With 8 H100 GPUs per node, up to 8 inference pods run per GPU node. The scheduler ensures no GPU is double-allocated — each pod gets exclusive access to its assigned GPU.

For anihpj/jobpost, each inference pod requests nvidia.com/gpu: 1. With 8 H100 GPUs per node, up to 8 inference pods run per GPU node. The scheduler ensures no GPU is double-allocated — each pod gets exclusive access to its assigned GPU.

Sched/MIG Q106 of 200
How do you ensure GPU workloads only schedule on GPU nodes using labels and selectors?
A) GPU scheduling is automatic — Kubernetes detects GPUs and only schedules GPU pods on GPU nodes without labels
B) GPU Feature Discovery (GFD) auto-labels nodes with nvidia.com/gpu.present=true — use nodeSelector or nodeAffinity in pod specs to target GPU nodes. Without this, GPU pods may schedule on CPU-only nodes and fail with CUDA errors
C) GPU nodes must be in a separate Kubernetes cluster — you cannot mix GPU and CPU nodes in the same cluster
D) Use taints to prevent GPU pods from scheduling on CPU nodes — labels are not sufficient for GPU scheduling
✅ Correct Answer: B

GFD automatically labels GPU nodes with nvidia.com/gpu.present: "true". Pods use nodeSelector: {nvidia.com/gpu.present: "true"} to target GPU nodes. Without nodeSelector, a GPU pod requesting nvidia.com/gpu: 1 may still schedule on a GPU node (scheduler filters by resource), but other pods in the same Deployment without GPU requests could land on CPU nodes. For strict GPU targeting, always pair GPU resource requests with nodeSelector.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Node Labeling & Node Affinity Scheduling
  GPU Node Selector (simplest):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ spec:                                                      │
  │   nodeSelector:                                            │
  │     nvidia.com/gpu.present: "true"                         │
  │     nvidia.com/gpu.product: NVIDIA-H100-80GB-HBM3          │
  │   resources:                                               │
  │     limits:                                                │
  │       nvidia.com/gpu: 1                                    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  GPU Node Affinity (more flexible):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ spec:                                                      │
  │   affinity:                                                │
  │     nodeAffinity:                                          │
  │       requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution:      │
  │         nodeSelectorTerms:                                 │
  │         - matchExpressions:                                │
  │           - key: nvidia.com/gpu.product                    │
  │             operator: In                                   │
  │             values: ["NVIDIA-H100-80GB-HBM3"]              │
  │       preferredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution:     │
  │         - weight: 100                                      │
  │           preference:                                      │
  │             - key: nvidia.com/gpu.memory                   │
  │               operator: Gt                                 │
  │               values: ["40000"]  # Prefer 40GB+ GPUs      │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  GFD (GPU Feature Discovery) auto-labels nodes:
    nvidia.com/gpu.product = "NVIDIA-H100-80GB-HBM3"
    nvidia.com/gpu.memory = "81920" (MB)
    nvidia.com/gpu.count = "8"
    nvidia.com/cuda.driver = "550.54.15"

For anihpj/jobpost inference Deployment: nodeSelector targets H100 nodes, GPU resource request ensures exclusive GPU allocation. Without nodeSelector, the HPA might scale a non-GPU sidecar onto a CPU node.

Sched/MIG Q107 of 200
How do taints and tolerations reserve GPU nodes exclusively for GPU workloads?
A) Taints prevent ALL pods from scheduling on GPU nodes — even GPU pods are blocked
B) Taints are the same as nodeSelector — they both select nodes for pod scheduling
C) Taints only apply to CPU resources — GPU nodes cannot be tainted for GPU-specific scheduling
D) A taint like nvidia.com/gpu=present:NoSchedule repels all pods without a matching toleration — add the toleration only to GPU pods, ensuring non-GPU workloads never consume resources on expensive GPU nodes
✅ Correct Answer: D

Taints are the repellent mechanism — they push pods away. Tolerations are the exemption — they allow specific pods to ignore the taint. Taint GPU nodes with nvidia.com/gpu:NoSchedule, add matching toleration to GPU pods. Result: non-GPU pods (web servers, databases, monitoring) never schedule on expensive GPU nodes, preserving GPU capacity for AI workloads. Combine with nodeSelector for defense-in-depth: taint repels non-GPU pods, nodeSelector attracts GPU pods.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Taints & Tolerations
  # Taint GPU node
  kubectl taint node gpu-node-01 nvidia.com/gpu=true:NoSchedule

  # GPU Pod — has toleration to bypass taint
  tolerations:
  - key: nvidia.com/gpu
    operator: Equal
    value: "true"
    effect: NoSchedule

  # Non-GPU Pod — no toleration → cannot schedule on GPU node ✅

For anihpj/jobpost, all H100/A100 nodes are tainted with nvidia.com/gpu:NoSchedule. Only inference and training pods (with matching tolerations) run on GPU nodes. The Django web server, PostgreSQL, and Redis pods are repelled — they stay on cheaper CPU nodes.

Sched/MIG Q108 of 200
How does GPU time-slicing allow multiple pods to share one physical GPU in Kubernetes?
A) Time-slicing uses CUDA time-slice scheduling — the GPU driver interleaves CUDA contexts from different pods in time, and the device plugin advertises N replicas per GPU (e.g., 4 replicas), making Kubernetes see 4 allocatable GPUs instead of 1 physical GPU
B) Time-slicing physically divides GPU memory into equal partitions — each pod gets a dedicated memory region
C) Time-slicing uses Kubernetes pod priority to preempt GPU access — higher priority pods interrupt lower priority ones
D) Time-slicing creates virtual GPU devices via SR-IOV — hardware virtualization, not software interleaving
✅ Correct Answer: A

Time-slicing is a software-level multiplexing strategy. The GPU Operator configures the device plugin to advertise N replicas per physical GPU via a ConfigMap (e.g., replicas: 4). Kubernetes sees 4 allocatable GPUs and schedules 4 pods on one physical GPU. The NVIDIA driver time-slices between the 4 CUDA contexts — each pod gets GPU time in proportion to demand. Unlike MIG (hardware isolation), there's no memory or cache separation — pods compete for resources.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Time-Slicing — 4 Pods Sharing 1 GPU
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Time: ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────▶│
  │ Pod A: [████████░░░░░░░░████████░░░░░░░░████████]                │
  │ Pod B: [░░░░░░░░████████░░░░░░░░████████░░░░░░░░]                │
  │ Pod C: [░░░░████░░░░░░░░████░░░░░░░░████░░░░░░░░]                │
  │ Pod D: [████░░░░░░░░████░░░░░░░░████░░░░░░░░████░░]              │
  │ GPU:   [████████████████████████████████████████] ~100% utilized  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost dev environments, time-slicing with 4 replicas on L40S GPUs allows 4 developers to run PyTorch experiments simultaneously. Each gets ~25% GPU time on average — sufficient for prototyping, not for production inference.

Sched/MIG Q109 of 200
How do you configure GPU time-slicing through the GPU Operator ClusterPolicy?
A) Time-slicing is a kernel parameter — set nvidia.time_slicing=4 in /etc/modprobe.d/nvidia.conf
B) Time-slicing is enabled with a single CLI flag: nvidia-ctk time-slice --replicas 4
C) Create a time-slicing ConfigMap with replicas count, reference it in ClusterPolicy via devicePlugin.config.name, and deploy — the device plugin picks up the ConfigMap and advertises multiplied GPU resources
D) Time-slicing is set per-pod via an annotation: gpu.nvidia.com/time-slice: enabled
✅ Correct Answer: C

Time-slicing is configured declaratively. (1) Create a ConfigMap named time-slicing-config specifying replicas: 4 for nvidia.com/gpu, (2) Reference it in ClusterPolicy under devicePlugin.config.name: time-slicing-config, (3) The device plugin reads the ConfigMap and advertises 4× the physical GPU count. To change replicas, update the ConfigMap and restart the device plugin DaemonSet.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Time-Slicing ConfigMap + ClusterPolicy
  # ConfigMap
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: ConfigMap
  metadata: {name: time-slicing-config}
  data:
    any: |-
      sharing:
        timeSlicing:
          resources:
          - name: nvidia.com/gpu
            replicas: 4  # 1 GPU → 4 allocatable

  # ClusterPolicy reference
  devicePlugin:
    config:
      name: time-slicing-config

For anihpj/jobpost, time-slicing is enabled on L40S nodes with 2 replicas (conservative sharing). The ConfigMap is versioned in Git and applied via ArgoCD — changing replicas is a Git commit, not a manual node operation.

For anihpj/jobpost, time-slicing is enabled on L40S nodes with 2 replicas (conservative sharing). The ConfigMap is versioned in Git and applied via ArgoCD — changing replicas is a Git commit, not a manual node operation.

Sched/MIG Q110 of 200
How do exclusive, time-slicing, MIG, and MPS GPU allocation strategies compare for Kubernetes?
A) Exclusive (1 pod/GPU, highest performance), time-slicing (N pods/GPU, low overhead), MIG (up to 7 pods/GPU, hardware isolation), MPS (multi-process on 1 GPU, lowest latency) — each with different isolation, overhead, and performance tradeoffs
B) All strategies provide identical performance — the choice is purely about configuration syntax preference
C) Only exclusive allocation is supported in Kubernetes — all other strategies require Slurm or custom schedulers
D) MIG and time-slicing are the same strategy implemented differently — they produce identical results
✅ Correct Answer: A

GPU allocation strategies form a spectrum: Exclusive = 1 pod per GPU, no sharing, best performance, simplest; Time-Slicing = N pods share via time multiplexing, no isolation, lowest overhead for sharing; MIG = up to 7 hardware-isolated slices per GPU, predictable performance, complex config; MPS = multi-process within single GPU context, lowest latency for small jobs, single-user only. Choose based on isolation requirements and workload patterns.

GPU Allocation Strategy Comparison
  ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐
  │ Strategy │ Pods/GPU │ Isolation│ Overhead │ Best For │
  ├──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┤
  │ Exclusive│ 1        │ N/A      │ 0%       │ Production│
  │ Time-Slice│ 2-8     │ None     │ ~5%      │ Dev/CI   │
  │ MIG      │ 1-7      │ Hardware │ ~0%      │ Multi-tenant│
  │ MPS      │ 1-48     │ None     │ ~0%      │ Single-user│
  └──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: exclusive on H100 (inference), MIG on A100 (multi-tenant dev), time-slicing on L40S (CI/CD). Three strategies, one cluster, managed by GPU Operator with node-group targeting.

Sched/MIG Q111 of 200
What is MPS (Multi-Process Service) and when is it preferred over MIG?
A) MPS provides hardware isolation like MIG but with lower overhead — it's always preferred
B) MPS is the legacy name for MIG — they are the same technology renamed for Hopper GPUs
C) MPS partitions GPU memory like MIG but uses software instead of hardware — provides memory isolation without GPU reset
D) MPS allows multiple CUDA processes from the SAME user to share a GPU with reduced context-switching overhead — ideal for single-user multi-process workloads. Unlike MIG, MPS provides NO isolation between processes and shares one GPU context
✅ Correct Answer: D

MPS is a CUDA feature that allows multiple processes to share GPU resources through a single CUDA context — eliminating the overhead of context switching between independent CUDA contexts. Best for: a single user running multiple small GPU jobs (hyperparameter sweeps, inference batching). NOT suitable for multi-tenant: all processes share one address space, one crash affects all. MIG uses hardware isolation; MPS uses context sharing — fundamentally different approaches.

MPS Architecture — Context Sharing vs Isolation
  WITHOUT MPS (4 independent processes):
  ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐
  │ Proc A │ │ Proc B │ │ Proc C │ │ Proc D │
  │ Ctx A  │ │ Ctx B  │ │ Ctx C  │ │ Ctx D  │
  └───┬────┘ └───┬────┘ └───┬────┘ └───┬────┘
      │ CUDA    │ CUDA    │ CUDA    │ CUDA     ← 4 independent contexts
      ▼         ▼         ▼         ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           GPU (H100)                     │
  │  Context switch overhead: ~20µs/switch   │
  │  4 concurrent kernels max (time-sliced)  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────┘

  WITH MPS (Multi-Process Service):
  ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐
  │ Proc A │ │ Proc B │ │ Proc C │ │ Proc D │
  └───┬────┘ └───┬────┘ └───┬────┘ └───┬────┘
      │         │         │         │
      └─────────┼─────────┼─────────┘
                ▼         ▼
        ┌──────────────────────┐
        │    MPS Server        │
        │  (Single CUDA Context)│ ← ONE context, no switching!
        │  Merges all kernels  │
        └──────────┬───────────┘
                   ▼
        ┌──────────────────────┐
        │    GPU (H100)         │
        │  All 4 run concurrently│
        │  Context switch: ~0µs │
        └──────────────────────┘

  MPS vs MIG Comparison:
  ┌──────────┬────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │ Feature  │ MPS                │ MIG                  │
  ├──────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Isolation│ NONE (shared ctx)  │ Hardware-enforced    │
  │ Overhead │ Very low           │ Slight (partition)   │
  │ Multi-   │ Same user only     │ Different users OK   │
  │ tenant   │                    │                      │
  │ Use Case │ 1 user, many small │ Multi-tenant GPU     │
  │          │ parallel jobs      │ sharing              │
  │ Crash    │ All jobs crash!    │ Only 1 instance      │
  └──────────┴────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, MPS is used for hyperparameter optimization runs — one ML engineer launches 8 parallel training configurations sharing 2 GPUs via MPS. The MPS server merges all kernels into one CUDA context, eliminating context-switch overhead. However, since all jobs share one address space, a CUDA OOM in one kills all 8 — MPS is only used for non-critical exploratory tuning, never for production training.

Sched/MIG Q112 of 200
Why does GPU topology-aware scheduling matter for multi-GPU workloads?
A) GPU topology doesn't affect performance — any GPU can communicate with any other at full speed regardless of topology
B) GPUs on different NUMA nodes or PCIe switches communicate through the CPU interconnect (UPI/QPI), not directly via NVLink — cross-NUMA GPU pods suffer 30-50% NCCL bandwidth loss. Kubernetes Topology Manager with single-numa-node policy ensures pods use GPUs on the same NUMA node
C) Topology-aware scheduling only matters for CPU cores — GPU memory is NUMA-independent by design
D) NVLink makes topology irrelevant — all GPUs in an NVLink domain communicate at the same speed regardless of NUMA placement
✅ Correct Answer: B

In dual-socket GPU servers, GPUs 0-3 connect to CPU socket 0 (NUMA node 0), GPUs 4-7 connect to CPU socket 1 (NUMA node 1). A pod on NUMA 0 using GPU 7 (NUMA 1) forces all GPU data through the UPI interconnect — adding latency and halving effective bandwidth. Topology Manager with single-numa-node policy aligns pod CPU, memory, and GPU on the same NUMA node.

NUMA-Aware GPU Scheduling — Why Topology Matters
  Dual-Socket DGX Server (2× CPU, 8× GPU):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NUMA Node 0 (Socket 0)       │  NUMA Node 1 (Socket 1)    │
  │  ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐  │  ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐│
  │  │GPU 0 │ │GPU 1 │ │GPU 2 │  │  │GPU 4 │ │GPU 5 │ │GPU 6 ││
  │  │ NVLinked ──── NVLinked │  │  │ NVLinked ──── NVLinked ││
  │  └──┬───┘ └──┬───┘ └──┬───┘  │  └──┬───┘ └──┬───┘ └──┬───┘│
  │     │ PCIe   │       │       │     │ PCIe   │       │       │
  │  ┌──┴───────┴───────┴──┐    │  ┌──┴───────┴───────┴──┐    │
  │  │   CPU 0 (56 cores)  │    │  │   CPU 1 (56 cores)  │    │
  │  │   Local RAM: 1TB    │    │  │   Local RAM: 1TB    │    │
  │  └──────────┬──────────┘    │  └──────────┬──────────┘    │
  └─────────────┼───────────────┴─────────────┼───────────────┘
                │    UPI/QPI (slow!)         │
                └────────────────────────────┘

  Performance Impact:
  ┌────────────────────────────┬────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │ Configuration              │ NCCL BW    │ Training Speed   │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Pod on NUMA0 + GPU 0-3     │ 900 GB/s   │ 100% (baseline)  │
  │ Pod on NUMA0 + GPU 4-7     │ 450 GB/s   │ 65% (cross-NUMA!) │
  │ Topology Mgr single-numa   │ 900 GB/s   │ 100% (aligned!)  │
  └────────────────────────────┴────────────┴──────────────────┘

  Fix: Kubernetes Topology Manager with 'single-numa-node' policy
       Ensures pod CPU + Memory + GPU all on the same NUMA node

For anihpj/jobpost distributed training, Topology Manager single-numa-node ensures all 8 GPU processes in a training job align with their GPU's NUMA node — maximizing NCCL bandwidth. Without it, a pod on NUMA 0 using GPU 7 forces all data through the slow UPI interconnect, cutting training speed by 35%.

Sched/MIG Q113 of 200
What is the GPU bin packing problem and how does it affect cluster efficiency?
A) Bin packing is a GPU memory optimization that compresses model weights to fit more models on one GPU
B) Bin packing refers to physically stacking GPUs in a server chassis — maximizing GPU density per rack unit
C) GPU bin packing is the scheduling challenge of fitting pods with different GPU requirements onto nodes efficiently — 4 pods each needing 1 MIG slice may block a pod needing a full GPU, leaving GPUs underutilized. MIG mixed strategies and GPU-aware bin packing algorithms address this
D) GPU bin packing is solved automatically by the Kubernetes scheduler — no special configuration needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU bin packing is the GPU utilization optimization problem. Example: an A100 node with 7 MIG 1g.5gb slices has 5 slices occupied by dev pods. A training pod needs a full GPU (7g.80gb) but can't schedule because 5 slices block the GPU — 71% of GPU capacity sits idle. Solutions: MIG mixed strategy (reserve some GPUs as full, others as MIG), GPU-aware descheduling (evict small pods to consolidate), separate node pools for different GPU allocation strategies.

GPU Bin Packing — The Fragmentation Problem
  Scenario: A100 Node with 8 GPUs (7 MIG 1g.5gb slices each = 56 total slices)

  BAD: Fragmented GPU Allocation
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ GPU0: [Dev-A][Dev-B][Dev-C][Free][Free][Free][Free]       │
  │ GPU1: [Dev-D][Free][Free][Free][Free][Free][Free]         │
  │ GPU2: [CI-1][CI-2][Free][Free][Free][Free][Free]          │
  │ GPU3: [Free][Free][Free][Free][Free][Free][Free]  ← FULL  │
  │ GPU4: [Dev-E][Free][Free][Free][Free][Free][Free]         │
  │ GPU5: [Dev-F][Dev-G][Free][Free][Free][Free][Free]        │
  │ GPU6: [Test][Test][Test][Free][Free][Free][Free]          │
  │ GPU7: [Free][Free][Free][Free][Free][Free][Free]  ← FULL  │
  │                                                            │
  │ Training pod needs 1 FULL GPU (7g.80gb) → CANNOT SCHEDULE! │
  │ 41/56 slices FREE (73%), but no single GPU is fully free!  │
  │ → Dev pods scattered across GPUs block full-GPU training   │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  GOOD: MIG Mixed Strategy (Prevents Fragmentation)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ GPU0-1: FULL GPU (reserved for training)                   │
  │ GPU2-3: FULL GPU (reserved for training)                   │
  │ GPU4-5: MIG (7× 1g.5gb each) → All dev/test pods here     │
  │ GPU6-7: MIG (7× 1g.5gb each) → All CI/CD pods here        │
  │                                                            │
  │ Training pod needs 1 FULL GPU → Scheduled on GPU0-3 ✅     │
  │ Dev pod needs 1 MIG slice → Scheduled on GPU4-7 ✅         │
  │ No contention between full-GPU and MIG workloads!          │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Solutions Summary:
  ┌─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Strategy                │ How It Works                     │
  ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ MIG Mixed Strategy      │ Some GPUs full, some MIG-sliced  │
  │ GPU Descheduler         │ Evict small pods to consolidate  │
  │ Separate Node Pools     │ training-pool vs inference-pool  │
  │ GPU-Aware Bin Packing   │ Scheduler prefers filling partial│
  │                         │ GPUs before starting new ones    │
  └─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, A100 nodes use MIG mixed strategy: GPUs 0-3 = full (for BERT training CI/CD), GPUs 4-7 = MIG 1g.5gb × 7 each (28 dev slices). This prevents the bin packing problem — full-GPU training jobs and MIG dev pods never compete. The GPU descheduler runs nightly to consolidate scattered MIG slices and free whole GPUs for morning training runs.

Sched/MIG Q114 of 200
Your GPU pod is stuck in Pending with "insufficient nvidia.com/gpu". How do you debug this?
A) kubectl describe pod → check Events for scheduling failures; kubectl describe nodes → check nvidia.com/gpu capacity and allocatable; kubectl get pods -A -o wide → identify which pods are using GPUs; check if GPU nodes are cordoned or have taints blocking scheduling
B) The GPU is faulty — replace the GPU hardware and retry scheduling
C) Restart kubelet on all GPU nodes — the GPU count needs to be refreshed after driver updates
D) Delete all GPU pods and recreate them — the scheduler cannot rebalance GPU allocations while pods are running
✅ Correct Answer: A

Debugging "insufficient nvidia.com/gpu" follows systematic investigation: (1) kubectl describe pod — Events show which nodes were considered and why GPU count was insufficient, (2) kubectl describe node <gpunode> | grep nvidia — shows capacity vs. allocatable vs. used, (3) kubectl get pods -A --field-selector spec.nodeName=<node> -o json | jq '.items[].spec.containers[].resources' — find which pods consume GPUs, (4) check node conditions (Ready? Cordoned?) and taints.

GPU Scheduling Debug — Systematic Investigation
  Step 1: Check Pod Events
    kubectl describe pod bert-trainer-xyz | grep -A10 Events
    → "0/4 nodes are available: 3 node(s) didn't match Pod's node affinity,
       1 Insufficient nvidia.com/gpu."

  Step 2: Check GPU Node Capacity
    kubectl describe node dgx-1 | grep -A5 "nvidia.com/gpu"
    → nvidia.com/gpu:  8  (Capacity)
    → nvidia.com/gpu:  0  (Allocatable — ALL 8 GPUs in use!)

  Step 3: Find GPU Consumers
    kubectl get pods -A --field-selector spec.nodeName=dgx-1 \
        -o json | jq '.items[] | {name: .metadata.name, gpu: .spec.containers[].resources.requests."nvidia.com/gpu"}'
    → 8 inference pods consuming 1 GPU each

  Step 4: Check Taints/Tolerations
    kubectl describe node dgx-1 | grep Taints
    → nvidia.com/gpu:NoSchedule (GPU node dedicated to inference)

  Decision Tree:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ GPU Pod Pending?                                           │
  │ ├─ Node has GPUs? → No → Add GPU node or check labels     │
  │ ├─ GPUs available? → No → Who's using them? Scale/release  │
  │ ├─ Node tainted? → Yes → Add toleration to pod             │
  │ └─ MIG profile mismatch? → Check node MIG labels vs request │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost ops, this is the #1 scheduling issue: all 8 H100 GPUs are allocated to inference pods, and a new training pod can't schedule. The fix is either scaling down inference (lower HPA min) or adding a GPU node to the cluster.

Sched/MIG Q115 of 200
How do you check GPU resource availability on Kubernetes nodes?
A) Run nvidia-smi on each node via SSH — kubectl cannot show GPU resource information
B) kubectl describe node | grep -A5 nvidia shows Capacity (total GPUs) and Allocatable (GPUs available for pods). kubectl get nodes -o json | jq '.items[].status.allocatable["nvidia.com/gpu"]' shows per-node GPU count. kubectl top is not available for GPUs — use DCGM
C) kubectl top node shows GPU utilization alongside CPU and memory utilization
D) GPU resources are invisible to kubectl — only the device plugin can query GPU allocation state
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU resources appear in node status as extended resources. kubectl describe node | grep -A3 nvidia.com/gpu shows capacity and allocatable counts. Note: kubectl top does NOT show GPU utilization — it only tracks CPU and memory. For GPU utilization, use DCGM metrics via Prometheus or kubectl exec <dcgm-pod> -- dcgmi dmon. The allocatable count decreases as pods consume GPUs — monitoring this helps predict when GPU capacity will be exhausted.

GPU Node Resource Reporting — kubectl describe node
  kubectl describe node gpu-node-01 | grep -A8 nvidia.com
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Capacity:                                                │
  │   cpu:                  112                              │
  │   memory:               2115472Ki                        │
  │   nvidia.com/gpu:       8         ← Physical GPUs       │
  │ Allocatable:                                             │
  │   cpu:                  110                              │
  │   memory:               2013472Ki                        │
  │   nvidia.com/gpu:       3         ← GPUs free for pods  │
  │ Allocated resources:                                     │
  │   nvidia.com/gpu:       5         ← 5 GPUs in use       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Quick JSON query:
  kubectl get node gpu-node-01 -o json | jq '.status.allocatable."nvidia.com/gpu"'
  → "3"  (3 GPUs available)

  kubectl top does NOT show GPU utilization!
  → Use DCGM metrics: DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL via Prometheus/Grafana

For anihpj/jobpost monitoring, a Prometheus alert fires when allocatable GPUs drop below 2 (85% capacity). This triggers either scale-down of non-critical GPU pods or a notification to add GPU nodes before capacity is exhausted.

Sched/MIG Q116 of 200
How does nvidia.com/gpu work as a Kubernetes extended resource?
A) Extended resources are compiled into kubelet — NVIDIA contributed nvidia.com/gpu directly to the Kubernetes source code
B) Extended resources are defined in CustomResourceDefinitions — nvidia.com/gpu is a CRD specifying GPU count per node
C) Extended resources are advertised by device plugins via the Device Plugin API — kubelet discovers them dynamically and adds them to node capacity. nvidia.com/gpu follows the Kubernetes resource model (requests/limits) and is OPAQUE (kubelet doesn't understand GPU topology, just counts)
D) nvidia.com/gpu is a standard Kubernetes resource like cpu and memory — it's always present in node capacity regardless of device plugin
✅ Correct Answer: C

Extended resources are dynamically registered — Kubernetes doesn't know about GPUs natively. The device plugin (DaemonSet) registers with kubelet via Unix socket, advertises the resource name and count, and kubelet propagates this to the API server as node capacity. Extended resources are opaque — kubelet doesn't understand what a GPU is, it just tracks the integer count and calls the device plugin's Allocate() when a pod requests the resource. This design allows any hardware vendor to integrate without modifying Kubernetes core.

Kubernetes Extended Resources — Device Plugin Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  How nvidia.com/gpu Becomes a Schedulable Resource          │
  │                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────┐     Unix Socket      ┌──────────────────┐│
  │  │  kubelet     │◀────Register()───────│ NVIDIA Device    ││
  │  │              │     /var/lib/kubelet/│ Plugin (DaemonSet)││
  │  │  ┌─────────┐ │     device-plugins/  │                  ││
  │  │  │Resource │ │                      │ ListAndWatch()   ││
  │  │  │ Manager │ │───Allocate(GPU:0)──▶│ → Returns GPU    ││
  │  │  └────┬────┘ │                      │   device paths   ││
  │  └───────┼──────┘                      └──────────────────┘│
  │          │ Update Node Status                                │
  │  ┌───────▼──────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Kubernetes API Server                                │   │
  │  │  Node: dgx-1                                          │   │
  │  │    capacity:                                          │   │
  │  │      cpu: "112"                                       │   │
  │  │      memory: "2Ti"                                    │   │
  │  │      nvidia.com/gpu: "8"    ← Extended (opaque!)      │   │
  │  │    allocatable:                                       │   │
  │  │      nvidia.com/gpu: "8"                              │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                             │
  │  Built-in resources: kubelet understands CPU/memory topology │
  │  Extended resources: kubelet only tracks INTEGER count      │
  │  → Device plugin translates count into GPU device files      │
  │  → Vendor-neutral design: any hardware can integrate!       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, this means NVIDIA updates the device plugin independently of Kubernetes versions — the H100 device plugin works on K8s 1.27-1.31 without any kubelet changes. The extended resource API is stable and vendor-neutral — the same pattern supports AMD GPUs (amd.com/gpu) and Intel FPGAs (intel.com/fpga).

3.3 Slurm Workload Manager for AIQ117-Q126

Sched/MIG Q117 of 200
What is Slurm and what are its core components for managing HPC GPU clusters?
A) Slurm is a Kubernetes distribution optimized for HPC — it replaces kubelet and kube-scheduler
B) Slurm is a monitoring tool like Prometheus — it collects GPU metrics from DGX nodes
C) Slurm is an open-source HPC workload manager with slurmctld (central controller), slurmd (node daemon), and slurmdbd (accounting database) — it schedules batch jobs, manages GPU allocations via GRES, and enforces fair-share policies across users and groups
D) Slurm is NVIDIA's proprietary job scheduler that ships exclusively with DGX systems — it cannot be used on non-DGX hardware
✅ Correct Answer: C

Slurm is the dominant HPC workload manager. Core components: slurmctld (central controller — one per cluster, handles job queue and scheduling), slurmd (one per compute node, executes jobs and reports status), slurmdbd (accounting database — stores job history for fair-share). GPU management: Slurm's GRES (Generic Resource) plugin tracks GPUs as schedulable resources — --gres=gpu:8 requests 8 GPUs. Slurm excels at batch scheduling with queue priorities, job arrays, and gang scheduling.

Slurm Architecture
  ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐
  │ slurmctld    │◀──▶│ slurmdbd     │    │ User (sbatch)│
  │ (controller) │    │ (accounting) │    │              │
  └──────┬───────┘    └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘
         │ manages
    ┌────┴────┬────────────┬────────────┐
    ▼         ▼            ▼            ▼
  slurmd    slurmd       slurmd       slurmd  (GPU nodes)

For anihpj/jobpost, Slurm manages the weekly BERT fine-tuning jobs on the DGX cluster. The ML team submits sbatch scripts specifying GPU count and walltime. Slurm queues, prioritizes, and gang-schedules the training jobs across available GPUs.

Sched/MIG Q118 of 200
How do you submit a GPU job to Slurm with specific GPU requirements?
A) kubectl apply -f gpu-job.yaml — Slurm uses Kubernetes YAML for job submission
B) docker run --gpus all — Slurm submits jobs as Docker containers directly to GPU nodes
C) srun --gpus=8 python train.py — srun is the only way to request GPUs in Slurm
D) sbatch with #SBATCH directives: --gres=gpu:8 (request 8 GPUs), --cpus-per-gpu=8 (CPU cores per GPU), --mem=500G (memory), --time=24:00:00 (walltime) — Slurm allocates resources, then runs the script on allocated nodes
✅ Correct Answer: D

sbatch is the primary batch job submission command. #SBATCH directives specify resources: --gres=gpu:a100:8 (8 A100 GPUs), --cpus-per-gpu=8 (8 CPU cores per GPU for data loading), --ntasks=8 (one task per GPU), --nodes=2 (spread across 2 nodes). Slurm allocates matching resources, sets environment variables (SLURM_GPUS_ON_NODE, CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES), and runs the script.

Slurm GPU Job Submission
  #!/bin/bash
  #SBATCH --job-name=bert-training
  #SBATCH --gres=gpu:a100:8     # 8 A100 GPUs
  #SBATCH --cpus-per-gpu=8      # 64 CPU cores total
  #SBATCH --nodes=2              # Across 2 DGX nodes
  #SBATCH --time=04:00:00        # 4 hour walltime

  srun python train_bert.py --batch-size 256

For anihpj/jobpost weekly training, this sbatch script requests 8 A100 GPUs for 4 hours. Slurm queues it with fair-share priority, allocates 2 DGX nodes, and the training script uses NCCL for multi-node GPU communication automatically.

Sched/MIG Q119 of 200
What is the difference between sbatch, srun, and salloc in Slurm?
A) sbatch submits a script for batch execution (queued, runs when resources available); srun runs a command interactively within an existing allocation; salloc creates an interactive allocation (shell on allocated nodes) for debugging
B) All three are identical — they submit the same type of job with different syntax
C) sbatch is for CPU jobs, srun is for GPU jobs, salloc is for memory allocation — they handle different resource types
D) sbatch and srun are deprecated — only salloc is supported in Slurm 23.02+
✅ Correct Answer: A

The three Slurm job commands serve different purposes: sbatch = "submit a batch script" — the primary production command, jobs are queued and run when resources become available; salloc = "allocate resources interactively" — gives you a shell on allocated nodes for debugging; srun = "run a command within an allocation" — used inside sbatch scripts or salloc sessions to launch parallel tasks. For GPU debugging: salloc --gres=gpu:1 gives you an interactive GPU node for testing.

Slurm Job Commands — When to Use Each
  ┌──────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Command  │ Purpose & Behavior                                   │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ sbatch   │ Submit batch script → QUEUED → runs when resources   │
  │          │ available. Output goes to slurm-.out file.    │
  │          │ Use for: Production training, nightly jobs, CI/CD    │
  │          │ Example: sbatch --gres=gpu:8 train_bert.sh           │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ salloc   │ Allocate resources → INTERACTIVE SHELL on GPU node.  │
  │          │ You get a terminal on the allocated node immediately. │
  │          │ Use for: Debugging, testing code, inspecting GPU     │
  │          │ Example: salloc --gres=gpu:1 --time=01:00:00         │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ srun     │ Run command WITHIN an existing allocation.           │
  │          │ Used inside sbatch scripts or salloc sessions.       │
  │          │ Handles parallel task launch (one per GPU).           │
  │          │ Example: srun --ntasks=8 python train.py             │
  └──────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Typical ML Engineer Workflow:
    $ salloc --gres=gpu:1 -t 01:00:00      # Get interactive GPU node
    $ srun nvidia-smi                        # Verify GPU visible
    $ srun python debug_model.py             # Test code on GPU
    $ exit                                   # Release allocation
    $ sbatch train_bert.sh                   # Submit production job

For anihpj/jobpost ML engineers: sbatch for production BERT training (queued, fair-share priority), salloc --gres=gpu:1 for interactive GPU debugging sessions, and srun inside sbatch scripts to launch one training process per GPU with proper NCCL environment setup.

Sched/MIG Q120 of 200
How do you request specific GPU models in Slurm using GRES?
A) All GPUs are treated identically in Slurm — you cannot request specific GPU models
B) Use --gres=gpu:a100:2 to request 2 A100 GPUs, or --gres=gpu:h100:4 for 4 H100 GPUs — Slurm's GRES plugin supports GPU type labels configured by the administrator in slurm.conf
C) GPU model selection is done via CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES — Slurm doesn't distinguish GPU types
D) Use --constraint="h100" — GPU type is a node feature, not a GRES resource
✅ Correct Answer: B

Slurm GRES supports typed GPU resources. In slurm.conf, GPUs are configured with type labels: GresTypes=gpu and nodes defined with NodeName=gpu[01-08] Gres=gpu:a100:8. Users request specific types: --gres=gpu:a100:2 or --gres=gpu:h100:4. Multiple GPU types can coexist in one cluster — Slurm tracks each type separately and schedules accordingly. This enables heterogeneous GPU clusters: A100 for training, H100 for inference, each schedulable independently.

Slurm GPU Type Requests — Heterogeneous GPU Management
  slurm.conf — Define GPU types per node:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ GresTypes=gpu                                                │
  │ NodeName=dgx[01-04] Gres=gpu:a100:8  # 8× A100 per DGX     │
  │ NodeName=dgx[05-08] Gres=gpu:h100:8  # 8× H100 per DGX     │
  │ NodeName=gpu[09-12] Gres=gpu:l40s:4  # 4× L40S per node    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Job submission with GPU type targeting:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ sbatch --gres=gpu:a100:2  train_bert.sh                     │
  │ → Slurm schedules ONLY on dgx01-dgx04 (A100 nodes)          │
  │                                                              │
  │ sbatch --gres=gpu:h100:4  train_llm.sh                      │
  │ → Slurm schedules ONLY on dgx05-dgx08 (H100 nodes)          │
  │                                                              │
  │ sbatch --gres=gpu:1        inference.sh                     │
  │ → Any GPU type (Slurm picks based on availability)          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  This enables cost-aware scheduling: training on cheaper A100s,
  latency-critical inference on faster H100s.

For anihpj/jobpost, A100 DGX nodes are configured as GRES type a100, H100 nodes as type h100. Training jobs request a100, inference jobs request h100 — Slurm schedules each to the appropriate hardware pool.

Sched/MIG Q121 of 200
How do you monitor Slurm jobs and GPU resource usage?
A) kubectl top pod — Slurm resources are exposed through the Kubernetes metrics API
B) Only the Slurm GUI dashboard shows job status — there are no CLI commands for monitoring
C) squeue shows queued/running jobs with GPU allocation; sinfo shows partition/node status with GPU availability; sacct shows completed job history with GPU usage; scontrol show job <id> shows detailed job info including GPU GRES allocation
D) nvidia-smi is the only way to monitor GPU usage — Slurm doesn't track GPU utilization
✅ Correct Answer: C

Slurm provides comprehensive CLI monitoring: squeue --format="%.18i %.9P %.8j %.8u %.2t %.10M %.6D %R" shows job state and reason, sinfo -o "%n %P %.11T %.14C %G" shows nodes with GPU availability, sacct -j <jobid> --format=JobID,AllocGPUS,Elapsed,MaxRSS shows historical GPU usage, scontrol show job <jobid> shows the exact GRES allocation (which specific GPUs were assigned).

Slurm GPU Monitoring — squeue & sinfo
  squeue — GPU Job Queue (like "kubectl get pods")
  ┌──────────┬──────────┬─────────┬──────────┬────────┬─────────────┐
  │ JOBID    │ PARTITION│ NAME    │ USER     │ STATE  │ NODELIST    │
  ├──────────┼──────────┼─────────┼──────────┼────────┼─────────────┤
  │ 45678    │ a100-trn │ bert-hp │ alice    │ RUNNING│ gpu[01-04]  │
  │ 45679    │ a100-trn │ llm-sft │ bob      │ RUNNING│ gpu[05-08]  │
  │ 45680    │ a100-trn │ bert-v2 │ carol    │ PENDING│ (Resources) │
  └──────────┴──────────┴─────────┴──────────┴────────┴─────────────┘

  sinfo — GPU Node Status (like "kubectl get nodes")
  ┌──────────┬──────────┬────────┬────────┬──────────┬──────────────┐
  │ PARTITION│ AVAIL    │ TIMELIMIT│ NODES │ STATE    │ NODELIST     │
  ├──────────┼──────────┼────────┼────────┼──────────┼──────────────┤
  │ a100-trn*│ up       │ 3-00:00│    4   │ allocated│ gpu[01-04]   │
  │ a100-trn*│ up       │ 3-00:00│    4   │ allocated│ gpu[05-08]   │
  │ h100-llm │ up       │ 7-00:00│    4   │ idle     │ gpu[09-12]   │
  └──────────┴──────────┴────────┴────────┴──────────┴──────────────┘

  Key Insight: squeue tells you WHO, sinfo tells you WHAT's available.
              Together they give complete GPU visibility across the cluster.

For anihpj/jobpost ops, squeue and sinfo provide the GPU equivalent of "kubectl get pods -o wide" — at-a-glance GPU fleet utilization. sacct feeds the monthly GPU cost allocation report.

Sched/MIG Q122 of 200
A Slurm GPU job is stuck in PENDING state with Reason=Resources. How do you diagnose why?
A) The job will eventually run — PENDING always resolves on its own, no investigation needed
B) Delete and resubmit the job — the scheduler sometimes misses job queue updates
C) Increase the job priority — PENDING always means the job priority is too low to compete with other jobs
D) scontrol show job <id> shows detailed reason (Resources, Priority, PartitionNodeLimit, AssocGrp*Limit); sinfo shows GPU availability; check QoS limits (MaxTRESPerUser); check if partition has enough GPUs of the requested type
✅ Correct Answer: D

PENDING diagnosis uses scontrol show job which reveals the exact reason: Resources = not enough GPUs available (other jobs using them), Priority = higher-priority jobs ahead in queue, PartitionNodeLimit = partition configured with max nodes per job exceeded, AssocGrpGPUMinutesLimit = user/group GPU quota exhausted. Check GPU availability with sinfo -p <partition> -o "%G" and running jobs with squeue --partition=<name>.

Slurm PENDING Diagnosis — Systematic Debugging
  scontrol show job 45678
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ JobState=PENDING Reason=Resources Dependency=(null)          │
  │ Priority=4281  Nice=0  Account=ml-engineering                │
  │ ReqTRES=cpu=64,mem=500G,node=2,gres/gpu:a100=8              │
  │                                                              │
  │ Possible PENDING Reasons & Fixes:                            │
  ├──────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Resources    │ Not enough GPUs free → Wait or use other type │
  │ Priority     │ Higher-priority jobs ahead → Wait, or improve │
  │ Partition    │ Partition node limit exceeded → Use other part│
  │ AssocGrpLimit│ Group GPU-minutes quota exhausted → Wait/talk │
  │ QOSMaxLimit  │ Per-user GPU limit hit → Reduce GPU request   │
  │ Dependency   │ Waiting for job 45677 to complete → Check     │
  └──────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Quick check: sinfo -p a100-trn -o "%n %G %t"
  → gpu01 gpu:a100:4 allocated   (0 free!)
  → gpu02 gpu:a100:4 allocated   (0 free!)
  → All A100 GPUs in use → job PENDING until one finishes ✅

For anihpj/jobpost, this happens when all A100 GPUs are busy with training jobs. The fix: wait for a job to finish, or use H100 GPUs instead (different GRES type, may be available).

3.3 Slurm & Workload Management (continued)Q123-Q126

Sched/MIG Q123 of 200
What are Slurm partitions, and how do they organize GPU resources in a heterogeneous AI cluster?
A) Partitions are disk partitions on Slurm nodes — they divide storage, not compute resources
B) Partitions are user groups that control who can submit GPU jobs — they are purely an access control mechanism
C) Partitions are logical groupings of nodes (similar to Kubernetes node pools) that organize GPU resources by type, priority, or purpose. A cluster might have partitions like "a100" (A100 GPUs for training), "h100" (H100 GPUs for large models), and "inference" (L40S GPUs for serving). Each partition has its own QoS limits, job priority, and allowed user groups — enabling multi-tenant GPU cluster management
D) Partitions are NVIDIA GPU memory partitions created by MIG — they slice GPU HBM into smaller chunks
✅ Correct Answer: C

Slurm partitions are the primary mechanism for organizing GPU resources. A partition defines: (1) Node list — which nodes belong to this partition; (2) Default/max time — job time limits; (3) QoS — resource limits per user/group/account; (4) Priority — job priority weight (training gets higher priority than inference); (5) Allowed accounts — which user groups can submit. This enables separating ML Research (access to all GPUs) from Production Inference (access to dedicated L40S nodes) from Batch Processing (lowest priority, preemptible).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Slurm Partitions — Multi-Tenant GPU Cluster
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Slurm GPU Cluster (32 A100, 16 H100, 24 L40S)              │
  │                                                             │
  │  Partition: a100-training (Priority: 100)                   │
  │  ├─ Nodes: gpu[01-08] (32× A100 80GB)                      │
  │  ├─ MaxTime: 72 hours                                       │
  │  ├─ AllowedAccounts: ml-research, ml-engineering            │
  │  └─ Jobs: BERT training, hyperparameter tuning              │
  │                                                             │
  │  Partition: h100-llm (Priority: 200)                        │
  │  ├─ Nodes: gpu[09-12] (16× H100 80GB)                      │
  │  ├─ MaxTime: 168 hours (7 days!)                            │
  │  ├─ AllowedAccounts: llm-team                               │
  │  └─ Jobs: LLM pretraining, large model experiments          │
  │                                                             │
  │  Partition: inference (Priority: 50)                        │
  │  ├─ Nodes: gpu[13-18] (24× L40S 48GB)                      │
  │  ├─ MaxTime: INFINITE                                       │
  │  ├─ AllowedAccounts: ml-inference                           │
  │  └─ Jobs: Model serving (persistent, never stops)           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, Slurm partitions separate BERT training (a100-training partition, 32 GPUs, max 72h) from model serving (inference partition, 24 L40S GPUs, persistent). This prevents training jobs from starving inference and vice versa — similar to how Kubernetes namespaces with ResourceQuota separate workloads.

Sched/MIG Q124 of 200
How can Slurm and Kubernetes coexist in an AI infrastructure, and what are common integration patterns?
A) Slurm and Kubernetes cannot coexist — you must choose one or the other for the entire GPU cluster
B) Kubernetes replaces Slurm entirely when using the NVIDIA GPU Operator — Slurm is deprecated for GPU workloads
C) Slurm runs INSIDE Kubernetes as a pod — it's deployed as a containerized workload on the K8s cluster
D) Integration patterns include: (1) Slurm for batch AI training + K8s for inference serving on separate GPU partitions; (2) NVIDIA BCM bridges Slurm and K8s — unified job submission across both; (3) Slurm Kubernetes Operator (SUNK) runs Slurm-managed pods on K8s; (4) K8s CronJob triggers Slurm jobs via sbatch for periodic training. The key insight: Slurm excels at large-scale HPC-style batch jobs, K8s excels at microservice orchestration — use each where it's strongest
✅ Correct Answer: D

Slurm + Kubernetes coexistence is the dominant pattern in production AI infrastructure. Slurm is purpose-built for HPC batch scheduling — it excels at: gang scheduling (all GPUs start simultaneously for distributed training), topology-aware placement (GPU-NVLink affinity), and massive-scale job arrays (10,000+ hyperparameter tuning runs). Kubernetes excels at: microservice orchestration (inference APIs, model registries), rolling updates, and cloud-native ecosystem (Helm, Prometheus, GitOps). The two systems manage separate GPU pools or use bridging tools like NVIDIA Base Command Manager for unified submission.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Slurm + Kubernetes — Complementary Orchestrators
