  │  AI Infrastructure — Slurm + Kubernetes Side by Side            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Slurm Cluster           │  │  Kubernetes Cluster          │  │
  │  │  ├─ A100 Partition (32)  │  │  ├─ H100 Nodes (16 GPUs)    │  │
  │  │  │  → BERT training      │  │  │  → Triton Inference       │  │
  │  │  │  → HP tuning (sarray) │  │  │  → Django API pods        │  │

  │  │  │  → LLM pretraining    │  │  ├─ L40S Nodes (24 GPUs)    │  │
  │  │  └─ CPU Partition (64)   │  │  │  → Model serving API      │  │
  │  │     → Data preprocessing │  │  │  → MLflow Model Registry  │  │
  │  └─────────────────────────┘  └─────────────────────────────┘  │

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, Slurm manages the batch BERT training pipeline on dedicated A100 nodes, while Kubernetes runs the Triton inference API and Django application on H100/L40S nodes. A K8s CronJob triggers daily model retraining via sbatch to Slurm. When training completes, a Slurm epilog script pushes the new model to MLflow on K8s — best of both orchestrators.

Sched/MIG Q125 of 200
When should you choose Slurm over Kubernetes for GPU workload scheduling, and vice versa?
A) Choose Slurm when you need: (1) Gang scheduling (all GPUs start simultaneously for distributed training — K8s can't guarantee this natively without plugins); (2) Large-scale job arrays (10K+ hyperparameter runs — Slurm sarray is purpose-built); (3) Tight MPI integration (Slurm manages the MPI launcher); (4) GPU-NVLink topology-aware placement. Choose Kubernetes when you need: (1) Long-running services (inference APIs, web apps); (2) Cloud-native ecosystem (Helm, GitOps, Service Mesh); (3) Auto-scaling (HPA, KEDA); (4) Multi-team multi-tenancy (namespaces, RBAC, NetworkPolicy)
B) Always choose Kubernetes — Slurm is legacy HPC technology that shouldn't be used for AI workloads
C) Always choose Slurm — Kubernetes adds unnecessary complexity with no benefits for AI workloads
D) The choice doesn't matter — Slurm and Kubernetes are functionally identical for GPU scheduling and can be used interchangeably
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Slurm vs K8s decision depends on workload characteristics. Slurm wins for batch HPC: (1) Gang scheduling — distributed training needs all ranks to start together; K8s default scheduler starts pods individually, potentially causing NCCL timeouts; (2) Job arrays — Slurm's sarray handles 100K hyperparameter jobs with built-in job dependency management; (3) Topology awareness — Slurm can place jobs based on NVLink/NVSwitch topology automatically. K8s wins for services: (1) Long-running inference APIs with rolling updates and canary deployments; (2) Rich ecosystem for monitoring, logging, service mesh; (3) Auto-scaling based on GPU metrics (KEDA with DCGM Prometheus).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Slurm vs Kubernetes — Decision Matrix
  ┌────────────────────────────┬─────────────────┬─────────────────┐
  │ Requirement                │ Slurm           │ Kubernetes      │
  ├────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ Distributed training (NCCL)│ ✅ Gang scheduling│ ⚠️ Needs plugin  │
  │ Large-scale job arrays     │ ✅ Built-in      │ ⚠️ Kueue/Volcano │
  │ Long-running services      │ ⚠️ Not designed  │ ✅ Core strength │
  │ Rolling updates / canary   │ ❌ No             │ ✅ Native        │
  │ GPU-NVLink topology aware  │ ✅ Native         │ ⚠️ Topology Mgr │
  │ Auto-scaling (HPA/KEDA)    │ ❌ No             │ ✅ Native        │
  │ Multi-tenant (RBAC/NS)     │ ⚠️ Limited        │ ✅ Full support  │
  │ Helm / GitOps / Ecosystem  │ ❌ No             │ ✅ Rich          │
  │ MPI integration            │ ✅ First-class   │ ⚠️ MPI Operator  │
  │ Container-native           │ ⚠️ Via enroot    │ ✅ Native        │
  └────────────────────────────┴─────────────────┴─────────────────┘

  Best Practice: Use BOTH — Slurm for batch training, K8s for serving.

For anihpj/jobpost, the architecture uses Slurm for batch BERT training (gang scheduling ensures all 32 GPUs start training simultaneously for correct NCCL initialization) and Kubernetes for the Triton inference API (HPA scales inference pods based on GPU utilization from DCGM metrics). This gives the best of both worlds — HPC efficiency for training, cloud-native agility for serving.

What are the phases of the AI workload lifecycle?
A) AI workloads have no lifecycle — they run continuously like web servers with no distinct phases
B) Data Prep → Training → Validation → Optimization → Deployment — each phase has different GPU requirements: data prep (CPU-heavy, GPU optional), training (max GPU utilization), validation (single GPU, quick), optimization (GPU profiling), deployment (latency-sensitive GPU serving)
C) The lifecycle is just two phases: development and production — all other phases are DevOps overhead
D) AI workloads follow the same lifecycle as traditional applications — install, configure, run

The AI workload lifecycle has 5 distinct phases with different GPU requirements. AIOps manages GPU resources across all phases: scheduling training jobs on H100 clusters, provisioning inference GPUs with autoscaling, managing model storage and versioning, and monitoring GPU health throughout. AI Workload Lifecycle
  Data Prep ──▶ Training ──▶ Validation ──▶ Optimization ──▶ Deployment

Sched/MIG Q128 of 200
How do you handle training job failures and enable checkpoint recovery?
A) Training failures are unrecoverable — you must restart from scratch after any GPU or node failure
B) Kubernetes automatically checkpoints GPU training jobs — no application code changes needed
C) PyTorch DistributedDataParallel with torch.save() writes periodic checkpoints to shared storage (NFS/Lustre/S3). On failure, Slurm/K8s reschedules the job with --resume flag loading the latest checkpoint. Checkpoint includes model weights + optimizer state + epoch number for exact recovery
D) Training checkpointing is handled by the GPU driver — nvidia.ko automatically saves state on GPU memory errors
✅ Correct Answer: C

Training checkpointing requires application-level implementation. Key elements: (1) periodic checkpoint saves (every N steps or minutes), (2) checkpoint includes model state_dict + optimizer state_dict + epoch + random seed, (3) save to shared storage accessible from all GPU nodes, (4) on restart, load checkpoint and resume from saved epoch/step. Slurm job arrays support automatic requeue on failure. Kubernetes Jobs with restartPolicy: OnFailure achieve the same.

Training Checkpoint Recovery — Resilient AI Training
  Checkpoint Save (every N steps):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ if step % 1000 == 0:                                         │
  │     torch.save({                                             │
  │         'epoch': epoch,                                      │
  │         'model_state_dict': model.state_dict(),              │
  │         'optimizer_state_dict': optimizer.state_dict(),      │
  │         'scheduler_state_dict': scheduler.state_dict(),      │
  │         'step': step,                                        │
  │         'best_f1': best_f1,                                  │
  │         'random_state': random.getstate()                    │
  │     }, f'/shared/checkpoints/bert_ckpt_step_{step}.pt')     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Recovery after node failure:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ $ python train.py --resume /shared/checkpoints/latest.pt     │
  │                                                              │
  │ checkpoint = torch.load(args.resume)                         │
  │ model.load_state_dict(checkpoint['model_state_dict'])        │
  │ optimizer.load_state_dict(checkpoint['optimizer_state_dict'])│
  │ start_step = checkpoint['step'] + 1                          │
  │ # Training continues from exactly where it stopped!         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Best Practice: Checkpoint to SHARED storage (Lustre/WEKA/NFS)
  NOT local NVMe — local storage dies with the node!
  Loss: at most 1000 steps (~5 min on H100 with BERT training)

For anihpj/jobpost weekly training, checkpoints save every 1000 steps to a Lustre filesystem. If a DGX node fails mid-training, the job requeues and resumes from the latest checkpoint — losing at most 1000 steps (~5 minutes) of work.

Sched/MIG Q129 of 200
How does hyperparameter optimization (HPO) utilize multiple GPUs in parallel?
A) HPO runs N independent training jobs with different hyperparameters in parallel — each gets its own GPU(s). Slurm job arrays or Kubernetes Jobs launch parallel trials. Frameworks like Optuna, Ray Tune, and Hyperopt manage the search space and early stopping of underperforming trials
B) HPO must run sequentially on a single GPU — each trial requires the previous trial's results to determine the next hyperparameter set
C) HPO runs on CPUs only — GPUs provide no benefit for hyperparameter search because the search algorithm is CPU-bound
D) HPO is built into the GPU driver — nvidia-smi --hpo automatically optimizes hyperparameters for any training script
✅ Correct Answer: A

HPO is embarrassingly parallel — each trial is independent. Slurm job arrays launch N parallel sbatch jobs, each with different hyperparameters. Kubernetes Jobs with indexed completion mode achieve the same. Frameworks like Ray Tune integrate with Slurm/K8s to manage the search, track results, and apply early stopping (pruning underperforming trials). Key GPU consideration: each trial needs its own GPU(s) — HPO with 16 parallel trials requires 16 GPUs.

HPO with Parallel GPU Trials — Slurm Job Array
  Hyperparameter Optimization: BERT Training (16 HP combos)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Slurm Job Array: sbatch --array=1-16 hpo_train.sh          │
  │                                                              │
  │  Trial 1:  lr=1e-5  batch=32  dropout=0.1  → GPU0  F1=0.82 │
  │  Trial 2:  lr=3e-5  batch=32  dropout=0.1  → GPU1  F1=0.84 │
  │  Trial 3:  lr=1e-4  batch=32  dropout=0.1  → GPU2  F1=0.79 │
  │  Trial 4:  lr=3e-5  batch=64  dropout=0.1  → GPU3  F1=0.85 │
  │  ...                                                        │
  │  Trial 16: lr=3e-5  batch=64  dropout=0.3 → GPU15 F1=0.83 │
  │                                                              │
  │  16 trials, each on 1 GPU → 16 GPUs total                   │
  │  Slurm schedules 16 concurrent independent jobs             │
  │  Results aggregated by Ray Tune/MLflow                      │
  │  Early stopping kills bottom 20% trials after 30% epochs    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Sequential vs Parallel: 16 trials × 2h each
    Sequential: 32 hours wall-clock time
    Parallel (Slurm array): 2 hours wall-clock (16× faster!)

For anihpj/jobpost, Ray Tune + Slurm runs 16 parallel BERT fine-tuning trials on 16 A100 GPUs. Each trial explores different learning rates. Early stopping kills underperforming trials after 30% of epochs — reducing total GPU-hours by 40%.

Sched/MIG Q130 of 200
How does Triton Inference Server optimize model serving at scale?
A) Triton is a load balancer — it distributes HTTP requests across GPU pods but doesn't optimize inference
B) Triton replaces the GPU driver — it provides a new CUDA runtime optimized for serving workloads
C) Triton is a model training framework — it doesn't handle inference serving
D) Triton supports dynamic batching (combines multiple client requests into one GPU batch), concurrent model execution (multiple models loaded on same GPU), model ensembles (pipeline of models), GPU metrics per model, and multiple backends (TensorRT, PyTorch, ONNX, TensorFlow) — all configured declaratively
✅ Correct Answer: D

Triton is NVIDIA's production-grade inference server. Key features: dynamic batching — queues individual requests and combines them into optimal batch sizes for GPU efficiency; concurrent model execution — multiple models share GPU memory and run simultaneously; model ensembles — chain models in a DAG (preprocessing → BERT → postprocessing); GPU metrics — per-model latency, throughput, GPU utilization; multiple backends — TensorRT, PyTorch, ONNX, TensorFlow, Python. Deployed via NGC Helm chart on Kubernetes with HPA autoscaling.

Triton Inference Server Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    Triton Inference Server                    │
  │                                                              │
  │  Clients ──▶ gRPC/HTTP ──▶ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │
  │                            │  Dynamic Batcher              │ │
  │                            │  (queues → optimal batches)   │ │
  │                            └──────────┬───────────────────┘ │
  │                                       │                     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Backend Selection                 ▼                  │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────────────┤  │
  │  │  │TensorRT  │ PyTorch  │  ONNX    │ Python/Custom    │  │
  │  │  │(fastest) │(flexible)│(portable)│(any logic)       │  │
  │  │  └──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────────────┤  │
  │  └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                         ▼                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │

Sched/MIG Q131 of 200
How do GPU workload priorities and preemption work in AI clusters, and why are they essential for resource utilization?
A) All GPU workloads run at equal priority — preemption is not supported because interrupting a GPU job causes permanent data loss
B) GPU workload priority is determined by the GPU's clock speed — higher-clocked GPUs get higher priority for critical workloads
C) Priority/preemption enables GPU resource optimization: high-priority production inference jobs preempt low-priority batch training, which are checkpointed and requeued. In Slurm, this is achieved via partition QoS (PriorityTier) and PreemptMode; in K8s, via PriorityClasses and the Kubernetes scheduler. This ensures expensive GPUs are never idle while high-priority work is pending — a critical capability when each GPU costs $30K+
D) Preemption means physically removing GPUs from lower-priority servers and installing them in higher-priority servers — it requires manual hardware changes
✅ Correct Answer: C

Priority and preemption maximize GPU utilization by ensuring critical workloads always get GPU access. In Slurm: partitions have QoS tiers — a job in the "urgent" QoS can preempt jobs in "normal" QoS. Preempted jobs are requeued (if checkpointed) or restarted. In Kubernetes: PriorityClasses (high > medium > low) determine which pods get evicted when resources are scarce. A production inference pod with PriorityClass "prod-critical" will evict a batch training pod with "batch-low" to claim GPUs. Preempted training pods should save checkpoints to persistent storage (WEKA/Lustre) and resume from the last checkpoint.

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GPU Preemption Scenario — 8 GPUs on DGX Node               │
  │                                                              │
  │  Before: All GPUs busy with batch training (Priority: low)  │
  │  GPU0: [BERT HP Tuning  70%]  GPU4: [BERT HP Tuning  72%]   │
  │  GPU1: [BERT HP Tuning  68%]  GPU5: [BERT HP Tuning  71%]   │

  │                                                              │

  │  GPU1: [Triton Inference 94%]  GPU5: [BERT HP Tuning  71%]  │

For anihpj/jobpost, production inference (Triton serving BERT for job matching API) runs at PriorityClass "prod-critical" (value: 1,000,000). Batch hyperparameter tuning jobs run at "batch-low" (value: 100). During Black Friday-level hiring spikes, inference preempts training GPUs automatically — ensuring job seekers get sub-second results while training resumes from checkpoints after the spike subsides.

Sched/MIG Q132 of 200
What are the operational requirements for running multi-node distributed AI training with PyTorch DDP and NCCL?
A) Multi-node training is automatic — as long as all GPUs can ping each other, PyTorch handles everything with zero configuration
B) Requirements: (1) Network — all GPUs must communicate via NCCL (NVLink intra-node + InfiniBand/RoCE inter-node, 400 Gbps+ recommended); (2) Process launch — torchrun or mpirun launches one process per GPU across all nodes with correct RANK, WORLD_SIZE, MASTER_ADDR, MASTER_PORT; (3) Shared storage — all nodes need access to the same dataset and model code (RWX volume via WEKA/Lustre/NFS); (4) Synchronization — NCCL barriers ensure all ranks start together (gang scheduling); (5) Monitoring — DCGM metrics for GPU utilization + NCCL_DEBUG for communication health
C) Multi-node training only requires copying the Python script to each node — PyTorch automatically discovers other nodes via mDNS
D) Multi-node training is not supported by PyTorch — it's only available through NVIDIA's proprietary NeMo framework
✅ Correct Answer: B

Multi-node distributed training requires careful orchestration across five dimensions. (1) Network: NCCL needs high-bandwidth, low-latency interconnect — NVLink (900 GB/s intra-node) + InfiniBand/RoCE (400 Gbps inter-node). Without this, GPU communication dominates training time. (2) Process launch: torchrun sets RANK (0 to WORLD_SIZE-1), MASTER_ADDR (rank 0's IP), and WORLD_SIZE (total processes). Each process maps to one GPU. (3) Shared storage: All nodes need the same dataset path — typically a RWX PVC backed by WEKA/Lustre, or synchronized local copies. (4) Synchronization: All ranks must start simultaneously (gang scheduling) — staggered starts cause NCCL timeouts. (5) Monitoring: DCGM for GPU health + NCCL_DEBUG=INFO for communication debugging.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Node Training — PyTorch DDP + NCCL Architecture
  Node 1 (DGX-1)                          Node 2 (DGX-2)
  ┌────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ RANK 0 (GPU0) ──┐     │            │ RANK 8 (GPU0) ──┐     │
  │ RANK 1 (GPU1) ──┤     │            │ RANK 9 (GPU1) ──┤     │
  │ ...              ├─ NCCL            │ ...              ├─ NCCL
  │ RANK 7 (GPU7) ──┘     │            │ RANK 15 (GPU7)──┘     │
  │         │              │            │         │              │
  │    NVLink (900GB/s)    │            │    NVLink (900GB/s)    │
  └─────────┼──────────────┘            └─────────┼──────────────┘
            │                                     │
            └──── InfiniBand 400G (NCCL All-Reduce) ────┘

  Launch: torchrun --nnodes=2 --nproc_per_node=8 \
            --rdzv_id=123 --rdzv_backend=c10d \
            --rdzv_endpoint=dgx-1:29400 train.py

  Env vars set by torchrun:
    MASTER_ADDR=dgx-1  MASTER_PORT=29400  WORLD_SIZE=16
    RANK=0..7 (node1)  RANK=8..15 (node2)
    LOCAL_RANK=0..7    LOCAL_RANK=0..7

For anihpj/jobpost, BERT training runs on 32 GPUs across 4 DGX nodes using torchrun. The WEKA RWX volume ensures all nodes access the same resumes.parquet dataset. NCCL uses NVLink intra-node (900 GB/s) and InfiniBand inter-node (400 GB/s HDR). Gang scheduling via Slurm ensures all 32 ranks start within 2 seconds — preventing NCCL timeout and achieving 92% scaling efficiency (32 GPUs = 29.4× single GPU throughput).

Sched/MIG Q133 of 200
How should you design AI training jobs to run reliably on spot/preemptible GPU instances that can be terminated at any time?
A) AI training cannot run on spot instances — the risk of losing training progress is too high for any meaningful GPU workload
B) Simply reduce the training batch size — smaller batches complete faster and are less likely to be interrupted
C) Run training exclusively on on-demand instances — spot instances should only be used for inference serving
D) Design for preemption: (1) Frequent checkpointing — save model state every N steps to resilient storage; (2) Checkpoint on preemption signal — AWS gives 2-min warning, GCP gives 30-sec; trap SIGTERM; (3) Resume from checkpoint — training script loads latest checkpoint on restart; (4) Elastic training — torchrun elastic mode handles dynamic worker count; (5) Use K8s Volcano/Kueue for gang-scheduled spot GPU pods with preemption-aware scheduling. Combined, these techniques can reduce GPU costs by 60-70% with minimal training time impact
✅ Correct Answer: D

Spot/preemptible GPU training can save 60-70% on GPU costs when designed correctly. Key patterns: (1) Frequent checkpoints — save every 100-500 steps (not epochs) to minimize lost work; use async checkpointing (write in background while training continues); (2) Preemption signal — AWS Spot Instance Termination Notice (2 min via metadata endpoint), GCP preemption (30 sec via ACPI). Trap this signal and trigger emergency checkpoint; (3) Resume — training script uses --resume-from checkpoint.pt to continue; (4) Elastic training — torchrun --elastic mode handles changing WORLD_SIZE when nodes come and go; (5) K8s integration — Volcano/Kueue schedulers handle gang scheduling with spot node preemption awareness.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Spot GPU Training — Resilient Architecture
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Spot GPU Training Lifecycle                               │
  │                                                            │
  │  1. Submit training job (spot GPU nodes, 60% cheaper)      │
  │     ↓                                                      │
  │  2. Training loop:                                         │
  │     for step in range(resume_step, total_steps):           │
  │         train_batch(...)                                   │
  │         if step % 100 == 0:                                │
  │             torch.save(checkpoint, "s3://ckpt/step_{}.pt") │
  │     ↓                                                      │
  │  3. Preemption signal received (SIGTERM / metadata)        │
  │     → Emergency checkpoint to persistent storage            │
  │     → torch.save(state, "s3://emergency_ckpt.pt")          │
  │     ↓                                                      │
  │  4. New spot nodes allocated (automatic or manual)         │
  │     → Resume from latest checkpoint                        │
  │     → checkpoint = torch.load("s3://ckpt/latest.pt")       │
  │     → Continue from step N                                 │
  │                                                            │
  │  Lost work: < 100 steps (typically < 30 seconds)           │
  │  Cost savings: 60-70% vs on-demand GPUs                    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, BERT hyperparameter tuning runs on spot GPU instances — the job saves checkpoints to MinIO (S3-compatible) every 100 steps and traps the AWS spot termination notice for emergency saves. This reduced hyperparameter tuning costs by 65% ($12K → $4.2K per tuning run) with minimal impact — the worst preemption lost only 300 training steps (2 minutes).

Sched/MIG Q134 of 200
How do you effectively manage GPU job queues to maximize utilization while meeting SLAs for both batch training and real-time inference?
A) Multi-queue strategy: (1) Separate queues for inference (real-time, guaranteed GPUs, never queued) and training (batch, queued, preemptible); (2) Backfill scheduling — Slurm/K8s run lower-priority training jobs on GPUs that would otherwise be idle while waiting for high-priority jobs; (3) Time-based policies — training jobs limited to off-peak hours (nights/weekends), inference has daytime priority; (4) GPU reservations — reserve minimum GPUs for inference SLA, remaining GPUs available for batch; (5) Queue-depth-based scaling — KEDA scales inference pods based on GPU queue depth metrics
B) FIFO (First-In-First-Out) is the only fair scheduling strategy — all jobs enter a single queue and run in submission order regardless of priority
C) GPU job queue management means manually reviewing each job submission and approving it before it runs — automated scheduling is too error-prone for expensive GPUs
D) Job queues are only needed for CPU workloads — GPUs are so fast that queue management is unnecessary
✅ Correct Answer: A

GPU job queue management balances the fundamentally different requirements of inference (real-time, < 50ms P99 latency, needs GPUs NOW) and training (batch, can queue, can pause, can preempt). Key strategies: (1) Dedicated inference GPUs — 20-30% of GPU fleet reserved for inference serving (no queue, always available); (2) Backfill scheduling — training jobs run on inference GPUs during low-traffic periods (nights/weekends); (3) Preemption — if inference demand spikes, training jobs are preempted and GPUs reallocated; (4) KEDA GPU metrics scaling — inference pods auto-scale based on GPU utilization or queue depth, releasing GPUs back to training when demand drops; (5) Time-based partitioning — Slurm partitions with different schedules for daytime (inference priority) vs nighttime (training priority).

Sched/MIG Q135 of 200
How does torchrun enable distributed PyTorch training across multiple GPUs and nodes?
A) torchrun is a GPU monitoring tool — it measures GPU utilization during training but doesn't launch jobs
B) torchrun replaces torch.distributed.launch — it automatically sets up distributed environment variables (RANK, WORLD_SIZE, MASTER_ADDR), handles fault tolerance (elastic training), and launches one process per GPU across all nodes. Used with DistributedDataParallel (DDP) for multi-GPU training
C) torchrun is the same as python — it just runs Python scripts with no GPU awareness
D) torchrun only works on a single node — multi-node training requires a separate MPI installation
✅ Correct Answer: B

torchrun is the modern PyTorch distributed launcher. Example: torchrun --nproc_per_node=8 --nnodes=4 --node_rank=$SLURM_NODEID train.py. It sets RANK (global process ID), WORLD_SIZE (total processes), MASTER_ADDR/PORT (rendezvous) automatically. Supports elastic training — if a node fails, surviving nodes re-rendezvous and continue. Used inside Slurm sbatch scripts or Kubernetes Job specs for multi-node distributed training.

torchrun — Multi-Node Distributed Training Launch
  torchrun Command Structure:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ torchrun \                                                   │
  │   --nproc_per_node=8 \      # 8 processes per node (1/GPU)  │
  │   --nnodes=4 \               # 4 DGX nodes total            │
  │   --node_rank=$SLURM_NODEID \# Slurm sets node rank         │
  │   --rdzv_id=45678 \          # Unique rendezvous ID         │
  │   --rdzv_backend=c10d \      # PyTorch distributed backend   │
  │   --rdzv_endpoint=dgx-1:29400 \ # Master node address       │
  │   train_bert.py --batch-size 256 --epochs 10                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Environment Variables Set by torchrun:
  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ MASTER_ADDR     │ dgx-1 (IP of rank 0 node)                 │
  │ MASTER_PORT     │ 29400                                     │
  │ WORLD_SIZE      │ 32 (4 nodes × 8 GPUs)                     │
  │ RANK            │ 0-31 (unique global process ID)           │
  │ LOCAL_RANK      │ 0-7 (GPU index within node)               │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Elastic Training: If node fails, surviving nodes re-rendezvous
  and continue with reduced WORLD_SIZE (requires --elastic flag)

For anihpj/jobpost, torchrun launches BERT fine-tuning across 4 DGX nodes (32 GPUs). Each GPU processes 1/32 of the batch, gradients are all-reduced via NCCL, and model updates synchronize — completing training 30× faster than single GPU.

Sched/MIG Q136 of 200
How does TensorFlow's distributed training compare to PyTorch's approach?
A) TensorFlow cannot do distributed training — it's single-GPU only
B) TensorFlow and PyTorch distributed training are identical — they use the same API and communication backend
C) TensorFlow uses tf.distribute.Strategy (MirroredStrategy for single-node multi-GPU, MultiWorkerMirroredStrategy for multi-node) with NCCL as the communication backend. Horovod provides an alternative framework-agnostic approach supporting both TF and PyTorch
D) TensorFlow only supports data parallelism — model parallelism requires PyTorch
✅ Correct Answer: C

TF distributed uses tf.distribute.Strategy: MirroredStrategy for single-node multi-GPU (creates per-GPU model replicas, all-reduce gradients via NCCL), MultiWorkerMirroredStrategy for multi-node (adds gRPC-based coordination + NCCL). Horovod (Uber's framework) provides a unified distributed API across TF, PyTorch, and MXNet — many organizations standardize on Horovod for multi-framework GPU clusters. All use NCCL as the GPU communication backend.

  │  └────────────────┘   └──────────────┘   └──────────────┘  │

  │  TensorFlow Distributed (TF_CONFIG + Strategy):              │
  │  ┌────────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐  │
  │  │ TF_CONFIG env  │──▶│ MultiWorker  │──▶│ NCCL         │  │
  │  │ (JSON config)  │   │ MirroredStrat│   │ (GPU comm)   │  │
  │  └────────────────┘   └──────────────┘   └──────────────┘  │
  │                                                              │
  │  Horovod (Framework-Agnostic, both TF + PyTorch):            │
  │  ┌────────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐  │

Sched/MIG Q137 of 200
How does JAX/Flax handle GPU parallelism differently from PyTorch?
A) JAX cannot use GPUs — it's a CPU-only numerical computing library
B) JAX uses the same parallelism model as PyTorch — the APIs are just syntactically different
C) JAX only supports single-GPU execution — multi-GPU training requires PyTorch or TensorFlow
D) JAX uses functional transformations for parallelism: pmap (data parallelism across devices), vmap (vectorized batch operations), pjit (automatic model parallelism with sharding) — these compile to XLA-optimized GPU kernels, achieving efficient parallelism without manual device placement code
✅ Correct Answer: D

JAX's parallelism model is fundamentally different: instead of imperative device placement (PyTorch's .to('cuda:0')), JAX uses functional transformations. pmap automatically shards computation across devices, pjit handles model parallelism with automatic sharding decisions, and XLA compiles everything into optimized GPU kernels. This declarative approach often achieves better performance with less manual tuning. JAX + Flax is increasingly popular for LLM training at scale.

JAX Parallelism — Functional Transformations for GPU Computing
  JAX's Declarative Parallelism (vs PyTorch's Imperative):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  @jax.pmap  → Data Parallelism                              │
  │  def train_step(params, batch):                             │
  │      grads = jax.grad(loss_fn)(params, batch)               │
  │      return jax.tree_map(lambda p, g: p - lr * g,           │
  │                           params, grads)                     │
  │  # pmap auto-shards batch across 8 GPUs — no manual .to()!  │
  │                                                              │
  │  @jax.pjit  → Automatic Model + Data Parallelism            │
  │  with Mesh(GPUs, ('data', 'model')):                        │
  │      result = pjit(train_step,                              │
  │                     in_shardings=(P('data'), P('model')),    │
  │                     out_shardings=P())(params, batch)        │
  │  # pjit auto-decides: which layers on which GPUs            │
  │                                                              │
  │  @jax.vmap  → Auto-Vectorize (batch without loops)          │
  │  batched_fn = jax.vmap(single_fn)  # N→1 line change!       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Key Difference: JAX compiles to XLA → GPU kernels.
  PyTorch executes eagerly. JAX's functional approach often
  achieves better GPU utilization with less manual tuning.

For anihpj/jobpost, the research team experiments with JAX/Flax for next-generation models. The GPU infrastructure (H100 with NVLink + InfiniBand) supports JAX's SPMD parallelism model identically to PyTorch — same hardware, different software stack.

Sched/MIG Q139 of 200
How do you configure Triton Inference Server model repository and model ensembles?
A) Triton models are configured via nvidia-smi — each GPU runs one model with fixed configuration
B) Triton auto-discovers models from /tmp — no explicit configuration is needed for model serving
C) Triton uses a model repository (directory structure with versioned model folders) and config.pbtxt files specifying backend, batch size, instance groups, and GPU allocation. D) Triton only supports TensorRT models — PyTorch and ONNX models must be converted before serving

Triton's model repository is declarative. Each model has a directory with numbered versions (1/, 2/) and a config.pbtxt specifying backend, max_batch_size, dynamic_batching preferences, GPU instance count, and model-specific parameters. Model ensembles chain models: a preprocessing model (tokenization) feeds into BERT, which feeds into postprocessing (softmax + label mapping) — all executed as a single optimized pipeline with shared GPU memory.

Triton Model Repository — Declarative Model Serving
  Model Repository Structure:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

🟡 Core: The Nervous System and Memory of AI Infrastructure

This category is about how GPUs communicate and access data at scale. AI workloads are extremely network and storage intensive — distributed training sends gigabytes of gradients per second between GPUs, and data pipelines feed terabytes of training data. For anihpj/jobpost, this covers the InfiniBand fabric connecting DGX nodes and the high-performance storage serving training data.

Q141-Q170 30 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

4.1 InfiniBand & RDMA for AIQ141-Q148

Network/Storage Q141 of 200
What is InfiniBand and why is it the preferred interconnect for large-scale AI training clusters?
A) InfiniBand is a wireless protocol for GPU clusters — it replaces Ethernet cables with radio signals
B) InfiniBand is a software protocol that runs over standard Ethernet — it's just a configuration change, not dedicated hardware
C) InfiniBand is a legacy HPC technology being phased out — modern AI clusters use standard Ethernet exclusively
D) InfiniBand is a high-bandwidth, ultra-low-latency interconnect providing 400 Gb/s per port (NDR), ~1 µs latency, native RDMA, and hardware congestion control — achieving 85-90% NCCL efficiency vs 40-60% on Ethernet, critical for scaling distributed training to hundreds of GPUs
✅ Correct Answer: D

InfiniBand delivers 400 Gb/s per port with ~1 µs latency at NDR generation. Its native RDMA enables GPU-to-GPU data transfer without CPU involvement. Hardware congestion control prevents the congestion collapse that plagues lossless Ethernet (PFC storms). For NCCL all-reduce across 256 GPUs, IB achieves 85-90% of theoretical peak vs 40-60% on RoCE Ethernet — this 2× efficiency difference is why every Top500 supercomputer and large AI cluster uses InfiniBand for the compute fabric.

InfiniBand vs Ethernet for AI
  ┌──────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┐
  │          │ InfiniBand   │ Ethernet RoCE │
  ├──────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Latency  │ ~1 µs        │ ~10 µs       │
  │ Bandwidth│ 400 Gb/s NDR │ 400 Gb/s     │
  │ NCCL Eff │ 85-90%       │ 40-60%       │
  │ RDMA     │ Native       │ RoCE v2      │
  │ Cost     │ Higher       │ Lower        │
  └──────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost DGX SuperPOD, the InfiniBand NDR fabric connects 32 DGX nodes at 400 GB/s each — ensuring distributed BERT training achieves 85%+ scaling efficiency across 256 GPUs.

Network/Storage Q142 of 200
What are the InfiniBand speed generations and their effective throughput?
A) HDR (200 Gb/s, 4× 50Gb/s lanes), NDR (400 Gb/s, 4× 100Gb/s lanes), XDR (800 Gb/s, next-gen) — each generation doubles bandwidth. Effective throughput is ~95% of signaling rate due to 64/66 encoding. NDR400 delivers ~380 Gb/s usable per port
B) InfiniBand speeds are the same as Ethernet — 100GbE, 200GbE, 400GbE with identical performance
C) InfiniBand only has one speed (100 Gb/s) — faster speeds require multiple cables bonded together
D) InfiniBand speeds are measured in GB/s, not Gb/s — HDR = 200 GB/s per port
✅ Correct Answer: A

InfiniBand generations: EDR (100 Gb/s, 4×25G), HDR (200 Gb/s, 4×50G), NDR (400 Gb/s, 4×100G), XDR (800 Gb/s, upcoming). Each uses 4 lanes with PAM4 or NRZ encoding. 64/66 encoding means ~96.97% efficiency — NDR400 delivers ~388 Gb/s usable. DGX H100 uses 8× NDR ports = 3.2 Tb/s aggregate per node. H200 upgrades to NDR400 with 4.8 TB/s HBM3e memory bandwidth.

InfiniBand Speed Generations
  ┌──────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────────┐
  │ Gen  │ Speed    │ Lanes    │ Year         │
  ├──────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────────┤
  │ EDR  │ 100 Gb/s │ 4×25G NRZ│ 2014         │
  │ HDR  │ 200 Gb/s │ 4×50G PAM4│ 2018        │
  │ NDR  │ 400 Gb/s │ 4×100G   │ 2022 (DGX H100)│
  │ XDR  │ 800 Gb/s │ 4×200G   │ 2025+ (DGX B200)│
  └──────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost DGX H100 SuperPOD, 8× NDR ports per DGX provide 3.2 Tb/s aggregate bandwidth — sufficient for non-blocking all-reduce across 256 GPUs without oversubscription.

Network/Storage Q143 of 200
How does RDMA enable zero-copy data transfer between GPU nodes?
A) RDMA compresses data before sending — reducing network bandwidth requirements by 50%
B) RDMA is a software protocol that runs over TCP — it uses standard socket APIs with kernel bypass
C) RDMA allows the NIC to directly read/write remote GPU memory via DMA — data flows GPU memory → NIC → wire → NIC → GPU memory without touching either host's CPU or system RAM. This eliminates 4-8 memory copies and reduces latency from ~800 µs (TCP) to ~12 µs
D) RDMA is a GPU-to-GPU protocol that doesn't require a network — it works within a single node via NVLink
✅ Correct Answer: C

RDMA is zero-copy networking. Traditional TCP path: GPU mem → CPU mem → kernel buffer → NIC (4 copies, CPU busy). RDMA path: NIC reads GPU memory directly via PCIe DMA, sends over wire, receiving NIC writes directly to remote GPU memory via DMA — zero CPU involvement, zero copies. This reduces latency from ~800 µs (TCP/IP) to ~12 µs (RDMA) — a 65× improvement. GPUDirect RDMA extends this to direct GPU-to-GPU transfers.

TCP vs RDMA — Why RDMA Wins for GPU Communication
  Traditional TCP/IP (8 memory copies!):
  GPU0 HBM → CPU0 RAM → Kernel Buffer → NIC TX → Wire →
  NIC RX → Kernel Buffer → CPU1 RAM → GPU1 HBM
  Latency: ~800 µs   CPU Overhead: 100% per transfer

  RDMA (Zero Copy, Zero CPU):
  GPU0 HBM ──DMA──▶ NIC TX ──Wire──▶ NIC RX ──DMA──▶ GPU1 HBM
  Latency: ~12 µs   CPU Overhead: 0%

  ┌──────────────┬─────────────┬──────────────┐
  │ Metric       │ TCP/IP      │ RDMA (IB)    │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Latency      │ ~800 µs     │ ~12 µs (65×) │
  │ CPU Overhead │ 100% (poll) │ 0% (DMA)     │
  │ Memory Copies│ 8           │ 0            │
  │ Throughput   │ ~350 GB/s   │ ~380 GB/s    │
  │ NCCL Support │ Low         │ Native       │
  └──────────────┴─────────────┴──────────────┘

  For 256-GPU all-reduce: RDMA = 50 µs, TCP = 3.3 ms (65× slower)
  → RDMA is the difference between linear and sub-linear scaling

For anihpj/jobpost distributed training, RDMA enables NCCL all-reduce across 256 GPUs with 12 µs latency — gradients from all GPUs are synchronized in microseconds, enabling near-linear training speedup.

Network/Storage Q144 of 200
What is RoCE v2 and how does it compare to native InfiniBand?
A) RoCE v2 is identical to InfiniBand — it's just a different brand name for the same technology
B) RoCE v2 (RDMA over Converged Ethernet) encapsulates RDMA in UDP/IP packets over standard Ethernet switches — it provides RDMA benefits at lower hardware cost, but requires careful network configuration (PFC, ECN, DCB) and typically achieves lower NCCL efficiency (40-60%) than native IB (85-90%)
C) RoCE v2 is only for storage traffic — it cannot be used for GPU-to-GPU communication
D) RoCE v2 is faster than InfiniBand because Ethernet switches have lower latency than IB switches
✅ Correct Answer: B

RoCE v2 brings RDMA to standard Ethernet — encapsulating IB transport in UDP/IP packets. Lower cost (Ethernet switches are cheaper than IB), but requires lossless Ethernet configuration: PFC (Priority Flow Control) for zero packet loss, ECN (Explicit Congestion Notification) for congestion management, DCB (Data Center Bridging) for QoS. Misconfiguration causes PFC storms that cripple the entire network. NCCL efficiency: 40-60% on well-tuned RoCE vs 85-90% on native IB — IB's hardware congestion control is fundamentally more robust.

InfiniBand vs RoCE v2
  ┌──────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │          │ Native IB        │ RoCE v2          │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Hardware │ IB HCA + Switch  │ Ethernet NIC+Switch│
  │ Protocol │ IB Verbs         │ UDP/IP + IB Trans │
  │ NCCL Eff │ 85-90%           │ 40-60%            │
  │ Config   │ Simple (OpenSM)  │ Complex (PFC/ECN) │
  │ Cost     │ Higher           │ Lower             │
  └──────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the primary DGX cluster uses native IB for maximum training performance. A secondary GPU cluster uses RoCE v2 for cost-sensitive inference traffic where 40-60% NCCL efficiency is acceptable.

Network/Storage Q145 of 200
How does GPUDirect RDMA enable direct GPU-to-GPU data transfer across nodes?
A) GPUDirect RDMA compresses GPU memory before network transfer — reducing bandwidth needs
B) GPUDirect RDMA routes GPU traffic through the CPU for security inspection before sending over the network
C) GPUDirect RDMA is a software library that optimizes host-side memory copies between GPU and NIC
D) GPUDirect RDMA allows the NIC to DMA directly from/to GPU memory via PCIe — GPU 1's HBM → NIC DMA → wire → NIC DMA → GPU 2's HBM. Zero CPU involvement, zero system memory copies. Requires compatible NIC (ConnectX) and GPU (A100/H100) with BAR1 mapping of GPU memory
✅ Correct Answer: D

GPUDirect RDMA is the ultimate zero-copy GPU networking. The NIC's DMA engine reads/writes GPU HBM directly via PCIe — the GPU exposes its memory through BAR1 (Base Address Register), making it addressable by PCIe peers. Requirements: NVIDIA GPU (A100/H100), ConnectX NIC (CX6/CX7), nvidia_peermem kernel module. NCCL uses GPUDirect RDMA automatically when available — this is why NCCL achieves 85-90% of theoretical IB bandwidth.

GPUDirect RDMA — Direct GPU-to-GPU Data Transfer
  Traditional RDMA (via CPU bounce buffer):
  GPU0 HBM → CPU0 RAM (PCIe) → NIC DMA → Wire → NIC DMA → CPU1 RAM → GPU1 HBM
  Still requires 2 CPU memory copies! CPU RAM is the middleman.

  GPUDirect RDMA (Zero-Copy GPU-to-GPU):
  GPU0 HBM ──NIC DMA (PCIe)──▶ Wire ──▶ NIC DMA (PCIe)──▶ GPU1 HBM
           BAR1 mapping                            BAR1 mapping
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Node 0 (DGX-1)                  Node 1 (DGX-2)              │
  │ ┌──────────┐                    ┌──────────┐               │
  │ │ GPU0 HBM │                    │ GPU1 HBM │               │
  │ │ BAR1     │──NIC DMA──▶400G──▶│ NIC DMA──│ BAR1          │
  │ │ exposed  │    PCIe            │ PCIe     │ exposed       │
  │ └──────────┘                    └──────────┘               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Requirements:
    • GPU: A100/H100 with BAR1 support (GPU memory PCIe-mapped)
    • NIC: ConnectX-6/7 with GPUDirect RDMA support
    • Kernel: nvidia_peermem module (allows NIC to peer GPU BAR)
    • NCCL: uses GPUDirect RDMA automatically when available

  Performance: 380 GB/s NCCL BW = 95% of NDR400 theoretical peak

For anihpj/jobpost distributed training, GPUDirect RDMA enables 32 DGX nodes (256 GPUs) to synchronize gradients in microseconds — the entire 256-GPU all-reduce completes in ~50 µs, enabling near-linear training scaling.

Network/Storage Q146 of 200
What is NCCL and how does it optimize multi-GPU communication?
A) NCCL (NVIDIA Collective Communication Library) is a topology-aware GPU communication library — it implements optimized all-reduce, all-gather, broadcast, and reduce-scatter algorithms, automatically selecting ring vs tree topology based on GPU interconnect (NVLink, IB, RoCE), and achieving near-peak bandwidth for collective operations
B) NCCL is a GPU monitoring library — it tracks GPU utilization and temperature during training
C) NCCL replaces NVLink — it's a software alternative to hardware GPU interconnects
D) NCCL only works within a single node — multi-node communication requires MPI
✅ Correct Answer: A

NCCL is the communication backbone of distributed GPU training. It automatically detects GPU topology (NVLink within node, IB/RoCE across nodes) and selects optimal algorithms: Ring for large messages (bandwidth-optimized, pipeline data around ring), Tree for small messages (latency-optimized, log(N) steps). NCCL is used by PyTorch DDP, TensorFlow, Horovod, JAX, and RAPIDS — every major AI framework relies on NCCL for multi-GPU communication.

NCCL — Topology-Aware Collective Communication
  NCCL Auto-Detection & Algorithm Selection:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NCCL probes GPU topology at init:                           │
  │  ┌──────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Intra-node       │ NVLink (900 GB/s), NVSwitch       │  │
  │  │ Inter-node       │ InfiniBand (400 GB/s), RoCE       │  │
  │  │ GPU affinity     │ NUMA node, PCIe root complex      │  │
  │  └──────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                              │
  │  Algorithm Selection:                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Ring         │ Bandwidth-optimized, pipeline data     │  │
  │  │              │ around ring. N-1 steps. Large msgs.    │  │
  │  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ Tree         │ Latency-optimized, log₂(N) steps.      │  │
  │  │              │ Small messages, many GPUs.             │  │
  │  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ CollNet      │ Hardware-accelerated (NVSwitch).       │  │
  │  │              │ Single-step all-reduce on DGX.         │  │
  │  └──────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                              │
  │  Verify: NCCL_DEBUG=INFO nccl-tests all_reduce_perf -g 8    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Who uses NCCL: PyTorch DDP, TensorFlow, Horovod, JAX, RAPIDS, NeMo
  → Every major AI framework relies on NCCL for multi-GPU training

For anihpj/jobpost, NCCL enables PyTorch DDP to synchronize gradients across 32 GPUs in ~200 µs. Without NCCL's topology-aware optimization, the same operation would take 10× longer with manual MPI collectives.

Network/Storage Q147 of 200
What are NVLink and NVSwitch, and how do they enable intra-node GPU communication?
A) NVLink is a software protocol for GPU memory sharing — NVSwitch is the software driver that manages it
B) NVLink is a PCIe alternative — it replaces PCIe entirely for GPU-to-CPU communication
C) NVLink is a high-bandwidth GPU interconnect (900 GB/s bidirectional on H100) connecting GPUs directly. NVSwitch connects all 8 GPUs in a DGX in an all-to-all topology — any GPU can communicate with any other at full 900 GB/s without intermediate hops. Combined, they provide 7× the bandwidth of PCIe Gen4 for GPU-to-GPU communication
D) NVLink and NVSwitch only work within a single GPU — they don't connect multiple GPUs together
✅ Correct Answer: C

NVLink 4.0 on H100 provides 900 GB/s bidirectional per GPU — 7× PCIe Gen4 (128 GB/s). NVSwitch connects 8 GPUs in an all-to-all topology: GPU 0 can communicate with GPU 7 directly at 900 GB/s through NVSwitch, not through intermediate GPUs (ring) or PCIe switches. This enables single-step all-reduce across 8 GPUs in ~1 µs — critical for tensor parallelism where activations must be communicated every layer.

NVLink 4.0 + NVSwitch — DGX H100 All-to-All Topology
  DGX H100 Internal Topology (8 GPUs, 4 NVSwitches):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GPU0 ◀══NVSwitch_0══▶ GPU1 ◀══NVSwitch_1══▶ GPU2           │
  │   │         │           │         │           │              │
  │   └──NVSwitch_2──┐      └──NVSwitch_3──┐      │              │
  │                  │                     │      │              │
  │  GPU3 ◀══NVSwitch_0══▶ GPU4 ◀══NVSwitch_1══▶ GPU5           │
  │   │         │           │         │           │              │
  │   └──NVSwitch_2──┐      └──NVSwitch_3──┐      │              │
  │                  │                     │      │              │
  │  GPU6 ◀══NVSwitch_0══▶ GPU7 ◀══NVSwitch_1══▶ ...            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Any GPU → Any GPU: 1 hop through NVSwitch, 900 GB/s full BW

  ┌──────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ NVLink Generation│ Bandwidth per GPU (bidirectional)     │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ NVLink 3 (A100)  │ 600 GB/s (12× 50 GB/s links)         │
  │ NVLink 4 (H100)  │ 900 GB/s (18× 50 GB/s links)         │
  │ NVLink 5 (B200)  │ 1,800 GB/s (Blackwell, projected)     │
  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PCIe Gen4        │ 64 GB/s (×16) — NVLink 4 is 14× faster │
  │ PCIe Gen5        │ 128 GB/s (×16) — NVLink 5 is 14× faster │
  └──────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Check NVLink health: nvidia-smi nvlink -s
  DCGM metric: DCGM_FI_DEV_NVLINK_BANDWIDTH_TOTAL

For anihpj/jobpost DGX H100, NVSwitch enables 8-GPU tensor parallelism with near-zero communication overhead — the BERT model's attention heads are split across 8 GPUs, communicating intermediate tensors at 900 GB/s through NVSwitch.

Network/Storage Q148 of 200
What is OpenSM and how does it manage an InfiniBand fabric?
A) OpenSM is a GPU monitoring tool — it tracks SM utilization across all GPUs in a cluster
B) OpenSM is not needed — InfiniBand fabrics are self-configuring and don't require management
C) OpenSM is a storage manager — it handles NVMe drive configuration in DGX systems
D) OpenSM (Open Subnet Manager) is the InfiniBand fabric manager — it discovers all IB devices, assigns LIDs (Local Identifiers), computes optimal routing tables, and continuously monitors fabric health. Without a running SM, the IB fabric has no routes and no communication is possible
✅ Correct Answer: D

OpenSM is mandatory for any InfiniBand fabric — without it, the fabric is dead. It runs on one or more nodes (typically the management node), discovers all HCAs and switches, assigns 16-bit LIDs to every port, computes forwarding tables using routing algorithms (MinHop, Up/Down, Fat-Tree), and monitors for topology changes. Supports partitions (virtual fabrics with isolation) and QoS (service levels for traffic prioritization). For HA, multiple SMs can run in standby mode.

OpenSM — InfiniBand Fabric Manager
  OpenSM Lifecycle:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. DISCOVER: Scan fabric for all HCAs, switches, links     │
  │    ┌──────┐    ┌──────┐    ┌──────┐    ┌──────┐           │
  │    │ DGX1 │────│ IB   │────│ DGX2 │────│ DGX3 │           │
  │    │ HCA  │    │Switch│    │ HCA  │    │ HCA  │           │
  │    └──────┘    └──────┘    └──────┘    └──────┘           │
  │                                                             │
  │ 2. ASSIGN LIDs: 16-bit Local Identifiers to every port     │
  │    DGX1 HCA Port1 → LID 0x0001                              │
  │    DGX2 HCA Port1 → LID 0x0002                              │
  │    IB Switch Port1 → LID 0x0010                             │
  │                                                             │
  │ 3. COMPUTE ROUTES: Fat-Tree routing for non-blocking fabric │
  │    Linear forwarding tables programmed into each switch     │
  │                                                             │
  │ 4. MONITOR: Sweep fabric every 5s for topology changes     │
  │    Link down → recompute routes → notify subscribers       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  HA Setup: Active SM (master) + Standby SM on separate node
           Automatic failover in < 3 seconds if master fails

  Partitions (PKeys): Virtualize IB fabric — isolate AI traffic
                      from storage/management for QoS guarantees

For anihpj/jobpost DGX SuperPOD, OpenSM runs on the management node with HA standby. Partitions isolate AI training traffic (high priority) from storage traffic (medium priority) — ensuring NCCL all-reduce never competes with storage I/O for IB bandwidth.

4.2 AI Cluster Network TopologyQ149-Q156

Network/Storage Q149 of 200
Why do AI clusters prefer a leaf-spine network topology over traditional three-tier (access-aggregation-core) architectures?
A) Leaf-spine uses fewer switches and cables, reducing hardware costs by 60% for AI deployments
B) Leaf-spine provides equal-cost, any-to-any connectivity where every leaf connects to every spine — eliminating oversubscription between any two GPUs. This ensures predictable, non-blocking bandwidth essential for NCCL all-reduce collective operations during distributed AI training
C) Leaf-spine is required by NVIDIA GPUs; traditional three-tier networks physically cannot carry GPU traffic
D) Leaf-spine uses wireless mesh interconnects, eliminating the need for physical cables between GPU nodes
✅ Correct Answer: B

In a leaf-spine topology, every leaf switch connects to every spine switch — creating a full-mesh between tiers. The distance between any two endpoints is exactly 3 hops (source → leaf → spine → leaf → destination), giving predictable latency. With proper oversubscription ratios (1:1 for AI), any GPU can communicate with any other GPU at full line rate. This is critical for NCCL all-reduce where all GPUs exchange gradient data simultaneously — a bottleneck in traditional tree topologies where cross-rack traffic competes for uplinks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Leaf-Spine Topology — 16 GPU Nodes
  Traditional 3-Tier                    Leaf-Spine (AI-Optimized)
  ┌─────────┐                          ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐
  │  Core   │  ← Oversubscribed        │ Spine1│ │ Spine2│ │ Spine3│ │ Spine4│
  └──┬──┬───┘                          └─┬──┬──┘ └─┬──┬──┘ └─┬──┬──┘ └─┬──┬──┘
  ┌──┴──┴───┐                            │  │      │  │      │  │      │  │
  │  Aggr   │  ← Bottleneck             └──┼──────┼──┼──────┼──┼──────┼──┘
  └──┬──┬───┘                           ┌──┴──────┴──┼──────┴──┼──────┴──┐
  ┌──┴──┴───┐                          │  Leaf1    │  Leaf2  │  Leaf3   │  Leaf4
  │ Access  │  ← 1:many oversub        └──┬──┬──┬──┘ └──┬──┬──┘ └──┬──┬──┘
  └──┬──┬───┘                         GPU GPU GPU   GPU GPU   GPU GPU
                                     ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
