5.2 GPU Metrics & Prometheus IntegrationQ181-Q186

Monitoring/Security Q181 of 200
Write the PromQL query to calculate the average GPU utilization across all H100 GPUs on DGX nodes over the last 5 minutes.
A) avg(rate(DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL{model="NVIDIA H100"}[5m])) — this calculates the per-second rate of GPU utilization (converting the cumulative counter to a rate), then averages across all matching GPUs. For gauge metrics, simply avg(DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL{node=~"dgx-.*"}) gives the current average
B) SELECT AVG(gpu_util) FROM nvidia_gpus WHERE model='H100' AND time > now() - 5m — Prometheus uses SQL-like syntax for querying time-series data
C) dcgmi query --metric GPU_UTIL --average --time 5m — Prometheus queries are done through the dcgmi CLI, not PromQL
D) nvidia-smi --query-gpu=utilization.gpu --format=csv | awk '{sum+=$1} END {print sum/NR}' — Prometheus cannot query GPU metrics; you must use nvidia-smi
✅ Correct Answer: A

PromQL (Prometheus Query Language) is used to query all Prometheus metrics, including DCGM GPU metrics. The key functions: rate() — calculates per-second rate of increase for counter metrics; avg() — averages across time series; max()/min() — find extremes; topk()/bottomk() — find top/bottom N; histogram_quantile() — calculate percentiles from histograms. DCGM metrics use labels for powerful filtering: {node="dgx-1"}, {gpu="0"}, {model="NVIDIA H100"}. Note that many DCGM metrics are cumulative counters (e.g., ECC error counts), so you need rate() or increase() to compute meaningful rates.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Common PromQL Queries for GPU Monitoring
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Query                                              │ What It Does │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ avg(DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL)                          │ Avg util now │
  │ max(DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_TEMP)                          │ Hottest GPU  │
  │ avg(rate(DCGM_FI_DEV_PCIE_TX_THROUGHPUT[5m]))       │ PCIe BW rate │
  │ rate(DCGM_FI_DEV_ECC_SBE_VOL_TOTAL[1h])*3600       │ SBE/hour     │
  │ count(DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL < 10)                   │ Idle GPUs    │
  │ topk(5, DCGM_FI_DEV_POWER_USAGE)                   │ Top 5 power  │
  │ DCGM_FI_DEV_CLOCK_THROTTLE_REASONS > 0             │ Throttled    │
  │ DCGM_FI_DEV_FB_USED / DCGM_FI_DEV_FB_TOTAL * 100    │ VRAM % used  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┘

  Alert rule example (Prometheus):
    - alert: GPUThrottling
      expr: DCGM_FI_DEV_CLOCK_THROTTLE_REASONS{node=~"dgx-.*"} > 0
      for: 5m
      annotations:
        summary: "GPU {{ $labels.gpu }} on {{ $labels.node }} is throttling"

For anihpj/jobpost, the primary Grafana dashboard uses avg(rate(DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL{node=~"dgx-.*"}[5m])) by (node) to show per-node GPU utilization trends. A recording rule pre-computes avg_gpu_util_5m:dgx for faster dashboard queries, reducing Prometheus query load during 32-GPU monitoring.

Monitoring/Security Q182 of 200
What are the essential Grafana dashboard panels for monitoring an AI GPU cluster in production?

B) Grafana cannot visualize GPU metrics because they are time-series data formatted differently from standard Prometheus metrics
C) The only Grafana panel needed for GPUs is a utilization bar chart — other metrics like temperature and memory are only relevant for hardware engineers, not AI operators
D) Essential panels: (1) GPU Utilization heatmap (all GPUs, color-coded green→red, spot idle/anomalies instantly); (2) GPU Temperature timeseries (per-GPU lines with 85°C threshold); (3) GPU Memory Usage gauge (VRAM used/total, alert near OOM); (4) GPU Power draw vs TDP (identify power throttling); (5) ECC Error counter (per-GPU, cumulative); (6) NVLink Bandwidth timeseries (detect fabric degradation). A comprehensive GPU Grafana dashboard provides multi-dimensional visibility into GPU fleet health. The NVIDIA DCGM dashboard (Grafana.com ID: 12239) is the starting point. Key panels: (1) Utilization heatmap — grid of all GPUs, color from green (100%) to red (0%), instantly spot idle or imbalanced GPUs; (2) Temperature timeseries — per-GPU lines with a red threshold line at 85°C; (3) Memory gauge — VRAM used vs total, with 90% warning and 95% critical thresholds; (4) Power gauge — actual draw vs TDP, throttling manifests as power below TDP under full load; (5) ECC counter — cumulative SBE/DBE per GPU over time; (6) NVLink throughput — per-GPU timeseries to detect degraded links.

Monitoring/Security Q183 of 200
A GPU shows 95% GPU_UTIL but only 15% MEM_COPY_UTIL. What does this tell you about the AI workload?
A) The GPU is perfectly healthy — 95% utilization means the workload is running optimally with no bottlenecks
B) The GPU compute cores are nearly saturated (95%), but memory bandwidth is barely used (15%) — this indicates a compute-bound workload (heavy matrix math with small data movement, like inference with small batch sizes) OR a data-loading bottleneck where the GPU is waiting for data to arrive in VRAM before compute can start, causing idle compute cycles between bursts
C) The GPU is about to fail — high compute utilization with low memory bandwidth indicates a hardware fault in the memory controller
D) GPU_UTIL and MEM_COPY_UTIL always track together — a difference this large is impossible and indicates a bug in DCGM metric collection
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU_UTIL (compute utilization) and MEM_COPY_UTIL (memory bandwidth utilization) measure different GPU subsystems. High GPU_UTIL + low MEM_COPY_UTIL means compute-bound — the GPU cores are the bottleneck, not data movement. This is common in: (1) Inference with small batches (lightweight memory access, heavy compute on few tensors); (2) Small models that fit entirely in L2 cache; (3) Stalled pipelines — GPU compute shows "busy" while actually waiting for data (DCGM reports GPU_UTIL based on kernel occupancy, not useful work). The reverse pattern (low GPU_UTIL + high MEM_COPY_UTIL) indicates memory-bound — typical of data loading phases or large tensor operations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU_UTIL vs MEM_COPY_UTIL — Decoding Utilization Patterns
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ GPU_UTIL │ MEM_COPY_UTIL │ What's Happening                      │
  ├──────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 95%      │ 85%           │ ✅ Ideal: Compute + data in balance   │
  │ 95%      │ 15%           │ ⚠️ Compute-bound: small model/batch  │
  │          │               │    or stalled waiting for data        │
  │ 30%      │ 90%           │ ⚠️ Memory-bound: data loading phase  │
  │          │               │    or large tensor copies             │
  │ 95%      │ 95%           │ ✅ Full throttle: large model train  │
  │ 10%      │ 5%            │ ❌ Idle or crashed process            │
  └──────────┴───────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  The "95/15 Trap": DCGM GPU_UTIL = kernel slot occupancy, not useful work.
  A GPU waiting on a CUDA stream barrier shows 95% GPU_UTIL but does zero
  useful computation! Always check MEM_COPY_UTIL as the "real work" indicator.

For anihpj/jobpost, the BERT inference service initially showed 95% GPU_UTIL with only 15% MEM_COPY_UTIL — the inference batch size was too small (batch=1), making each GPU do light compute on a single resume at a time. Increasing to batch=32 pushed MEM_COPY_UTIL to 82% and improved throughput 6× with the same 95% GPU_UTIL — a common GPU optimization pattern.

Monitoring/Security Q184 of 200
How do you detect and alert on underutilized GPUs in a shared Kubernetes cluster, and why is this important for AI infrastructure cost management?
A) Underutilized GPUs are not a problem — idle GPUs consume negligible power and have no cost impact on the organization
B) GPU idle detection requires physically checking each GPU's LED status lights — software monitoring cannot reliably determine GPU utilization
C) Monitor DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL over time — if GPU_UTIL < 10% for > 1 hour during business hours, the GPU is likely idle (crashed pod, finished job not cleaned up, or oversubscribed cluster). Prometheus alert: avg_over_time(DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL[1h]) < 10. GPU idle is expensive — an H100 costs ~$30K and $4-6/hour in cloud, so idle GPUs represent significant wasted investment. Automated detection triggers pod rescheduling or node cordoning
D) GPU idle detection requires a separate NVIDIA license (NVIDIA GPU Resource Manager) — it's not possible with the standard DCGM metrics included with GPU Operator
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU idle detection is critical for AI infrastructure cost management. An idle H100 GPU wastes approximately $4-6/hour in cloud or represents stranded capital on-prem. Detection strategy: (1) Short-term alert: DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL < 10 for 15m during training hours — indicates crashed training process; (2) Long-term analysis: avg_over_time(DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL[24h]) < 20 — identifies consistently underutilized GPUs for consolidation; (3) Zero-utilization alert: DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL == 0 for 5m — GPU not assigned to any workload. Automated responses: cordon node, migrate workloads, notify AI Ops team.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Idle Detection — Prometheus Alert Rules
  groups:
    - name: gpu_idle_alerts
      rules:
        - alert: GPUIdle
          expr: DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL < 10
          for: 15m
          labels:
            severity: warning
          annotations:
            summary: "GPU {{ $labels.gpu }} on {{ $labels.node }} is idle"

        - alert: GPUCrashedProcess
          expr: DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL == 0
          for: 5m
          labels:
            severity: critical
          annotations:
            summary: "GPU {{ $labels.gpu }} has ZERO utilization — likely crashed"

        - alert: GPUUnderutilized
          expr: avg_over_time(DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL[24h]) < 20
          labels:
            severity: info
          annotations:
            summary: "GPU {{ $labels.gpu }} underutilized over 24h — consider consolidation"

  Cost impact: 1 idle H100 for 1 week = ~$1,000 cloud cost (on-demand)
              10 idle H100s for 1 month = ~$40,000 wasted!

For anihpj/jobpost, GPU idle detection caught a training pod that completed but wasn't cleaned up — the GPU remained allocated to a terminated pod for 6 hours. The Prometheus alert triggered automatic GPU deallocation via a Kubernetes cleanup CronJob. This single fix saved ~$24 in cloud costs for that incident and prevented dozens of similar occurrences through automated cleanup.

Monitoring/Security Q185 of 200
Which DCGM metrics indicate NVLink fabric degradation, and how do you interpret them?
A) DCGM_FI_DEV_NVLINK_BANDWIDTH_TOTAL (throughput per GPU — drop indicates degraded link), DCGM_FI_DEV_NVLINK_CRC_FLIT_ERROR_COUNT_TOTAL (CRC errors on NVLink packets — any non-zero value indicates signal integrity issues), and nvidia-smi nvlink -s (per-lane status and error counters). A fully healthy NVLink shows 900 GB/s bidirectional per GPU (H100) with zero CRC errors. A 50% bandwidth drop with increasing CRC errors indicates a failing NVLink bridge or cable
B) NVLink health is indicated by GPU temperature — if the GPU is below 80°C, NVLink is working correctly; NVLink doesn't have its own metrics
C) NVLink metrics are only available through physical inspection of the NVSwitch LEDs — there are no software-accessible NVLink metrics in DCGM
D) DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL directly indicates NVLink health — if GPU utilization is above 90%, NVLink is working at full capacity
✅ Correct Answer: A

NVLink health monitoring is critical because degraded NVLink directly impacts multi-GPU training performance. Key metrics: (1) DCGM_FI_DEV_NVLINK_BANDWIDTH_TOTAL — total NVLink throughput in MB/s, should approach 900 GB/s bidirectional for H100 with all 18 links active; (2) DCGM_FI_DEV_NVLINK_CRC_FLIT_ERROR_COUNT_TOTAL — CRC errors on NVLink flits (flow control units); any non-zero value indicates signal integrity problems — check NVLink bridge seating, cable connections, or thermal issues; (3) nvidia-smi nvlink -s — shows per-GPU NVLink status with per-lane error counters and link states (Active/Inactive).

📝 Detailed Explanation
NVLink Health Monitoring — Key Indicators
  $ nvidia-smi nvlink -s
  GPU 0: NVIDIA H100 NVL
     Link 0: Active, 50 GB/s, CRC Errors: 0
     Link 1: Active, 50 GB/s, CRC Errors: 0
     ...
     Link 17: Active, 50 GB/s, CRC Errors: 0
     Total: 18/18 links Active, 900 GB/s (bidirectional)

  GPU 1: NVIDIA H100 NVL
     Link 0: Active, 50 GB/s, CRC Errors: 0
     Link 1: Active, 50 GB/s, CRC Errors: 0
     ...
     Link 12: Inactive (FAILED!), CRC Errors: 12,456  ← DEGRADED!
     Link 13: Active, 25 GB/s (DEGRADED!), CRC Errors: 3,201
     ...
     Total: 16/18 links Active, 750 GB/s (17% bandwidth loss)

  Impact on NCCL: 17% NVLink bandwidth loss → 30-40% all-reduce slowdown
                   (NCCL algorithms depend on symmetric full-bandwidth links)

For anihpj/jobpost, NVLink CRC monitoring caught a degrading NVSwitch bridge on DGX-3: CRC errors on GPU-4 Link-12 increased from 0 to 500+ over 3 days, with bandwidth dropping from 900 GB/s to 820 GB/s. The DGX was scheduled for maintenance during non-peak hours, NVSwitch bridge was replaced, and full 900 GB/s was restored — all before NCCL performance was visibly impacted.

Monitoring/Security Q186 of 200
What do GPU power metrics reveal about AI workload efficiency, and what is TDP vs actual power draw?
A) GPU power metrics are only useful for calculating electricity bills — they provide no insight into workload performance or GPU health
B) Higher GPU power draw always means better performance — you should run GPUs at maximum TDP for all workloads to maximize throughput
C) TDP (Thermal Design Power, 700W for H100) is the maximum sustained power the cooling system can dissipate. Actual power draw reveals: (1) Efficiency — TFLOPS/Watt, higher is better; (2) Throttling — power draw stuck at lower limit indicates power brake; (3) Workload type — inference (300-400W) vs training (600-700W); (4) Thermal headroom — power near TDP with high temps suggests cooling issues. DCGM_FI_DEV_POWER_USAGE and DCGM_FI_DEV_POWER_MANAGEMENT_LIMIT show draw vs limit
D) GPU power monitoring is only available on DGX systems with NVIDIA Base Command Manager — standard DCGM cannot monitor GPU power
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU power metrics reveal workload efficiency and thermal health. The H100 TDP is 700W (SXM) or 350W (PCIe). Key observations: (1) Inference typically draws 300-450W — below TDP because inference is memory-bandwidth-bound, not compute-bound; (2) Training draws 600-700W — near TDP, both compute and memory heavily utilized; (3) Power throttling — if power draw is capped at a limit below TDP (e.g., 500W), the GPU is being power-limited (check DCGM_FI_DEV_POWER_MANAGEMENT_LIMIT); (4) Efficiency — performance per watt (TFLOPS/W) peaks at 50-70% TDP, not 100% — running at max TDP may waste power for minimal throughput gain.

  │  600 ┤                                ████                    │
  │  500 ┤                            ████                        │
  │  400 ┤    ████████████████████████                             │
  │  300 ┤████                  Inference                          │

5.3 GPU Health & DiagnosticsQ187-Q192

Monitoring/Security Q187 of 200
What is the difference between SBE (Single-Bit Error) and DBE (Double-Bit Error) in GPU ECC memory, and what action should each trigger?
A) SBE and DBE are the same thing — both indicate GPU memory corruption and require immediate GPU replacement
B) SBE (Single-Bit Error) — a single flipped bit that ECC automatically corrects; common and harmless at low rates (< 100/day). DBE (Double-Bit Error) — two flipped bits in the same ECC word that CANNOT be corrected; any DBE indicates imminent memory failure and requires immediate GPU cordoning and RMA. SBE rate acceleration (rapidly increasing over time) is an early warning of impending DBE — monitor with rate(DCGM_FI_DEV_ECC_SBE_VOL_TOTAL[1h])
C) SBE errors require immediate GPU shutdown; DBE errors are automatically corrected by GPU firmware and can be safely ignored
D) SBE/DBE only apply to CPU RAM — GPUs use HBM which has built-in error correction that makes ECC errors impossible in practice
✅ Correct Answer: B

ECC (Error Correction Code) protects GPU HBM memory from cosmic ray-induced bit flips and hardware faults. SBE (Single-Bit Error): one bit flipped in a 72-bit ECC word — the ECC engine automatically corrects it with zero impact on computation. SBEs are normal; H100 spec allows up to ~10-50 SBEs/day from background radiation. DBE (Double-Bit Error): two bits flipped in the same ECC word — ECC can detect but NOT correct this. Any DBE means the GPU returned corrupted data. Action on DBE: immediate cordon → drain workloads → run Level 3 diagnostic → RMA if reproducible. DBE during AI training silently corrupts model weights.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SBE vs DBE — GPU Memory Error Handling
  SBE (Single-Bit Error) — CORRECTABLE           DBE (Double-Bit Error) — UNCORRECTABLE
  ┌────────────────────────────┐                ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Original:  1010 1100       │                │ Original:  1010 1100       │
  │ Received:  1010 0100 ← 1 bit flip           │ Received:  1010 0101 ← 2 bits flip│
  │            ECC detects &   │                │            ECC detects but │
  │            corrects:       │                │            CANNOT correct: │
  │ Corrected: 1010 1100 ✅    │                │ Result:   1010 0101 ❌ CORRUPTED! │
  └────────────────────────────┘                └────────────────────────────┘

  Monitoring Strategy:
    • SBE rate < 10/day: Normal (cosmic rays, background radiation)
    • SBE rate 10-100/day: Watch — schedule Level 3 diagnostic at next window
    • SBE rate > 100/day or accelerating: WARNING — page retirement may be needed
    • ANY DBE: CRITICAL — GPU returning corrupted data, IMMEDIATE RMA

  nvidia-smi ECC output:
    Volatile: SBE: 3, DBE: 0  ← Since last driver load (or GPU reset)
    Aggregate: SBE: 127, DBE: 0  ← Total since GPU manufacture

For anihpj/jobpost, the AI Ops team monitors ECC rates via rate(DCGM_FI_DEV_ECC_SBE_VOL_TOTAL[1h]) * 3600 to compute SBE/hour. When GPU-11 on DGX-2 showed SBE accelerating from 3/day to 50/day over a week, the GPU was proactively scheduled for Level 3 diagnostics — which confirmed a weak memory page that was retired before it could cause a DBE. This prevented potential silent model corruption.

Monitoring/Security Q188 of 200
What happens when a GPU exceeds its thermal threshold, and how does thermal throttling affect AI training performance?
A) GPUs automatically shut down at 85°C to prevent permanent damage — there is no intermediate throttling state
B) Thermal throttling only affects GPU fans running at higher speeds — GPU compute performance remains unchanged regardless of temperature
C) GPU thermal throttling increases GPU clock speeds to burn off excess heat faster — it actually improves performance briefly before shutdown
D) When GPU temperature exceeds thresholds: at ~85°C, the GPU begins reducing clock speeds (thermal throttling) — compute throughput drops 20-40%. At 90-95°C, further clock reduction — performance may drop 50%+. At 100°C+, emergency thermal shutdown. For AI training, thermal throttling is devastating: a training step that takes 200ms at full clocks takes 300-400ms when throttled, and NCCL collective operations may timeout due to uneven GPU speeds
✅ Correct Answer: D

Thermal management is critical for GPU performance. NVIDIA GPUs have multiple thermal thresholds: (1) 80-85°C: SW thermal throttling begins — GPU firmware reduces clock speeds to manage temperature; (2) 85-90°C: HW thermal slowdown activates — hardware-level clock reduction, performance drops 30-50%; (3) 95-100°C: HW thermal shutdown imminent — GPU enters emergency cooling; (4) 100°C+: GPU shuts down to prevent permanent damage. For AI training, even mild throttling is catastrophic — NCCL collective operations are synchronous; one slow GPU delays ALL GPUs in the communicator. Monitor DCGM_FI_DEV_CLOCK_THROTTLE_REASONS bit 0x10 (HW thermal slowdown).

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Thermal Throttling Impact on AI Training
  GPU Temperature (°C) vs Clock Speed (MHz) vs Training Throughput
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Temp  │ Clock  │ Throughput │ Status                             │
  ├───────┼────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ < 80°C│ 1980   │ 100%       │ ✅ Full performance                │
  │ 82°C  │ 1950   │ 98%        │ ✅ Minor adjustment                │
  │ 85°C  │ 1750   │ 82%        │ ⚠️ SW thermal throttling          │
  │ 88°C  │ 1500   │ 65%        │ 🔴 HW thermal slowdown            │
  │ 92°C  │ 1200   │ 45%        │ 🔴 Severe throttling              │
  │ 95°C  │  800   │ 25%        │ 🚨 Emergency — prepare shutdown   │
  │ 100°C │    0   │  0%        │ 💀 GPU shutdown to protect HW     │
  └───────┴────────┴────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  NCCL Impact: 1 throttled GPU (65% speed) + 7 full-speed GPUs
               → All 8 GPUs run at 65% effective speed (synchronous!)
               → Training throughput drops 35% across ALL GPUs

  Throttle Reasons (CLOCK_THROTTLE_REASONS bitmask):
    0x01 = GPU idle        0x02 = Applications clocks setting
    0x04 = SW power cap     0x08 = HW slowdown (power)
    0x10 = HW thermal slow  0x20 = HW power brake
    0x40 = SW thermal       0x80 = Sync boost

For anihpj/jobpost, GPU-7 on DGX-1 showed DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_TEMP at 88°C with CLOCK_THROTTLE_REASONS = 0x10 (HW thermal slowdown). Investigation found a blocked air intake in the DGX chassis. After cleaning, temperature dropped to 72°C and full 1980 MHz clocks were restored — BERT training throughput recovered from 65% to 100% across all 8 GPUs on that node.

Monitoring/Security Q189 of 200
What are GPU XID errors, and what do common XID codes (31, 43, 45) indicate?
A) XID errors are NVIDIA GPU hardware/software error codes logged by the driver. XID 31 = GPU memory page retirement (GPU detected bad memory and retired the page — monitor for increasing retired page count); XID 43 = GPU stopped processing (GPU hung — often thermal, power, or driver issue); XID 45 = GPU fell off the PCIe bus (physical connection lost — check PCIe seating, power cables, or failing riser). XID 48 = Double-bit ECC error. These appear in dmesg and nvidia-smi logs
B) XID errors are user-facing error codes displayed on the GPU's LED panel — they require physical inspection of the GPU to read the error code
C) XID errors are HTTP status codes for NVIDIA's cloud API — XID 31 = unauthorized, 43 = forbidden, 45 = not found
D) XID errors only occur on consumer gaming GPUs — data center GPUs like H100 have redundant hardware that prevents XID errors entirely
✅ Correct Answer: A

XID (Transaction ID) errors are NVIDIA's standardized error codes for GPU failures. They appear in kernel logs (dmesg | grep Xid) and nvidia-smi. Key XID codes for AI infrastructure: XID 31: Memory page retirement — GPU HBM has bad cells, driver retires the affected pages; acceptable if < 10 pages, RMA if > 50 pages; XID 43: GPU stopped processing — GPU processing pipeline hung, often caused by thermal issues, insufficient power, or driver bugs; XID 45: GPU fell off PCIe bus — physical connection lost, most commonly caused by PCIe riser issues, loose power cables, or failing PCIe switches; XID 48: Double-bit ECC error — uncorrectable memory error, immediate GPU RMA required; XID 79: GPU has fallen off the bus after an error — NVLink fabric issue.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Critical NVIDIA XID Errors for AI Operations
  ┌──────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ XID Code │ Meaning & Action Required                            │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 31       │ Memory page retirement — Monitor, RMA if >50 pages  │
  │ 43       │ GPU stopped processing — Check thermal/power/driver  │
  │ 45       │ GPU fell off bus — Check PCIe seating/power cables  │
  │ 48       │ Double-bit ECC error — IMMEDIATE RMA (data corruption)│
  │ 61       │ Internal micro-controller error — Reboot, else RMA  │
  │ 62       │ GPU thermal assertion — Emergency cooling failure    │
  │ 63       │ GPU power assertion — Power delivery failure         │
  │ 74       │ GPU NVLink CRC error — Check NVLink bridge/cables   │
  │ 79       │ GPU fell off bus (NVLink) — Check NVSwitch fabric   │
  │ 92       │ Internal parity error — RMA if recurring            │
  │ 119      │ GPU fallen off bus — Driver/firmware mismatch       │
  └──────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Check XID: dmesg | grep -i "xid\|NVRM"
            nvidia-smi -q | grep -A5 "Retired Pages"

For anihpj/jobpost, a DGX node started logging XID 45 errors — GPU-3 intermittently falling off the PCIe bus. Investigation found a partially unseated PCIe riser card (likely from vibration). Reseating the riser resolved the issue. XID 45 was caught by a Prometheus alert monitoring kernel logs for XID patterns, enabling the fix within 15 minutes of first occurrence.

Monitoring/Security Q190 of 200
What is nvidia-bug-report.sh, and when should an AI infrastructure operator run it?
A) nvidia-bug-report.sh is an automated GPU overclocking script that boosts GPU clock speeds for maximum AI training performance
B) nvidia-bug-report.sh is NVIDIA's comprehensive diagnostic data collection script. It gathers: nvidia-smi output, dmesg logs, XID errors, driver version, GPU VBIOS, PCIe topology, kernel module status, DCGM status, CUDA logs, and system information — all in a single .gz file. Run it whenever you encounter GPU issues (XID errors, performance drops, NCCL failures) to provide NVIDIA support with complete diagnostic data. It's the FIRST command NVIDIA support will ask for
C) nvidia-bug-report.sh is a GPU firmware flashing utility that reinstalls the GPU BIOS to fix hardware bugs — it permanently modifies GPU firmware
D) nvidia-bug-report.sh is a Python script for reporting GPU bugs to NVIDIA's public GitHub repository — it creates GitHub issues automatically from GPU error logs
✅ Correct Answer: B

nvidia-bug-report.sh is the essential first-step diagnostic tool for any GPU issue. Located at /usr/bin/nvidia-bug-report.sh (installed with NVIDIA driver), it generates a comprehensive nvidia-bug-report.log.gz file containing: (1) nvidia-smi detailed output (all GPUs, all metrics); (2) kernel logs (dmesg) with NVIDIA driver messages and XID errors; (3) GPU VBIOS versions and firmware details; (4) PCIe topology (lspci tree); (5) /proc/driver/nvidia/ contents (GPU, gpus, registry, warnings, etc.); (6) DCGM status and active watches; (7) CUDA driver/runtime version info; (8) System info (CPU, memory, OS, kernel version). Always run this before contacting NVIDIA Enterprise Support — it provides 95% of what they need.

Monitoring/Security Q191 of 200
What is GPU memory page retirement, and how does it affect usable GPU memory for AI workloads?
A) Page retirement is a GPU memory optimization that frees unused pages for other applications — it increases available GPU memory
B) Page retirement is a manual process where the operator selects which GPU memory pages to decommission — it requires physical jumper changes on the GPU board
C) When the GPU detects persistent ECC errors on a memory page (typically after multiple SBEs on the same page), it "retires" that page — marking it as unusable and mapping it out of the addressable memory space. Each retired page reduces usable GPU VRAM by the page size (~2MB for H100). A few retired pages (< 10) is normal; > 50 pages indicates significant memory degradation and the GPU may need RMA. Large AI models that need nearly all 80GB of HBM may fail if too many pages are retired
D) GPU memory page retirement only applies to system RAM used as GPU cache — it doesn't affect the GPU's own HBM memory at all
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU memory page retirement is the GPU's self-healing mechanism for failing memory. When the ECC engine detects repeated SBEs at the same physical memory location, the driver marks that page as "retired" (bad) and remaps it out of usable memory. This prevents future DBEs but reduces total available VRAM. Each retired page on H100 is typically 2MB. Thresholds: 0-10 retired pages = normal (manufacturing defects mapped out at production); 10-50 = monitor for acceleration; 50+ = GPU memory is significantly degraded, schedule RMA. For AI workloads: a model requiring 78GB on an 80GB H100 with 200+ retired pages (400MB lost) may still fit; but 2000 retired pages (4GB lost) would cause OOM.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Memory Page Retirement — Impact on VRAM
  H100 80GB HBM3 Memory Map
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ████████████████████████████████████████████████████████████│ ← 80GB Total
  │ ████████████████████████████████████████████████████████████│
  │ ████████████████████░░░░████████████████████████████████████│ ← Retired pages
  │ ██████████████████████████████░░░░░░████████████████████████│   (unusable)
  │ ████████████████████████████████████████████████████████████│
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    Usable: 80GB - (Retired Pages × 2MB)

  Example: 500 retired pages = 1GB lost → 79GB usable
           5000 retired pages = 10GB lost → 70GB usable → RMA needed!

  Check retired pages: nvidia-smi -q | grep -A5 "Retired"
  Alert on: DCGM_FI_DEV_RETIRED_PAGES_SBE > 50
            rate(DCGM_FI_DEV_RETIRED_PAGES_SBE[1d]) > 10 (accelerating)

For anihpj/jobpost, GPU-2 on DGX-1 showed accelerating page retirement: 5 → 15 → 45 pages over two weeks. The BERT model (72GB) still fit, but the trend was concerning. Level 4 diagnostic confirmed memory degradation, and the GPU was proactively RMAd during a scheduled maintenance window — avoiding an outage when the model would eventually fail to load.

Monitoring/Security Q192 of 200
What is RAS (Reliability, Availability, Serviceability) in the context of NVIDIA GPUs, and how does it apply to AI infrastructure?
A) RAS encompasses all GPU features that ensure continuous, error-free operation: Reliability (ECC memory, NVLink CRC, PCIe replay — detecting and correcting errors), Availability (GPU page retirement, NVLink lane reduction — degraded but still operational), Serviceability (XID error codes, nvidia-bug-report.sh, DCGM diagnostics, nvidia-smi health checks — tools to diagnose and fix issues). For AI infrastructure, RAS means your 32-GPU training job doesn't silently produce corrupted models due to undetected GPU errors
B) RAS is a GPU overclocking profile that prioritizes Reliability over Absolute Speed — it reduces clock speeds by 20% to ensure error-free operation
C) RAS stands for "Rapid AI Startup" — NVIDIA's program for quickly provisioning GPU clusters for AI research with pre-configured software stacks
D) RAS is a GPU networking protocol (Reliable AI Switching) that guarantees lossless GPU-to-GPU communication for NCCL all-reduce operations
✅ Correct Answer: A

RAS (Reliability, Availability, Serviceability) is the framework for GPU dependability in production AI. Reliability: features that prevent errors — ECC on HBM, CRC on NVLink flits, PCIe replay mechanism, parity on internal buses. Availability: features that keep the GPU operational despite faults — memory page retirement (map out bad cells), NVLink lane reduction (operate with fewer active links), GPU reset without node reboot. Serviceability: features that help diagnose and repair — XID error codes, DCGM diagnostic levels, nvidia-smi health checks, nvidia-bug-report.sh, field-replaceable components. For AI infrastructure at scale, RAS is critical: a single undetected GPU error can silently corrupt model weights during a multi-million-dollar training run.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU RAS — The Three Pillars for AI Production
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     GPU RAS Framework                           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ RELIABILITY  │  │ AVAILABILITY │  │ SERVICEABILITY       │  │
  │  │ (Prevent)    │  │ (Survive)    │  │ (Diagnose & Repair)  │  │

For anihpj/jobpost, GPU RAS features are part of the AI training SOP: ECC monitoring catches memory degradation before it corrupts model weights; NVLink CRC monitoring prevents all-reduce errors; and XID log monitoring triggers automated incident response. This RAS-first approach has prevented any silent model corruption incidents across 200+ BERT training runs.

5.4 NVIDIA GPU SecurityQ193-Q200

Monitoring/Security Q193 of 200
Why do GPUs need their own security model separate from CPU security in AI infrastructure?
A) GPUs don't need security — they only process mathematical computations that have no security implications
B) CPU security (SELinux, seccomp, AppArmor) automatically covers GPUs because GPU commands flow through the CPU — no additional GPU-specific security is needed
C) GPUs have their own memory (HBM, not system RAM), their own execution model (CUDA kernels, not CPU instructions), their own interconnects (NVLink, not system bus), and their own firmware (GPU VBIOS). They process sensitive data (training data, model weights, inference inputs) and have DMA access to system memory. CPU security controls cannot inspect GPU memory contents, inter-GPU NVLink traffic, or GPU firmware integrity — requiring GPU-native security: encrypted memory (Confidential Computing), firmware attestation (Secure Boot), hardware isolation (MIG), and GPU-specific access controls
D) GPU security only matters for multi-tenant cloud environments — on-premises DGX clusters are physically secure and don't need GPU-level security controls
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU security is essential because GPUs process and store sensitive data outside the visibility of CPU security controls. Key security concerns: (1) GPU memory (HBM) is separate from system RAM — SELinux/AppArmor can't control GPU memory access; (2) CUDA execution — GPU kernels run outside CPU instruction monitoring; (3) NVLink fabric — inter-GPU traffic within a DGX is invisible to network security tools; (4) GPU DMA — GPUs can read/write system memory via PCIe, potentially bypassing CPU memory protections; (5) GPU firmware — compromised VBIOS could intercept all GPU computations. Modern GPUs address these with Confidential Computing (encrypted GPU memory), Secure Boot (signed firmware), and MIG isolation.

  │  │ (HBM 80GB)   │   │ (900 GB/s)   │   │ (VBIOS)              │ │
  │  ├──────────────┤   ├──────────────┤   ├──────────────────────┤ │
  │  │ • Memory scrap│   │ • Traffic    │   │ • Firmware tampering │ │

For anihpj/jobpost, GPU security is critical because the BERT model processes sensitive resume data (PII) and proprietary job matching algorithms. Confidential Computing on H100 ensures that even the infrastructure operator cannot access model weights or inference inputs in GPU memory — essential for GDPR and SOC 2 compliance in the AI-powered recruitment platform.

Monitoring/Security Q194 of 200
How does MIG provide hardware-level security isolation between GPU instances, and why is this stronger than software-level GPU sharing?
A) MIG security is purely software-based — it uses Kubernetes namespaces to isolate GPU workloads, identical to CPU namespace isolation
B) MIG doesn't provide any security isolation — MIG instances share all GPU resources and can access each other's memory and computations freely
C) MIG security is only available with an additional NVIDIA Morpheus security license — the base MIG feature provides no isolation guarantees
D) MIG provides hardware-enforced isolation: each MIG instance gets dedicated Streaming Multiprocessors (compute), dedicated HBM slices (memory), dedicated L2 cache partitions, and dedicated memory bandwidth. No MIG instance can access another instance's memory, cache, or compute resources — at the silicon level. This is fundamentally stronger than software sharing (time-slicing, MPS) where multiple processes share the same GPU and a kernel bug could leak data between contexts
✅ Correct Answer: D

MIG security isolation is hardware-enforced at the silicon level. Each MIG instance receives: (1) Dedicated SMs — physically separate compute units, no shared execution resources; (2) Dedicated HBM slices — physically partitioned memory with hardware-enforced access control; (3) Dedicated L2 cache — partitioned cache with no cross-instance cache line leakage; (4) Dedicated memory bandwidth — guaranteed bandwidth allocation per instance. This means MIG Instance A cannot read MIG Instance B's memory, cache, or interrupts — even with a kernel-level exploit. This is the GPU equivalent of a hardware hypervisor vs. container sharing.

📝 Detailed Explanation
MIG Security — Silicon-Level Isolation vs Software Sharing
  MIG (Hardware Isolation)                  Software Sharing (Time-Slicing/MPS)
  ┌──────────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────────┐

  │  ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐     │            │  ┌──────────────────────┐ │

  │  │ 2g.20 │ │ 3g.40 │     │            │  │ Cache — ALL processes │ │

  │  │ Mem:20│ │ Mem:40│     │            │  │ Process A ↔ Process B │ │
  │  │ L2:5MB│ │ L2:10M│     │            │  │ (no HW isolation)     │ │
  │  └───┬───┘ └───┬───┘     │            │  └──────────────────────┘ │
  │  ─────┼─────────┼─────── │            │  ────────────────────────── │
  │       │         │         │            │  Kernel bug → data leak!    │
  │  ┌────▼───┐ ┌───▼─────┐  │            └──────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, MIG is used to run the BERT training job (MIG-1, 4g.40gb) and the inference API (MIG-2, 3g.40gb) on the same A100 — with hardware guarantee that the inference service cannot access training data or model weights being trained on MIG-1. This enables secure multi-tenant GPU sharing without risk of data leakage between workloads, critical for GDPR compliance when processing candidate resumes.

Monitoring/Security Q195 of 200
What is NVIDIA GPU Secure Boot, and how does it protect the GPU firmware supply chain?
A) GPU Secure Boot is the same as system UEFI Secure Boot — the GPU uses the motherboard's TPM for firmware verification with no GPU-specific security features
B) GPU Secure Boot verifies the cryptographic signature of GPU firmware at boot time using a hardware root of trust embedded in the GPU silicon. The GPU will ONLY execute firmware signed by NVIDIA's private key — preventing compromised or tampered firmware from running. This protects against supply-chain attacks (malicious firmware inserted during manufacturing/transit) and runtime firmware tampering
C) GPU Secure Boot is a software feature in the NVIDIA driver — it's not hardware-based and can be bypassed by any process with root access
D) GPU Secure Boot encrypts the GPU BIOS to prevent competitors from reverse-engineering NVIDIA's GPU architectures — it's an IP protection feature, not a security feature
✅ Correct Answer: B

GPU Secure Boot establishes a hardware-anchored chain of trust for GPU firmware. The process: (1) Boot ROM (immutable, in silicon) verifies the signature of the next stage; (2) Firmware stages each verify the next before loading; (3) Only NVIDIA-signed firmware executes — any unsigned or tampered firmware is rejected. This prevents: supply chain attacks (firmware tampered during shipping), evil-maid attacks (firmware replaced with malicious version), and runtime firmware compromise. On H100, Secure Boot is integrated with NVIDIA's Root of Trust (RoT) module and extends to GPU firmware attestation — a remote verifier can cryptographically confirm the GPU is running authentic firmware.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Secure Boot — Chain of Trust
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GPU Secure Boot Chain of Trust                              │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────┐     Verify     ┌──────────────┐           │
  │  │ Boot ROM     │───────────────▶│ Firmware     │           │
  │  │ (immutable,  │   Signature    │ Stage 1      │           │
  │  │  in silicon) │                │ (SPUB/HSB)   │           │
  │  └──────────────┘                └──────┬───────┘           │
  │                                        │ Verify             │
  │                                        ▼                    │
  │                                 ┌──────────────┐           │
  │                                 │ Firmware     │           │
  │                                 │ Stage 2      │──────────▶│
  │                                 │ (GPU Engine) │  Signed   │
  │                                 └──────┬───────┘  NVIDIA FW│
  │                                        │                    │
  │                                        ▼                    │
  │                                 ┌──────────────┐           │
  │                                 │ GPU Ready    │           │
  │                                 │ (attestation │           │
  │                                 │  available)  │           │
  │                                 └──────────────┘           │
  │                                                              │
  │  If ANY signature fails → GPU refuses to boot → alert!      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, GPU Secure Boot is enabled on all H100 GPUs processing sensitive candidate data. Firmware attestation reports are collected at node boot time and verified against NVIDIA's published firmware hashes — any mismatch triggers a security alert and prevents the GPU node from joining the Kubernetes cluster, ensuring no compromised GPU ever processes PII data.

Monitoring/Security Q196 of 200
What is NVIDIA Confidential Computing on H100, and how does it protect data in GPU memory?
A) H100 Confidential Computing creates a hardware-isolated Trusted Execution Environment (TEE) where GPU memory (HBM) is encrypted at all times — even during computation. The GPU encrypts/decrypts data transparently as it moves between HBM and compute units. The encryption keys are managed by the GPU's hardware root of trust and are never accessible to the host OS, hypervisor, or even NVIDIA's driver. This means even a compromised host cannot read GPU memory contents. Attestation allows remote verification that the TEE is genuine before sending sensitive data
B) Confidential Computing is a software encryption layer that encrypts CUDA kernels before execution — it protects model IP but not data in memory
C) Confidential Computing requires all data to be encrypted at rest on CPU before transfer to GPU — it's a storage encryption feature, not a GPU memory protection feature
D) Confidential Computing is only available in Azure cloud — on-prem DGX systems cannot use GPU memory encryption
✅ Correct Answer: A

NVIDIA Confidential Computing (CC) on H100 provides the strongest GPU data protection available: all data in HBM is encrypted at all times, decrypted only inside the GPU compute units during processing, and re-encrypted before being written back to memory. Key properties: (1) HW root of trust — encryption keys generated in silicon, never exposed to software; (2) Transparent encryption — existing CUDA applications work without modification; (3) Attestation — remote parties can cryptographically verify the GPU TEE is genuine before sharing sensitive data or models; (4) Protected from host — even a compromised hypervisor, OS, or NVIDIA driver cannot access GPU plaintext data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
H100 Confidential Computing — Protected GPU Memory
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  H100 Confidential Computing Architecture                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  GPU TEE (Trusted Execution Environment)                    │ │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐  │ │
  │  │  │ HBM      │───▶│ Compute  │───▶│ HBM                  │  │ │
  │  │  │ (Encrypt)│    │ (Decrypt,│    │ (Encrypt)            │  │ │
  │  │  │ ████████ │    │  Process)│    │ ████████              │  │ │
  │  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────────────────┘  │ │
  │  │       ▲                              │                      │ │
  │  │       │ Encryption keys (in silicon) │                      │ │
  │  │       │ NEVER leave GPU chip         │                      │ │
  │  └───────┼──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┘ │
  │          │                              │                        │
  │  ┌───────┴──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  Host (CPU, OS, Hypervisor, NVIDIA Driver)                  │ │
  │  │  SEES ONLY: Encrypted data (████████)                       │ │
  │  │  CANNOT ACCESS: Plaintext data, encryption keys             │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Attestation flow: Verifier → GPU: "Prove you're a genuine H100 CC"
                    GPU → Verifier: Signed hardware attestation report
                    Verifier: ✓ Verified → Send model weights (encrypted)

For anihpj/jobpost, H100 Confidential Computing will protect the proprietary BERT model weights and candidate PII data. Even if the Kubernetes node is compromised via a container escape, the attacker cannot read GPU memory — model IP and personal data remain encrypted. Attestation ensures the model is only served to verifiably genuine H100 GPUs, preventing model theft via GPU emulation.

Monitoring/Security Q197 of 200
What Kubernetes SecurityContext settings are important for securing GPU pods in production?
A) GPU pods don't need special SecurityContext — GPU access is controlled by nvidia.com/gpu resource limits, which automatically enforces all necessary security controls
B) GPU pods must always run as privileged: true — NVIDIA drivers require full host access and cannot function with any security restrictions applied
C) Key SecurityContext for GPU pods: allowPrivilegeEscalation: false (prevent privilege escalation), runAsNonRoot: true (run as non-root user with GPU access via nvidia group), seccompProfile: RuntimeDefault (restrict syscalls but allow nvidia-specific ioctls), capabilities.drop: ["ALL"] (drop all Linux capabilities). The GPU Operator handles device access — pods only need the nvidia.com/gpu resource, not privileged mode. Use Pod Security Standards "Restricted" where possible
D) GPU container security is entirely managed by NVIDIA GPU Operator's security policies — individual pod SecurityContext settings are overridden and ignored
✅ Correct Answer: C

GPU pods should follow least-privilege security principles — they do NOT need privileged mode. The GPU Operator provides device access via the NVIDIA Container Toolkit, which mounts /dev/nvidia* devices into the container. Pods requesting nvidia.com/gpu: 1 get GPU access without requiring privileged: true. Best practices: (1) runAsNonRoot: true — user needs to be in the nvidia group (or video/render groups) to access GPU devices; (2) allowPrivilegeEscalation: false — prevent setuid binaries from escalating; (3) seccompProfile: RuntimeDefault — blocks dangerous syscalls but allows GPU ioctls; (4) Drop ALL capabilities — GPU pods don't need CAP_SYS_ADMIN or any Linux capabilities.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Pod Security — Least Privilege Configuration
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: Pod
  metadata:
    name: bert-trainer
  spec:
    containers:
    - name: trainer
      image: nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch:24.01-py3
      resources:
        limits:
          nvidia.com/gpu: 1  # ← GPU access via GPU Operator, NO privileged needed!
      securityContext:
        runAsNonRoot: true          # Run as non-root (UID 1000 in nvidia group)
        runAsUser: 1000
        runAsGroup: 1001            # nvidia/video group for /dev/nvidia* access
        allowPrivilegeEscalation: false  # No setuid escalation
        seccompProfile:
          type: RuntimeDefault      # Allow GPU ioctls, block dangerous syscalls
        capabilities:
          drop: ["ALL"]             # No Linux capabilities needed
        readOnlyRootFilesystem: true # Immutable container filesystem
    serviceAccountName: bert-trainer-sa  # Dedicated SA with minimal RBAC

  # What NOT to do:
  # privileged: true  ← UNNECESSARY for GPU access with GPU Operator!

For anihpj/jobpost, GPU training pods run under Pod Security Standard "Restricted" with the SecurityContext shown above. The BERT trainer pod runs as UID 1000 (in nvidia group), drops ALL Linux capabilities, uses RuntimeDefault seccomp, and has a read-only root filesystem — yet has full GPU access via the GPU Operator's device plugin. This is a 🚀 Beyond crossover with CKS security best practices applied to GPU workloads.

Monitoring/Security Q198 of 200
What is GPU supply chain security, and how does firmware signing and SBOM protect AI infrastructure?
A) GPU supply chain security is handled by the shipping company's insurance — it covers physical theft during transit but has nothing to do with firmware or software
B) Supply chain security only applies to the software stack (CUDA, cuDNN) — the GPU hardware itself cannot be tampered with and doesn't need verification
C) GPU supply chain security means buying GPUs directly from NVIDIA.com — authorized retailers are the only security measure needed
D) GPU supply chain security ensures the GPU hardware, firmware, and software haven't been tampered with from factory to data center. Key measures: (1) Firmware signing — GPU Secure Boot verifies all firmware is authentic NVIDIA-signed code; (2) SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) — cryptographic inventory of every firmware component, driver, and library on the GPU; (3) Secure provisioning — GPU identity certificates provisioned at factory, verified at deployment; (4) Physical tamper evidence — tamper-evident packaging and hardware security modules (HSM) in DGX systems
✅ Correct Answer: D

GPU supply chain security protects against hardware-level attacks on AI infrastructure. In 2024, the risk of supply-chain-compromised GPUs is real — malicious firmware could exfiltrate model weights, inject backdoors into training, or create covert communication channels. NVIDIA addresses this through: (1) Secure manufacturing — GPU identity certificates and unique device keys are provisioned in NVIDIA's secure facilities; (2) Firmware attestation — at boot, the GPU proves it's running authentic, unmodified firmware to a remote verifier; (3) SBOM — a signed inventory of all firmware/software components enables vulnerability tracking (e.g., "Is our DGX fleet affected by CVE-2024-XXXX?"); (4) Physical security — DGX chassis have tamper-evident seals and HSMs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Supply Chain Security — Factory to Data Center
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │  │ • Identity cert │    │   packaging    │    │   verification   │ │

  │                  ✓ Match → GPU joins secure cluster              │

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, GPU supply chain security is critical because the BERT model processes candidate data from Fortune 500 companies. At deployment, each H100 GPU undergoes firmware attestation — verifying the GPU is running authentic, untampered NVIDIA firmware. SBOM validation checks all firmware components against known CVEs. A GPU that fails attestation is automatically quarantined and cannot process any data until investigated.

Monitoring/Security Q199 of 200
How do you implement fine-grained GPU access control in Kubernetes for multi-team AI clusters?
A) GPU access control is binary — either a namespace has GPU access or it doesn't; Kubernetes cannot provide finer granularity for GPU resources
B) Multi-layer GPU access control: (1) ResourceQuota limits nvidia.com/gpu count per namespace; (2) RBAC controls who can create pods with GPU requests; (3) GPU node labels + nodeSelector/affinity direct teams to specific GPU types (A100 vs H100); (4) MIG profiles in node labels enable per-MIG-instance scheduling; (5) OPA/Gatekeeper policies enforce GPU usage rules (e.g., "only prod namespace can request > 4 GPUs"); (6) Kyverno policies validate GPU pod SecurityContext. This creates fine-grained, policy-driven GPU governance
C) GPU access control is managed entirely by NVIDIA Base Command Manager, not Kubernetes — Kubernetes RBAC cannot control GPU-specific resources
D) The only way to control GPU access is to physically cable specific GPUs to specific teams' servers — software-based GPU access control doesn't exist
✅ Correct Answer: B

Fine-grained GPU access control in Kubernetes combines native K8s features with policy engines. The layered approach: (1) ResourceQuota in each namespace caps GPU consumption (nvidia.com/gpu: 8); (2) RBAC controls which users/service accounts can create GPU workloads; (3) Node affinity directs ML Engineering team to H100 nodes, Inference team to A100/L40S nodes via labels like nvidia.com/gpu.product: NVIDIA-H100; (4) MIG scheduling via node labels (nvidia.com/mig-3g.40gb: 2) for MIG profile allocation; (5) OPA/Gatekeeper policies enforce rules like "max 4 GPUs per pod" or "only production namespace can use > 2 GPUs"; (6) Kyverno validates SecurityContext for GPU pods.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Layer GPU Access Control in Kubernetes
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GPU Access Control Layers                                      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Layer 1: ResourceQuota (per namespace)                        │
  │    ml-training:  nvidia.com/gpu: 16  (max 16 GPUs)            │
  │    ml-inference: nvidia.com/gpu:  8  (max 8 GPUs)             │
  │                                                                 │

For anihpj/jobpost, GPU RBAC separates the ML Training team (access to H100 GPUs, up to 32 GPUs in ml-training namespace) from the Inference team (access to L40S GPUs, up to 8 GPUs in ml-inference namespace). OPA policies prevent training team from using inference GPUs and vice versa. ResourceQuota ensures no team monopolizes the GPU cluster. This is a 🚀 Beyond crossover — the same RBAC + ResourceQuota + policy patterns from CKA/CKS applied to GPU-specific resources.

Monitoring/Security Q200 of 200
What are the most significant GPU-specific security threats in AI infrastructure, and how are they mitigated?
A) Key GPU threats: (1) GPU memory scraping — attacker with host access reads GPU HBM via nvidia-smi/cuda API to extract model weights or inference data (mitigated by Confidential Computing encrypted memory); (2) CUDA malware — malicious CUDA kernels that hide from CPU monitoring, exfiltrate data via GPU DMA, or mine cryptocurrency (mitigated by GPU Secure Boot + kernel signing); (3) Side-channel attacks — monitoring GPU power/timing to infer model architecture or training data (mitigated by CC + power obfuscation); (4) NVLink eavesdropping — intercepting GPU-to-GPU traffic within DGX (mitigated by NVLink encryption on H100). 🚀 CKS crossover: Falco can detect anomalous CUDA syscalls, Kyverno can enforce GPU pod security policies
B) GPU security threats are theoretical — there are no documented cases of GPU-specific attacks because GPUs are inherently secure by design
C) The only GPU security threat is physical theft of the GPU hardware — software-based GPU attacks are impossible due to NVIDIA's driver security model
D) GPU security threats only affect gaming GPUs (GeForce) — data center GPUs like H100 have security features that eliminate all threat vectors
✅ Correct Answer: A

GPU security threats are real and growing as AI becomes more valuable. The four major categories: (1) GPU memory scraping — an attacker who compromises the host OS can read GPU HBM via nvidia-smi, CUDA APIs, or /dev/nvidia* devices, extracting model weights (IP theft) or inference data (PII leak); (2) CUDA malware — malicious GPU kernels can hide from traditional CPU-based security tools (AV, EDR) since they execute entirely on the GPU — used for cryptojacking (stealthy GPU mining) or data exfiltration via GPU DMA; (3) Side-channel attacks — by monitoring GPU power draw, temperature, or kernel execution timing, attackers can infer model architecture details or training data characteristics; (4) NVLink eavesdropping — inter-GPU communication within a DGX can be intercepted.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GPU Security Threat Landscape & Mitigations
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GPU Security Threats & Defenses                                     │
  │                                                                      │
  │  THREAT                     │ MITIGATION                             │
  │  ──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  GPU Memory Scraping        │ H100 Confidential Computing            │
  │  (read HBM from host)       │ Encrypted HBM + HW root of trust       │
  │                             │ Zero-trust: host can't read plaintext  │
  │  ──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  CUDA Malware               │ GPU Secure Boot + signed kernels       │
  │  (malicious GPU kernels)    │ Falco GPU syscall monitoring           │
  │                             │ DCGM anomaly detection (power/util)    │
  │  ──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  Side-Channel Attacks       │ Confidential Computing obfuscation    │
  │  (power/timing analysis)    │ Power draw randomization              │
  │                             │ Constant-time CUDA kernel design       │
  │  ──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  NVLink Eavesdropping       │ NVLink link encryption (H100)          │
  │  (inter-GPU traffic)        │ Physical security (DGX chassis)        │
  │  ──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  GPU Supply Chain           │ GPU Secure Boot + SBOM                 │
  │  (tampered firmware)        │ Firmware attestation at boot           │
  │                             │ Tamper-evident packaging               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  🚀 CKS Crossover: Falco rules for GPU syscalls
    - rule: GPU Memory Access by Non-GPU Container
      condition: evt.type=openat and fd.name contains "/dev/nvidia"
                 and container.image.repository not contains "nvidia"
      output: "Non-NVIDIA container accessing GPU device!"

For anihpj/jobpost, the GPU security strategy addresses all four threat categories: H100 Confidential Computing protects model weights and candidate PII in GPU memory; GPU Secure Boot prevents firmware tampering; DCGM anomaly detection identifies unusual GPU power/thermal patterns (CUDA malware indicator); and Falco rules monitor for unexpected GPU device access from non-GPU containers. This defense-in-depth approach ensures the AI recruitment platform meets enterprise security standards for Fortune 500 clients. 🚀 Beyond: These GPU security patterns directly parallel CKS domains — pod security policies, runtime security (Falco), supply chain security (SBOM), and network segmentation — all extended to the GPU layer.

💾 Category 1: Storage Troubleshooting (10 Issues)ST1-ST10

🟢 Storage Issues — PVC, PV, StorageClass, and Mount Failures

Storage is often the FIRST thing to break because it involves multiple components: PVC → StorageClass → Provisioner → PV → Mount. When any link in this chain fails, your pod can't start or your data is at risk.

📌 ST1–ST3: PVC Binding Failures

ST1
💾 CATEGORY 1: STORAGE — Issue ST1
PVC Stuck in Pending State — No PV Bound

🔍 Symptom: kubectl get pvc shows STATUS: Pending indefinitely. kubectl describe pvc shows "no persistent volumes available for this claim." The anihpj-db PostgreSQL pod can't start because its PVC won't bind.

  kubectl get pvc
  NAME         STATUS    VOLUME   CAPACITY   STORAGECLASS   AGE
  anihpj-pvc   Pending                      fast-ssd       5m

  kubectl describe pvc anihpj-pvc
  Events:
    Warning  VolumeBinding  no persistent volumes available for this claim
    Warning  VolumeBinding  storageclass.storage.k8s.io "fast-ssd" not found
🔴5 Most Likely Causes
  1. StorageClass does not exist: The PVC references a StorageClass that hasn't been created. kubectl get sc — if "fast-ssd" is missing, create it or fix the PVC's storageClassName.
  2. No matching PV available: In static provisioning, no PV matches the PVC's storage size, access mode, or selector. kubectl get pv — check capacity and accessModes.
  3. StorageClass provisioner not installed: The CSI driver (e.g., ebs.csi.aws.com) is not deployed. kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep csi — provisioner pods must be Running.
  4. Access mode mismatch: PVC requests ReadWriteMany but only ReadWriteOnce PVs exist. Check kubectl get pv -o yaml | grep -A2 accessModes.
  5. StorageClass has volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer and no pod uses the PVC yet. The PV is only created when a pod actually references the PVC.
🟡5 Less Likely Causes
  1. CSI driver pod CrashLoopBackOff — provisioner can't create volumes
  2. Cloud provider API rate limiting — too many PVCs created simultaneously
  3. PVC selector labels don't match any PV labels
  4. Node where CSI driver runs has taint without toleration
  5. storageClassName: "" (empty string, not null!) — requests a PV with NO StorageClass, but all PVs have one
🟣5 New Cluster Causes
  1. No default StorageClass set: kubectl get sc — look for "(default)". Without it, PVCs without storageClassName won't bind. Set: kubectl patch sc <name> -p '{"metadata":{"annotations":{"storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class":"true"}}}'
  2. CSI driver not yet installed — common in fresh kubeadm clusters
  3. Cloud credentials not configured for CSI driver to provision volumes
  4. RBAC for CSI driver service account missing
  5. kubelet missing --cloud-provider flag for legacy in-tree provisioners
🔍 What to Look At:
kubectl get sc,pv,pvc --all-namespaces The full storage stack in one view — see StorageClass, PV, and PVC together.
kubectl describe pvc <name> The Events section tells you exactly why binding failed — always read the events first.
🎯 #1 Root Cause: StorageClass does not exist or is misnamed. The PVC references a StorageClass that hasn't been created — the most common CKA exam pitfall.
🔧 How to Solve:
1. Verify StorageClass exists kubectl get sc If the StorageClass referenced by the PVC is missing, create it or fix the PVC's storageClassName.
2. Check provisioner and parameters kubectl describe sc <name> Verify the provisioner is correct and the CSI driver is installed and running.
3. Static provisioning kubectl get pv Create a PV matching the PVC's requested size, accessMode, and selector labels.
4. Dynamic provisioning Install the CSI driver: kubectl apply -f <csi-driver-url>. Verify CSI pods are Running in kube-system.
5. Set default StorageClass kubectl patch sc <name> -p '{"metadata":{"annotations":{"storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class":"true"}}}' PVCs without an explicit storageClassName will use the default.
💡 Personal Advice:
Always verify StorageClass FIRST. The #1 PVC issue on the CKA exam is a missing or misnamed StorageClass. Run kubectl get sc before writing any PVC YAML.
Empty string is NOT null. storageClassName: "" Requests a PV with NO StorageClass — different from omitting the field entirely. Know this distinction.
CSI driver must be Running. Even with correct StorageClass, if the CSI provisioner pod is CrashLoopBackOff, no volumes will be created.
WaitForFirstConsumer delays binding. If volumeBindingMode is WaitForFirstConsumer, the PV is only created when a pod actually uses the PVC. Don't mistake this for a failure.
